--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page begins the formal dedication of the work. It follows the title page (which introduced Snellius as the "Dutch Tiphys") and addresses the governing bodies of Holland and West Friesland. The author uses classical analogies, comparing the Dutch maritime spirit to that of ancient Tyre.</meta>

PREFACE

### To the Most Illustrious and Most Powerful

# STATES
## OF HOLLAND
## AND WEST FRIESLAND.

->*Most Illustrious States,*<-

<note>An ornate decorative woodcut initial 'T' is set within a square frame featuring scrolling acanthus leaves and floral motifs.</note> That Tyre, which was once famous for her offspring, for the cities she birthed—Leptis, Utica, and that rival of the Roman Empire, greedy for the whole world, Carthage; even Gades <note>modern Cádiz, Spain</note> founded beyond the known world, as Pliny says: that Tyre, I say, which filled all the shores of the Mediterranean Sea with her commerce and the export of merchandise, and by her zeal for navigation far surpassed the other maritime cities of Phoenicia: and she alone, having overcome the narrow straits of the land, slipped through the Erythraean Sea <note>original: *mare Erythræum*; in this context, the Red Sea and the Indian Ocean</note> to survey the shores of Asia and Africa, and dared to probe the outer seas, even as nature herself almost resisted and was unwilling:

<sig>* 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: to which (original: cui)</meta>

<summary>In this opening of the dedication, Snellius compares the maritime power and colonial reach of the Dutch Republic to the ancient Phoenician city of Tyre. He emphasizes the courage required to navigate beyond known boundaries into the Indian and Atlantic Oceans.</summary>
<keywords>Tyre, Phoenicia, Carthage, maritime history, States of Holland, navigation, Pliny the Elder, Erythraean Sea</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to the States of Holland and West Friesland. It moves from the geographical reach of ancient Tyre to its role as a cradle of science and its historical parallel to the rise of the Dutch maritime empire.</meta>

to which alone King Solomon <note>The biblical King Solomon, known for his wealth and wisdom, who partnered with King Hiram of Tyre to build the Temple in Jerusalem and manage maritime trade.</note>, the lord of that isthmus, decided his fleet should be entrusted for the transport of gold, silver, spices, precious stones, and gems. That Tyre, I say, set all Phoenicia <note>An ancient Mediterranean civilization of maritime traders, located in modern-day Lebanon and coastal Syria.</note> ablaze with a zeal for new things and for the investigation of the arts. For to her, Grammar owes its origin in the invention of letters; Arithmetic, for the use of numbers; Astronomy, in guiding the course of ships; and Geography, in the mapping of land and sea—to say nothing now of many other matters and inventions useful for human life.

For the Tyrians, because of the trade and commerce they conducted with all nations, found their minds spurred by utility and profit to search out hidden things, and they brought a great many discoveries to light. Whenever, therefore, I reflect upon this in my mind, I observe many things in this country of ours <note>the Dutch Republic</note> not unlike those ancient ones. This will be all the more evident if we compare the darkness of previous centuries with this current light.

For whereas long ago the Alexandrians in Africa and Asia, and the Venetians in Europe, held a monopoly on Arabian and Indian goods; and (as all things have their cycles, their rise and their fall) when finally Columbus <note>Christopher Columbus, whose 1492 voyage changed the European understanding of world geography.</note>, having undertaken an expedition, reached America: the Portuguese <note>original: *Lusitani*</note> also, having avoided...

<meta>catchword: a long [route] (original: longo)</meta>

<summary>Snellius credits ancient Tyre with the invention of the sciences (Arithmetic, Astronomy, Geography) driven by the needs of trade, and begins to draw a direct parallel between the ancient Phoenicians, the discoveries of Columbus, and the rising Dutch maritime power.</summary>
<keywords>King Solomon, Tyre, Phoenicia, Grammar, Arithmetic, Astronomy, Geography, Christopher Columbus, Portugal, maritime trade, history of science</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the comparison between the ancient maritime power of Tyre and the rising Dutch Republic. It tracks the shift of the global spice trade from the Mediterranean (Alexandria and Venice) to the Iberian powers (Portugal and Spain), and finally to the Dutch, resulting in the "Golden Age" of Amsterdam and the founding of the University of Leiden.</meta>

...also established a voyage to the East Indies <note>The regions of South and Southeast Asia, primarily sought for the spice trade.</note> and the spice-bearing islands by a long circuit around the lands and jutting capes <note>A reference to the sea route around the southern tip of Africa, the Cape of Good Hope, which bypassed the traditional Middle Eastern land routes.</note>. From that time, the customs duties of the Alexandrians <note>Alexandria, Egypt, served as the primary gateway for Eastern goods into the Mediterranean before the age of discovery.</note> were gradually cut away; and that most abundant flow of tax revenue to the Venetians <note>The Republic of Venice previously held a near-monopoly on the distribution of spices in Europe.</note> was diminished in no small part. Indeed, the kings of Portugal <note>original: *Lusitaniae*</note> brought all this wealth into their own treasury. By that success and opportunity, Spain <note>original: *Hispania*; this refers to the period when the Spanish and Portuguese crowns were united, giving Spain control over the global spice trade.</note> held almost all of Europe tributary for many years; until at last the New World itself (after it had grown ashamed of such a dire yoke), as the fates turned, constructed spice and pepper warehouses in your very inner chambers at Amsterdam and elsewhere, almost without you realizing it.

Roused by this opportunity, you filled all the seas with your fleets through a zeal for navigation. From this it has come to pass that our men, for whom previously nothing was more precious than idle leisure, now stir all the seas and, where land fails, delight in wandering the Ocean itself, seeking unknown lands. And from this also, such great splendor has come to your cities, and a singular and exceptional cultivation of those arts has shone forth, for the praise of which the Phoenicians <note>The ancient maritime civilization mentioned previously as the originators of navigation and letters.</note> are so greatly celebrated.

For **YOU**, although you were occupied by a very great and difficult war <note>The Eighty Years' War, the Dutch struggle for independence against the Spanish Empire.</note> by a most powerful enemy on land and sea, nevertheless established the University of Leiden <note>original: *Academia Lugduni in Batavis*; founded in 1575, it was the first university in the Dutch Republic and a reward for the city's endurance during a Spanish siege.</note>, with men most celebrated and learn... <note>The text breaks here; the catchword "ctrina" suggests the next page continues with "learned in doctrine" or "teaching."</note>

<summary>The author describes the transition of global economic power from Venice and Portugal to the Dutch Republic, crediting maritime trade for the country's cultural and academic flourishing. This prosperity is highlighted by the founding of the University of Leiden during the war for independence.</summary>

<keywords>East Indies, spice trade, Portugal, Spain, Amsterdam, navigation, Eighty Years' War, University of Leiden, maritime empire</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to the Dutch authorities, moving from a general praise of the Republic's maritime success to the specific intellectual environment of its universities. The author expresses his personal devotion to the state and introduces his scientific work on the mathematical principles of navigation.</meta>

...learning, summoned from all sides, through whose labor noble minds might be cultivated. And indeed, the outcome has not frustrated your expectations. For you possess minds awakened to perceive every kind of learning: men, I say, no less industrious than they are talented; who strive with the greatest effort to contribute all their studies and all their labor to the **safety and renown of the Fatherland** <note>original: *Patriae salutem*; here referring to the Dutch Republic's security and global reputation.</note>. To all of them, while I would not unwillingly grant the primary honors in learning and talent, yet I will deny that I yield to anyone in affection and a spirit inclined toward our country. And if I do not achieve as much as I might desire, yet at least whatever I am able to attain through diligence and industry, I will gladly offer up as sacred to God and the Fatherland.

And so that some pledge of this affection might exist—one which, to be sure, would not be worthy of your Greatness unless you yourselves chose to deem it not unworthy—since you manage the **care of maritime affairs** <note>original: *rei marittimae*</note> so anxiously and solicitously that you even go so far as to offer rewards to invite everyone to assist and promote them. Moved therefore by your authority, *Most Illustrious States* <note>original: *Ordines Illustrissimi*; this refers to the States-General, the sovereign body of the United Provinces of the Netherlands.</note>, I considered that I would be performing a labor neither useless nor fruitless if I were to set forth the **laws of navigation** <note>original: *navigandi leges*; this refers to the mathematical and physical principles required for accurate seafaring.</note> through accurate and unfailing demonstration. This is the very thing I saw that men highly experienced in this **learned dust** <note>original: *docto pulvere*; a classical idiom referring to the painstaking work of scholars or the dust of the schoolroom and gymnasium.</note> have always attempted with all their zeal. For after the human race [was granted...] by God...

<summary>The author praises the Dutch intellectual culture and dedicates his work on the mathematical "laws of navigation" to the States-General, citing their active support for maritime innovation.</summary>
<keywords>Dutch Republic, navigation, States-General, academic rigor, maritime science, patriotism</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to the Dutch authorities. Having previously mentioned the "laws of navigation," the author now transitions into a historical and technical defense of the science of seafaring, starting with the biblical precedent of Noah and moving toward the modern scientific challenge of determining position at sea.</meta>

* 4

...having learned to navigate with God as his teacher, and since the sea itself was then without shores, **Noah** <note>original: *Noachus*</note> was carried upon the waves alone, bearing the remains of the entire world. Afterward, through human ingenuity, sails were also devised to catch the winds and storms, by whose force the ship might be driven forward. But this same human industry also placed a **bridle** <note>original: *frenum*; a metaphor for the rudder</note> upon the ship and set a pilot at the helm, so that such a vast weight might be steered by a tiny **rudder** <note>original: *gubernaculo*</note>, adjusting its movements according to the pilot's will. In this way, its course could be shifted so that it might even push against the blowing winds, and by this means, it could carry goods back and forth across the globe. 

For this very reason, people have long applied themselves so diligently to this single task: that the logic of their courses might be clearly understood, and that a science of steering might exist—one whose rules and guidance would allow them to bring a ship safely into harbor. This has been especially true within the memory of our ancestors, ever since those long voyages across the **Great Ocean** <note>original: *mare Oceanum*; referring to the Atlantic and beyond</note> were first attempted with such immense and daring boldness. For then, learned men—those whose wisdom surpassed others—devoted their unique efforts to establishing the art of navigation through a reliable system. This art depends almost entirely on having explored and determined the **longitude and latitude** <note>original: *longitudinem & latitudinem*</note> of every location. One of these—latitude—is determined daily without much difficulty from observations of the heavens. However, regarding the investigation of **longitude**...

->**Noah, navigation, rudder, longitude, latitude, Ocean**<-

<meta>catchword: investiga- (investigation)</meta>

<summary>The author traces the history of navigation from Noah to modern times, arguing that the safety of global trade depends on the mathematical "art of navigation," specifically the determination of latitude and longitude.</summary>
<keywords>Noah, history of navigation, shipbuilding, rudder, longitude, latitude, maritime science, Dutch Golden Age</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to the Dutch authorities. Having previously discussed the general history of navigation from Noah to the Great Ocean, the author now focuses on the discovery of the magnet (the compass) as the essential tool for modern seafaring and the ongoing scientific quest to solve the problem of longitude.</meta>

...investigation, no help or protection was available anywhere. Finally, the **Magnet** <note>original: *Magnes*; referring to the lodestone or magnetic compass</note> was discovered and dug out from the very bowels of the earth; a stone unpolished indeed, without luster, dark, rough, and scabby, with a rusty color; but yet to be compared against any gemstone. This is that magnet which, having taken pity on us like a most dutiful **guide** <term>ξεναγωγος (xenagōgos): a Greek term for a guide or leader of strangers</term>, leads wretched mortals—even those reckless with their own lives—safe and sound across the whole world. Even in the darkness, it shines a torch upon those errors; and by night or by day, like some **attendant** <note>original: *nomenclator*; historically a servant who announced names or provided directions</note>, it reports the regions of all courses and performs the duty of a faithful monitor.

And from that point, the thought was struck into sagacious men to scrutinize more accurately the properties of these courses. From which it was immediately noted that a very fine method for investigating **longitude and latitude** could be derived from this source; and that from the region of the course and its estimated quantity, these coordinates come daily to the **calculation table** <note>original: *abacum*; referring to a counting board or maritime chart used for calculation</note>. 

Therefore, for an entire century now, both the learned and the unlearned have labored in competition to scrutinize the laws of that line which is described by ships under the guidance of the **magnetic needle** <note>original: *magneticæ acus*</note>, and to explain them more accurately. Since there exists no other way of finding **longitude** in the middle of the sea except that one which is drawn out by the evidence of the magnetic needle. And indeed, all the maritime shores have long been hammering at this **anvil** <note>original: *incudem*; a metaphor for working tirelessly on a difficult problem</note>; and not only privately, but also publicly. Your [Honors] have also felt this matter,

<summary>The author describes the discovery of the magnet as a divinely-sent guide for sailors, enabling the calculation of longitude and latitude which has been the primary scientific challenge of the last century.</summary>
<keywords>magnet, compass, navigation, longitude, latitude, magnetic needle, maritime history</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to the Dutch States General (the *Illustrissimi Ordines*). The previous page ended while discussing the magnetic needle; this page picks up by acknowledging the government's support for maritime science and the author's motivation to solve the problem of longitude.</meta>

*Most Illustrious States*, [you have shown] also your care. For how often have those who attempted to make the use of **rhumb lines** <term>loxodromia: paths of constant bearing on a chart, which appear as straight lines on a Mercator projection but as spirals on a globe</term> easier and more successful experienced your generosity? This remains true whether they explained the concept on flat charts or on the curved segments of spheres. Indeed, no one is thought to have aided maritime affairs in this regard who has not actually experienced that YOU strive with all solicitude and care so that this branch of science might be as perfect as its nature allows. You consider this work so worthy of your Majesty <note>original: *Majeſtate*; here referring to the sovereign authority of the Dutch States General</note> that you even voluntarily encourage the studies of everyone.

And not without cause: since the fortunes of so many men are daily committed to the rigging and float in that vast Ocean. For just as we yield to no nation in the industry of sailing, so in the multitude and agility of our vessels we are easily the equals of the rest. And since many men, out of a praiseworthy zeal for serving their country well, frequently weary your ears and desire to present to you methods for determining **longitude** <note>original: *longitudinis rationes*; the "longitude problem"—finding one's East-West position at sea—was the most critical scientific challenge for 17th-century navigation</note> explained for use at sea: I did not think that I too should be wanting to my country, or to those eager for these matters. 

Therefore, I have resolved to derive all these things from the very foundation with solid reasoning, so that from there it might be definitely established how much the matter itself yields at sea, or how close it approaches the truth. I have therefore made an effort so that, with the difficulties noted with the greatest diligence—which can be avoided, or [interposed]...

<summary>The author praises the Dutch States General for their ongoing patronage of maritime science, particularly the study of rhumb lines and longitude. He explains his intention to provide a mathematically sound foundation for these navigation methods to benefit the nation's vast merchant fleet.</summary>
<keywords>Dutch States General, rhumb lines, longitude problem, navigation, maritime history, cartography, Dutch East India Company era</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the formal dedication to the Dutch States General. ...it is necessary to explain, I might bring forth into the public eye certain and brief rules, easy in execution and ready for use, and establish all things confirmed by mathematical demonstrations, verified by practice, and organized by method and path. Just as I am most thoroughly convinced that these things will be welcomed by all good men and those eager for the truth, so I uniquely hope that this labor of mine might also be approved by you, **Most Illustrious States** <note>original: *Ordines Illustrissimi*; the States General, the governing body of the Dutch Republic</note>,

->*To Your Excellence*<-

->WILLEBRORD SNELLIUS<-
->*of Royen, son of Rudolph* <note>original: *à Royen R. F.*; the "R. F." stands for *Rudolphi Filius*, indicating he is the son of the mathematician Rudolph Snellius</note><-

<meta>This page begins the preface to the reader. There is no preceding page provided for continuity.</meta>

## TO THE KIND READER.

<note>A decorative initial letter 'C' is illustrated with a small human figure, perhaps a cherub or child, holding an object within a square frame decorated with leaves and scrolls.</note>

*When the providence of the supreme deity had separated that first mass of all things—under which the unformed bulk of all nature lay hidden—into its proper places; and when he had dissociated these lower parts, whether by a divinely implanted magnetic force or by a balanced <term>equilibrium<note>original: "ratione Isorropica," referring to the Greek concept of equal balance or weight distribution</note></term> of elements around that center point of the world: Indeed, that breathable air above us and the even purer element <note>The "purer element" refers to the Aether, which ancient and medieval science believed filled the celestial spheres above the air.</note> chose their own seats. But so that the nucleus of that mass—the globe of the earth, heavier than the rest and closely surrounded and encircled by watery moisture—might not be uninhabitable for land animals; therefore, that Divine Mind, the architect of the world, broke apart this Earth and commanded the mountains to rise and the valleys to sink. He did this so that in these cavities, as if in bosoms, the Earth might receive the moisture which would otherwise overflow and float upon everything, or so that it might roll out into the vast sea <note>original: "æquor"</note> that which had formed and been cast out from the Earth's bowels. And thus, with the boundaries of the various elements established—the earth rising and the water receding within the form of a perfect sphere—the Creator separated the human race, some in one place and some in another. From this, whether by necessity or by desire, a longing entered mortal minds to traverse lands divided by such an incompatible element <note>The "incompatible element" refers to the sea, which naturally separates landmasses.</note>. From the desire and ingenuity of men, just as neither the air nor the innermost <term>secrets<note>original: "penetralia," referring to the hidden or sacred inner parts of a place</note></term> of the earth itself could remain safe from exploration, so too neither the violence of the sea’s surface, nor the mad waves, nor the treacherous shallows, nor [the wind...]*

<sig>**</sig>
<meta>catchword: vento-</meta>

<summary>This preface describes the creation of the world, explaining how the Divine Architect separated the elements and shaped the Earth's geography to make it habitable for humans. It sets the stage for a discussion on man's innate drive to explore the seas and the hidden parts of the world despite natural barriers.</summary>
<keywords>Providence, Creation, Four Elements, Geography, Exploration, Divine Architect, Equilibrium, Equilibrium of Elements</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface "To the Kind Reader," following the discussion of how the Creator separated the land from the waters and divided the human race across the globe.</meta>

*...the force of the roaring winds could not restrain their efforts, nor prevent them from approaching regions separated by such an immense ocean for goods from another World, sought after for luxury. They would have sought heaven itself if any scent of profit breathed from there. Indeed, in the first centuries, and under the very cradle of the world, as long as mortals lived their lives in inland places, where they endured life sparingly and harshly on ancestral hearths and lands,*

->---The turbulent waters of the sea<-
->did not destroy ships upon the rocks and men.<-
->But the sea, with waves often rising, raged<-
->recklessly and in vain, and lightly set aside its empty threats.<-
->Nor could the treacherous allure of the swift sea<-
->entice anyone into deception with smiling waves.<-
->The wicked art <note>original: "ratio," here implying the method or system of navigation</note> of sailing then lay hidden in darkness.<-

<note>These verses are adapted from the Roman poet Lucretius’s work *On the Nature of Things*. They describe a "Golden Age" of human history where, despite the sea's natural violence, men were safe because they had not yet invented the "wicked" art of navigation driven by greed.</note>

---

*The Phoenicians first, with their trades and wares, imported greed, magnificence, and insatiable desires for all things into Greece,* says Cicero <note>The author here quotes a Greek phrase from Herodotus: "ἀπαγινέοντες φόρτια Αἰγύπτιά τε & Ἀσσύρια," meaning "carrying away Egyptian and Assyrian cargoes."</note>. *And truly, this nation of the Phoenicians was, according to Pliny, held in great glory for the invention of letters and the study of the stars, as well as for naval and military arts. Herodotus also writes at the threshold of his histories: The Phoenicians, he says, who set out from the sea called "Red"* <note>To ancient writers, the "Red Sea" often referred to the entire Indian Ocean or the Persian Gulf, rather than just the modern Red Sea.</note>, *having established their dwellings in that region which they inhabit even now, immediately applied themselves to long-distance voyages; and by transporting Egyptian and Assyrian goods, when into other*

<meta>catchword: [other]wise (continuing "alias")</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the transition from a primitive age of inland living to the era of maritime commerce, highlighting the Phoenicians as the pioneers of long-distance trade and navigation.</summary>
<keywords>Phoenicians, history of navigation, Cicero, Pliny, Herodotus, maritime trade, Golden Age, Red Sea, ancient commerce</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Phoenicians' role in early maritime history, following the mention of their reputation for trade and naval skill. It recounts the story of Io as told by the Persians in Herodotus's Histories.</meta>

<sig>** 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: rum</meta>

[...to other] regions, and they also reached Argos. For at that time, Argos excelled all the other states of Greece. The Persians recount that the Phoenicians, having put into port here, displayed their merchandise; and on the fifth or sixth day after their arrival, when almost everything had been sold off, several women came down to the sea, among whom was the king’s daughter. Her name was the same as that handed down by the Greeks: Io, daughter of Inachus <note>Inachus was the mythical founder and first king of Argos.</note>. While these women stood by the ship and were purchasing what they most desired, the Phoenicians, by common consent, made an attack upon them. While most of the women escaped by flight, Io and a few others were seized, and the Phoenicians turned their sails back toward Egypt. Surely, as it was said:

> You, O Nile, remained as the final task of that immense labor. <note>original: *Vltimus immenso restabas Nile labori.* This verse suggests that Egypt and the Nile were the final destination of Io’s long and wearying journey.</note>

Yet, the glory of the art of navigation and the method of ship construction is claimed for himself by the most ancient Prometheus in the works of Aeschylus:

"None other than I invented the sea-wandering, flaxen-winged <note>original Greek: *Λινόπτερ'* (linoptera), referring to the linen used for sails.</note> chariots of sailors." <note>original Greek: *Θαλασσόπλαγκτα δ' οὔτις ἄλλος ἀντ' ἐμοῦ / Λινόπτερ' εὗρε ναυτίλων ὀχήματα.* From the play *Prometheus Bound* by the Greek tragedian Aeschylus.</note>

And truly, those who brought ships to unknown shores, and who—whether by oars or by spread sails—scoured the seas in a vehicle never before seen by the inhabitants until that day, struck the minds of the Barbarians <note>In this context, "Barbarians" refers to non-Greek or non-Phoenician indigenous peoples.</note> with no less awe than the ship *Argo* struck the mind of that shepherd in the works of Attius <note>Referring to Lucius Accius, an early Roman tragic poet whose work often survives only in fragments.</note>. Having never seen a ship before, he gazed upon the divine and strange vessel of the Argonauts from afar, stunned and terrified. Indeed, such a discovery was so welcome in those times that whoever devised a more convenient or swifter type of vessel, or led an expedition to shores never before reached...

<summary>This page discusses the legendary origins of sailing, recounting the Phoenician abduction of Io to Egypt and citing the mythical Prometheus as the inventor of ships. It describes the profound awe and terror that early maritime technology inspired in people who had never seen ships before.</summary>

The flight of Phryxus and Helle <note>In Greek myth, these siblings fled their stepmother on the back of a magical golden ram.</note> is attributed to a golden-fleeced <note>original Greek: *χρυσομάλλῳ* (chrysomallō)</note> ram who carried them; for this kind service, it shines as the leader among the signs of the Zodiac <note>The constellation Aries.</note> and opens the spring for us. All the fables of the poets echo with this story; every stage teems with it, and from that event, a name was even given to the narrowest strait between Asia and Europe <note>The Hellespont, named after Helle, who fell into the sea during the flight.</note>. But while Phryxus was fleeing the snares of his stepmother with his sister Helle, he happened upon a ship whose figurehead was a ram <note>original Greek: *ἐτύχησε πλοίῳ ᾧ τὸ παράσημον κριὸς ἦν*</note>; he found a ship whose emblem—and thus also its name—was "The Ram." For it stood on the prow, either painted or sculpted.

Yet, because many accounts concerning this ship’s emblem or figurehead <note>original Greek: *παρασήμῳ* (parasēmō)</note> exist—passed down not only by grammarians but even by the most learned philologists—which are too playful and exceedingly absurd, it will be worthwhile to hear Joseph Scaliger <note>Joseph Justus Scaliger (1540–1609) was a towering figure in Renaissance scholarship, known for his work on historical chronology and classical texts.</note> explain these matters in detail. "The figurehead <note>original Greek: *παράσημον* (parasēmon)</note>," he says, "was on the prow: and there was placed a God, a Hero, a beast, or something else from which the ship took its name." In the 28th chapter <note>The Latin text cites chapter 27, but the account of the ship with the Dioscuri emblem occurs in Acts 28:11.</note> of the Acts of the Apostles: *on a ship with the figurehead of the Dioscuri* <note>original Greek: *ἐν πλοίῳ παρασήμῳ Διοσκύροις*</note>; clearly, the figurehead of the ship on its prow featured the Dioscuri <note>The "Heavenly Twins" Castor and Pollux, protectors of sailors. The son of a Trierarch <note>A commander of a trireme.</note>, from whose Liburnian ship <note>A fast, light galley used by the Roman navy.</note> the figurehead <note>original Latin: *parasia*, likely a corruption of the Greek word for emblem.</note> received its name. <meta>This page continues the discussion on how ancient ships were named and identified, specifically focusing on the distinction between a ship's figurehead and its guardian deity. The previous page established that the legendary "golden ram" was actually a ship with a ram figurehead.</meta>

<sig>** 3</sig>

...let it be allowed to take "parasia" for "figurehead," <note>original: *parasemo*</note> if the passage is not free of error. And that Liburnian ship <note>A fast, light type of galley used by the Romans.</note> had a Pegasus as its figurehead <note>original Greek: *παράσημον* (parasēmon)</note>. 

But the Guardian Spirit <note>original: *Tutela*</note> was placed on the stern. This differed from the figurehead in two ways: first, because the figurehead’s place was always on the prow, while the Guardian’s was on the stern; and second, because the Guardian was always a God or Goddess. The figurehead <note>original Greek: *παράσημον* (parasēmon)</note> could be a God, or a Hero, or an animal, or something else. Finally, a ship took its name from the figurehead, not from its Guardian Spirit. Ovid writes:

> I have a ship—and I pray it remains safe—under the protection of golden-haired Minerva,
> and it takes its name from a painted Helmet. <note>Ovid, *Tristia* 1.10.1-2. Here, the goddess Minerva protects the ship from the stern, but the ship is actually named "The Helmet" after the image on its prow.</note>

To be sure, "The Helmet" was the figurehead <note>original Greek: *τὸ παράσημον* (to parasēmon)</note>, and from it the ship was called *The Helmet*. Minerva, however, who was the Guardian, was stationed on the stern or in the stern-ornament <note>original: *Aplustri*, the decorative, fan-like carving at the rear of an ancient ship.</note>. In the first book of the *Iliad*: "For he hastens to lop off the high stern-ornaments of the ships." <note>Homer, *Iliad* 9.241. Original Greek: *σεῦται γὰρ νηῶν ἀποκόψειν ἄκρα κόρυμβα*.</note> A commentary <note>original: *Scholion*</note> notes: "Since upon the highest points of the sterns were statues and images of the gods." <note>Original Greek: *ἐπειδὴ ἐπὶ τῶν ἀκροτολίων ἦσαν ἀγάλματα καὶ εἰκόνες τῶν θεῶν*.</note> Hesychius <note>A 5th-century Greek grammarian and lexicographer.</note> says: *Pataikoi*. Gods of the Phoenicians, whom they set up on the sterns of their ships. <note>Original Greek: *Πάταικοι. Θεοὶ, Φοίνικες οὓς ἱστᾶσι κατὰ τὰς πρύμνας τῶν νεῶν*.</note> Persius writes:

> Great Gods from the stern ——— <note>Persius, *Satires* 6.30.</note>

An old glossary <note>original: *Glossa vetus*</note> states: "He speaks of the Guardian Spirit of the ships, which they have or paint on the sterns." Plutarch writes: "...inquiring the name of both the ship-owner and the pilot, and the figurehead of the ship." <note>Original Greek: *πυνθανόμενος τοῦ τε ναυκλήρου τὄνομα & τοῦ κῠβερνήτου, & τῆς νεὼς τὸ παράσημον*.</note> For the ship took its name from the figurehead <note>original Greek: *παρασήμου*</note>, just as that ship in which the Apostle Paul sailed was called *The Twin Sons of Zeus*. <note>Acts 28:11. The original Latin text uses *Castores*, referring to Castor and Pollux.</note> Indeed, I know that many have written concerning the Guardian Spirits of ships. But I see that they have not well...

to distinguish these things: then also that the Grammarians confuse the figurehead <note>original Greek: *παράσημον* (parasēmon)</note> with the Guardian Spirit <note>original: *Tutela*</note>. The Greeks have a word for the figurehead, but for the Guardian, they have no term by which to express it.

Therefore, the Ram of Phrixus <note>In Greek myth, Phrixus escaped his stepmother on a flying golden ram; the author argues this "ram" was simply a ship.</note> was not a ram, but a type of ship. The fable concerning the Bull is not dissimilar.

Thus Europa, the daughter of Agenor, King of Phoenicia, was snatched by the Cretans in a ship whose figurehead <note>original: *parasēmon*</note> insignia was a Bull; and this, as Herodotus interprets from the histories of the Persians, was an act of retribution <note>original Greek: *ἀντίποινον* (antipoinon)</note>, a compensation for injuries previously inflicted by the Phoenicians. <note>In the opening of his *Histories*, Herodotus records a Persian view that the kidnapping of Europa was a "tit-for-tat" response to the earlier kidnapping of the Greek princess Io.</note> Thus ships, either from their shape or from their figurehead, are turned into fables and miracles. <meta>This page continues the author’s argument that ancient myths involving flying or magical animals were actually allegorical descriptions of early sailing vessels. ...[he] taught [men] to feed on rejected fruits and, by the command of Ceres <note>The Roman goddess of agriculture (the Greek Demeter).</note>, established the art of agriculture.

> The fertile goddess harnessed a pair of snakes
> To her chariots, and reined in their mouths with bits:
> And was carried through the air between heaven and earth.
> And she sent her light chariot to the Tritonian citadel
> To Triptolemus <note>A mythological hero chosen by Ceres to spread the knowledge of grain-growing to the world.</note>, and commanded him to scatter the seeds given
> Partly in the wild earth, and partly in land long recultivated.
> Now the youth had been carried high over Europe and the land of Asia,
> And he turned toward the Scythian shores.
<note>A passage from Ovid’s *Metamorphoses* (5.642–649), describing Triptolemus’s journey to spread agriculture using a chariot pulled by dragons/snakes.</note>

*And what follows.* Yet Philochorus <note>An Athenian historian (c. 340–261 BCE) who specialized in the local history and myths of Athens.</note> is the authority who states that Triptolemus, arriving at maritime cities in a "long ship," distributed the grain. The ship was thought to be a winged serpent <note>original Greek: *πτερωτὸν ὄφιν* (pteroton ophin)</note> because it reflected this appearance in its shape. <note>original: *Triptolemum longa navi ad civitates maritimas appulsum frumentum distribuisse. navem autem visam esse anguem alatum, quia hanc specieim utcunque sua figura referret.*</note> I believe that the beaks <note>original: *rostra*</note> of ships expressed the likeness of the beaks or snouts of certain animals, which is why they were even called *rostra* <note>The Latin word *rostrum* refers to both a bird's beak and the prow of a ship.</note>. And thus the ancients wrapped the truth of historical events in the colors of fables, and passed them down through the generations. The fable of Daedalus and Icarus was composed by no other reasoning.

> Daedalus (as the story goes), fleeing the realms of Minos,
> Dared to trust himself to the sky on swift wings;
> He sailed away toward the frozen North by an unaccustomed path,
> And at last hovered lightly above the Chalcidian citadel.
<note>A quote from Virgil’s *Aeneid* (6.14–17), describing the flight of Daedalus from Crete. The author implies the "wings" were actually sails.</note>

But he also is fabled to have **sailed away** <note>original Greek: *ἀποπλεῦσαι μυθεύεται* (apopleusai mytheuetai)</note> with his son Icarus by taking a boat and escaping Minos; because he became invisible to his pursuer, it is told in the manner of a fable that he flew away with his son Icarus, because having obtained a ship he escaped Minos when those pursuing him could not find him. I would judge that he did not attempt his flight in a large ship, lest he give even the slightest suspicion of his escape to the Tyrant; but rather that they committed themselves to individual smaller skiffs <note>The author uses the Latin *cymbulis*, referring to small, light boats.</note> covered on all sides with leather: just as the barbarians even today near the **Davis Strait in America** <note>The Davis Strait lies between Greenland and Nunavut, Canada. <meta>This page continues the author’s rationalistic interpretation of Greek myths, moving from the flight of Daedalus to the rescue of Andromeda by Perseus and the legend of Bellerophon. The author argues that "monsters" and "winged horses" were actually the names of specific ships.</meta>

*...borders from our sea <note>The Mediterranean</note> as far as that part of Arabia that reaches the **Red Sea** <note>original Latin: *Erythræum mare*</note>. It is said that Cepheus had a daughter of extraordinary beauty sought by many suitors, including a certain Phoenix and Cepheus's own brother, Phineus. But Cepheus, after weighing various considerations regarding both men, finally decided to betroth his daughter to Phoenix. He chose to mask his own consent, however, by staging a kidnapping of the girl. Thus, Andromeda was snatched away from a certain deserted island where she used to go to offer sacrifices to **Venus** <note>The Roman name for Aphrodite, goddess of love.</note>. While Andromeda was being carried off in Phoenix’s ship—which was named the **Sea Monster** <note>original Latin/Greek: *Cetus*</note>, either because of its resemblance to that creature or by some other chance—she wailed miserably and tearfully called for help, as if her father knew nothing of the plan. By chance, Perseus, the son of Danaë, was sailing past and halted his course. At the first sight of the girl, he was seized by both pity and love. He then captured that ship named the *Cetus* and killed the crew, who were turned nearly to stone with fear. This, then, is the "Sea Monster" of the Greek fable, and these crewmen were the men "turned to stone" by the sight of the **Gorgon's head** <note>In myth, Perseus used Medusa's severed head to petrify his enemies; the author here suggests this was a metaphor for their paralyzing terror during a naval boarding.</note>. And Palaephatus, in the first book of his *Incredible Tales* <note>original Latin: *Mirabilium Historiarum*</note>—the only one of five volumes that survives today—reclaims this same fable for historical truth in a slightly different manner.*

**Bellerophon** (he says) was a Phrygian, a man of Corinthian descent, noble and good, who, having built a long ship, plundered coastal regions. The name of the ship was **Pegasus**, just as every ship today has its own name. <note>original Greek: *Βελλεροφόντης (φησὶ) ἦν Φρύγιος, ἀνὴρ τὸ γένος Κορίνθιος, καλὸς κἀγαθὸς, ὃς πλοῖον κατασκευάσας μακρὸν ἐλήϊζετο τὰ παραθαλάσσια χωρία. ὄνομα ἦν τῷ πλοίῳ Πήγασος, ὡς καὶ νῦν ἕκαστον τῶν πλοίων ὄνομα ἔχει.*</note> *Bellerophon, he says, was a Phrygian, a Corinthian by birth, a strenuous man, who, having built a long ship, [raided] from maritime locations...*

<meta>This page continues the author’s rationalistic interpretation of Greek myths. Having previously argued that the "winged horse" Pegasus and the "sea monster" Cetus were actually ships, the author now applies this logic to the monster Scylla and the voyage of the Argonauts.</meta>

...was taking plunder. The ship's name was ***Pegasus***; just as individual ships are assigned a specific name. So too in **Trojan times**, the **Tyrrhenians** <note>An ancient people of the Mediterranean, often associated with the Etruscans and frequently depicted as pirates in Greek myth.</note> practiced piracy, infesting the sea with their **scouts** <term>myoparonibus: light, fast vessels favored by pirates</term> and **triremes** <term>triremibus: standard ancient warships with three banks of oars</term>. One of these triremes, named the ***Scylla***, surpassed the others in speed and lay in wait for merchant vessels above all others. As **Palaephatus** <note>An ancient author whose work *On Incredible Tales* sought to explain myths as misunderstood history.</note> says, **Ulysses** <note>The Roman name for the Greek hero Odysseus.</note> successfully escaped that trireme by utilizing both the speed of his own ship and a favorable wind. <note>original Greek: ὡς ἡ ναῦς τειήρης ταχεῖα, τὸ τέ ὄνομα Σκύλλα · αὕτη ἡ τειήρης τὰ λοιπὰ πλοίων συλλαμβάνουσα πολλάκις εἰργάζετο βρῶμα. Translation: "Because the ship was a swift trireme, and its name was Scylla; this trireme, by capturing the rest of the ships, often produced 'food' [booty]."</note> The "prince of poets" <note>A traditional reference to the Roman poet Virgil.</note> seems to have had this in mind when, in his fifth book, he grants victory to **Cloanthus**, who was being carried by the *Scylla*.

---

— *She fled toward the land swifter than the **South Wind** <note>original Latin: Noto</note> and a winged arrow, and hid herself in the deep harbor.* <note>A quote from Virgil’s Aeneid, Book 5, describing the speed of the ship Scylla during a race.</note>

In these matters, everything has so far been shrouded in the fictions of myths. But what of that man who first of all tempted the seas with a **long ship** <term>longa nave: a galley or warship, typically longer and narrower than a merchant vessel</term>? With what great renown is he discussed in the speech of men? With what great scope is he celebrated? It truly seems to the poets that he "unlocked the Ocean" of the fables.

*Would that the fir-timbers, hewn by axes in the **Pelian grove** <note>A forest on Mount Pelion in Greece, where the wood for the Argo was sourced.</note>, had never fallen to the earth! And would that the beginning of the ship’s construction had never started there—that ship which is now called the ***Argo***, in which the chosen **Argive** men sailed to seek that golden fleece of the ram from **Colchis**, under the command of King **Pelias** by means of deceit.* <note>This is a translation of a famous passage from the Roman playwright Ennius’s *Medea*, based on Euripides, describing the launch of the first ship.</note>

<summary>The author explains the monster Scylla as a fast pirate ship and transitions into the history of the Argo, the first legendary warship. This continues the "Euhemerist" approach of interpreting mythological wonders as misunderstood maritime technology.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author’s rationalizing approach to mythology. Having previously argued that the "monsters" encountered by heroes were actually rival ships, the author now turns to the famous voyage of the Argo, providing a historical and technical breakdown of its crew and construction to show it was a real, albeit remarkable, vessel.</meta>

*** 2
<meta>catchword: --- Caesar</meta>

**Philostephanus** is the authority for the claim that **Jason** was the first to sail in a "long ship" <note>A "long ship" usually refers to a galley or warship, as opposed to a "round ship" used for merchant transport.</note>. **Pliny** says in the fifty-sixth chapter of his seventh book:

> The **Thessalian pine** <note>original Latin: *Thessala pinus*. This refers to the ship *Argo*, built from timber in Thessaly.</note> drew into one the treaties 
> of a well-divided world, 
> and ordered the sea to suffer lashings. <note>This quote is from the Roman playwright Seneca’s *Medea*, lamenting how the first ship broke the natural boundaries set by the gods.</note>

This was the first **penteconter** <term>penteconteros: an ancient Greek galley with fifty oars, twenty-five on each side</term>, and fifty-four **Argonauts** entered it with **Jason**. This was indeed that "select band of heroes," the **flower of the god-born heroes** <note>original Greek: θεῶν ἡρώων ἄωτος</note>. Into this single vessel the flower of all **Greece** gathered: **Hercules**, **Castor**, and **Pollux**, born of the gods; and others who were both kings and the sons of kings. The prophets **Mopsus** and **Idmon** were there, as was the shipbuilder **Argus**:

> ————— lest by any 
> part the stern draw in the silent sea, it is your lot 
> to close the fixed wounds from the waves 
> with either pitch or soft wax. <note>A quote from the *Argonautica* of Valerius Flaccus, describing the maintenance of the hull.</note>

The **boatswain** <term>celeustes: the officer who gave the stroke to the rowers</term> was **Orpheus** himself,

> ————— who by song teaches 
> the oars to move, so that they do not fight 
> here and there against the surface of the whirlpool.

The master and pilot of the ship was **Tiphys**,

> Who knew the ambushes of the sea, the stars of the 
> clear night, and for whom **Aeolus** <note>The divine keeper of the winds in Greek mythology.</note> marks the departure 
> from the closed caves. He would not fear to entrust 
> the heavens to him who ruled the ship's kingdom.

Finally, there is a legend that the ship itself was prophetic and spoke with a human voice; for it had **a keel made from the oak of Dodona** <note>original Greek: τρόπιν ἐκ τῆς δωδώνης δρυός. Dodona was the site of a famous oracle of Zeus where the rustling leaves of oak trees were interpreted as divine messages.</note>. For some say **Juno**, others **Minerva**...

*Hence also it is called the "divine vehicle of the Argonauts" by **Cicero**. Wherefore it is clearly evident from this that the individual nations bordering the Mediterranean Sea attempted to claim the invention of ships for themselves, or strove to commend their unique services and perils to posterity within the wrappings of fables. And so that they might fix these things more firmly in the minds of men, they were not ashamed to inscribe their names upon the stars and the heavens. For there shine for us **Aries** (the Ram), **Taurus** (the Bull), **Pegasus**, **Cete** (the Whale), and **Argo**—the names of ships—as so many witnesses of these expeditions; by their eternal fires and brilliance, the **Phoenicians**, **Cretans**, **Corinthians**, and **Thessalians** have made their industry attested to future generations. <meta>This page continues the transition from ancient seafaring myths to the biblical account of Noah. It picks up mid-sentence, describing the moral decline of humanity prior to the Flood.</meta>

*...had crossed the line, and would not listen to Noah’s preaching and warnings; but having disregarded the admonitions of the divine old man, they even rushed daily into worse behavior, and withdrew their step from all virtue. Then at last, as if dealing with a deep-seated evil and desperate circumstances, God himself—in order to damn this wickedness to eternal darkness—instructed Noah in **shipbuilding** (<term>ναυπηγίαν</term> <note>original Greek: naupēgian, the art of ship construction</note>), and showed him how He wished each thing to be done and the storehouse of the world to be guarded. He taught which living creatures were to be reserved for seed* <note>Meaning for the repopulation of the earth.</note>*, and how many of each kind were to be admitted into the ark, and where and how they should be stabled. That construction of the vessel lasted a full century; we call it an **Ark** from the form of a chest (<term>κίβωτον</term> <note>original Greek: kibōtos, meaning a wooden box or coffer</note>). God himself also prescribed the frame and the proportions of the ark.*

*"Let the length of the ark," He said, "be three hundred cubits, its width fifty, and its height thirty."* <note>original: "Trecentorum... cubitorum...". A cubit was an ancient unit of measurement, approximately the length of a man's forearm from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger.</note> *It was indeed a very ample and vast space, so that its **ground-area** (<term>podiſmus</term> <note>original: podismus, a technical term for the measurement of a surface area or "footprint"</note>) calculated from the length and width is found to be greater than a **Roman acre and a half** (<term>ſeſquijugero</term> <note>original: sesquijugero. A jugerum was an ancient Roman unit of area; one and a half jugera is roughly equivalent to one modern acre.</note>). But perhaps to certain overly inquisitive men, who are not ashamed to measure divine works by the standard of the human brain, these things might seem less believable if we compare this massive structure to the ships of our own age; furthermore, the symmetry of its parts might seem hardly suited to our current maritime customs. And yet, the extravagant follies of kings playing upon the sea, who built pleasure-galleys and vessels with bedchambers (<term>triremes</term> <note>original: triremes, which were typically three-banked warships, but here refers to massive, multi-decked luxury vessels of antiquity</note>), will provide more than enough proof. For these men, undoubtedly spurred on by this very example, emulated that great construction; for even this ark is mentioned by secular writers...*

...was celebrated in the records of literature; if we believe **Josephus** <note>Flavius Josephus (c. 37–100 AD) was a Romano-Jewish historian whose works provide crucial insights into First-Century Judaism.</note>, who calls upon the authority of **Berosus the Chaldean**, **Hieronymus the Egyptian**, **Mnaseas**, **Nicolaus of Damascus**, and many others. **Ptolemy Philopator** built a ship of forty rows, two hundred and eighty cubits long, thirty-eight wide within the gangways, and forty-eight high up to the figurehead ornament; moreover, it was fifty-three cubits from the highest part of the stern down to the sea. When it set out on its course at sea, it received more than four thousand rowers, four hundred attendants, and nearly three thousand marines, besides another crowd of men beneath the thwarts, and furthermore, a vast amount of provisions. <meta>This page continues the catalog of monumental ancient vessels, specifically moving from the massive ships of the Ptolemies in Egypt to the legendary Syracosia, built for Hiero II of Syracuse under the supervision of Archimedes. He also arranged for another ship to be built to sail on the Nile: a *thalamegos* <note>original Greek: <term>θαλάμηγον</term>, literally a "cabin-carrier." This was a massive, palatial river barge used by the Hellenistic kings of Egypt for royal progresses.</note> fitted with private chambers. It was half a stadium in length (which is three hundred feet), thirty cubits wide, and nearly forty cubits high. As for the rest of its ornamentation, the arrangement of its various parts, and its proportions, one may see them described in that same source <note>Likely referring to Callixenus of Rhodes, whose detailed descriptions of Alexandria were preserved by the author Athenaeus.</note>. 

But that famous ship of **Hiero, Tyrant of Syracuse**, easily surpassed all of these; for its construction, **Archimedes the geometer** was the overseer <note>original Greek: <term>ἧς Ἀρχιμήδης ὁ γεωμέτρης ὦν ἐπόπτης</term>. Archimedes (c. 287–212 BC) is considered the greatest mathematician of antiquity. His involvement in naval architecture resulted in the *Syracosia*, the largest ship of the ancient world.</note>. **Moschion** <note>An ancient writer known for his technical treatise on the "Syracosia," which is our primary source for the ship's incredible details.</note> explained the immense size and structure of this mass in an entire book dedicated to the subject. 

For with three hundred craftsmen working on the structure—besides the assistants—the timber required for sixty triremes <note>A trireme was the standard heavy warship of the Mediterranean, typically requiring a vast amount of oak, fir, and pine.</note> was gathered together to build it. The body of the ship was so divided within and distributed into its own quarters that specific places and accommodations were assigned for receiving the rowers, sailors, soldiers, and the rest of the passengers. In this same ship, there were furthermore various dining rooms, bedrooms, walkways, gardens, a fish pond, stables, lecture halls, a temple of Venus, baths, ovens, kitchens, mills, and many other things; and all of these were within the hull. 

Moreover, the ship itself was surrounded by an iron rampart and fortified by eight towers: two at the stern, the same number at the bow, and the rest arranged along the sides. Above the ship, on the outside, there was a wall with battlements; and in the wall, the towers, and the mast-tops <note>Latin: <term>carchesia</term>, referring to the structures at the top of the masts which served as "crow's nests" for lookouts and archers.</note> throughout the ship, many war machines were positioned. Among the rest was one machine <note>This likely refers to a giant catapult or "lithobolos" (stone-thrower) designed by Archimedes.</note> which could hurl a three-hundred-pound stone and a twelve-cubit-long arrow to a distance of six hundred feet. And lest [I recount] all...

Yet the Romans seem to have surpassed even this "Syracusan ornament" <note>original Greek: <term>ἐκκόσμην</term> (*ekkosmēn*), referring to the ornate and massive ship built by Hiero of Syracuse.</note> by constructing ships specifically for transporting obelisks from Alexandria all the way to **Ostia** <note>The primary harbor city of ancient Rome, located at the mouth of the Tiber River.</note>. Above all (says **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, *Natural History*, Book 36, Chapter 14 (noted as Chapter 9 in this text).</note> in Book 36, Chapter 9), there was the difficulty of transporting them to Rome by sea in ships that were quite remarkable.

The **Divine Augustus** had brought the first of these, and for the sake of the wonder it inspired, he dedicated it in the permanent docks at **Puteoli** <note>A major Roman port near modern-day Naples, known for its advanced engineering.</note>; but it was later consumed by fire. The **Divine Claudius** took the ship that **Gaius Caesar** <note>The Emperor Caligula.</note> had used for transport—a vessel more wonderful than any ever seen upon the sea—and after keeping it for several years, he brought it to Ostia. This is pozzolana, a volcanic ash found near Puteoli that allowed Roman concrete to set underwater.</note> and sank the ship to serve as a foundation for the harbor's breakwater.

The most learned **Lazarus Bayfius** <note>Lazare de Baïf (1496–1547), a French humanist whose work *De re navali* was a cornerstone of 16th-century naval archaeology.</note> pursued the subject of naval history—covering the types of ships, their parts, decoration, and armaments—in a quite substantial volume. <meta>This page transitions from the description of massive individual vessels like the Syracosia to a scholarly classification of ancient naval architecture. The author cites Joseph Scaliger, a preeminent Renaissance scholar, to provide a definitive taxonomy of "long ships" based on their banks and oars.</meta>

The preeminent man, and truly the peerless *Phoenix* of our century, **Joseph Scaliger** <note>Joseph Justus Scaliger (1540–1609) was one of the greatest scholars of the Renaissance, known for his work in chronology and classical philology.</note>, has explained this entire passage with a most successful and learned hand; thus, for your sake, it will not be bothersome to transcribe that illustrious selection here. For it is worthy of being read in more than just one place.

*Therefore (he says), the first **trireme** <note>original Latin: *triremis*; Greek: *τριήρης* (*triērēs*). A galley with three banks of oars.</note> was built at **Corinth**. For before that, they used only long ships. The first long ship was the one that carried the **Argonauts** <note>In Greek mythology, the Argonauts were the heroes who accompanied Jason on the ship *Argo* to find the Golden Fleece.</note>, as **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, author of the *Natural History*.</note> and almost all other ancient writers record. Today we call that kind of ship **Galleys**, and that name first took hold while the **Byzantine Empire** <note>original Latin: *imperio Constantinopolitano*.</note> was still flourishing. For in the **Tactics** <note>Referring to the *Tactica* of Emperor Leo VI or similar Byzantine military manuals.</note> we read of "single-banked galleys" <note>original Greek: <term>Γαλαίας μονήρια</term> (*Galaias monēria*).</note>. Our Galleys, and the ships of the ancients, were **moneres** <note>original Greek: <term>μονήρεις</term> (*monēreis*)</note>, which is to say, they were driven by a single row of oars. For just as a tower is called "single-tiered," "double-tiered," or "triple-tiered" <note>original Greek: <term>πύργος μονήρης, διήρης, τριήρης</term></note> based on the number of its floor-levels—which are otherwise called "one-story," "two-story," or "three-story" towers <note>original Greek: <term>πύργος μονόστεγος, δίστεγος, τρίστεγος</term></note>—so too a ship is called *moneres*, *dieres*, or *trieres* because it consisted of just as many decks, so to speak, and consequently as many banks of oars on each side. Since, therefore, the number of banks constitutes the difference between long ships, those having only one row of oars were the first in use. A double distinction is established for them: by the number of oars, and by the number of banks. Those named by the number of oars have names ending in **-oros** <note>original Greek: <term>-ορος</term></note>, such as the **eikosoros** (twenty-oared), **triakontoros** (thirty-oared), **tessarakontoros** (forty-oared), and **pentekontoros** (fifty-oared) <note>original Greek: <term>εἰκόσορος, τριακόντορος, τεσσαρακόντορος, πεντηκόντορος</term></note>. But those named by the number of rows of oars end in **-eres** <note>original Greek: <term>-ηρης</term></note>, such as the *moneres*, *dieres*, *trieres*, *tetreres*, and *penteres*. The ancient author of the **Tactics** <note>original Greek: <term>τακτικῶν</term></note> at the end of **Aelian** <note>Aelianus Tacticus, a 2nd-century Greek military writer.</note> says: "the thirty-oared and forty-*

<summary>This page presents a scholarly analysis by Joseph Scaliger on the origins and naming conventions of ancient Greek and Byzantine ships, distinguishing between those named for their total oar count and those named for their rows of oars.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the scholarly transcription of Joseph Scaliger’s work on ancient maritime history. It picks up mid-sentence from the previous page, detailing the specific Greek nomenclature for vessels based on their oar counts and internal structures. The catchword "runt," at the bottom refers to the completion of the word "fuerunt" (were) on the following page.</meta>

[...thirty]-oared ship <note>original Greek: <term>τριακόντορος</term> (*triakontoros*)</note>, and a fifty-oared ship <note>original Greek: <term>πεντηκόντορος</term> (*pentēkontoros*)</note> is named according to the number of its oars. The single-banked ship <note>original Greek: <term>μονήρης</term> (*monērēs*)</note>, the double-banked ship <note>original Greek: <term>διήρης</term> (*diērēs*)</note>, and so forth, are named according to the rows of oars placed one above the other in height. *But know that, below the twenty-oared ship <note>original Greek: <term>εἰκόσορος</term> (*eikosoros*)</note>, it is not possible to compose a name in this way.* 

*That is to say: a ship was not described by how many oars it had, but by how many thole-pins <note>original Latin: <term>scalmorum</term>. A thole-pin is a wooden peg or pin set in the gunwale of a boat to serve as a fulcrum for an oar.</note> it possessed, if indeed it had fewer than twenty oars.* [Such as] the fifteen-tholed ship <note>original Greek: <term>πεντεκαιδεκάσκαλμος</term> (*pentekaidekaskalmos*)</note>, and the twelve-tholed ship <note>original Greek: <term>δωδεκάσκαλμος</term> (*dōdekaskalmos*)</note>, *such as the* twelve-tholed ferry *of **Strabo** <note>Strabo (c. 64 BC – 24 AD) was a Greek geographer and historian.</note>. For a **scalmos** <note>original Greek: <term>σκάλμος</term></note> is a wooden peg to which an oar is bound.*

*Therefore, with a different understanding—as with many things—**Aeschylus** in his play "The Persians" called ships "three-tholed" <note>original Greek: <term>νῆες τρίσκαλμοι</term> (*nēes triskalmoi*)</note> instead of triremes; whereas a "three-tholed ship" <note>original Greek: <term>τρίσκαλμος ναῦς</term></note> can only truly be said of a vessel driven by a mere three oars.* <note>The author is noting that the poet Aeschylus used "three-tholed" as a poetic figure of speech for a massive trireme, even though technically a "three-tholed" boat would be a tiny skiff with only three oars.</note> 

*There was also another distinction: ships falling short of a certain size were called **hemioliae** <note>original Greek: <term>ἡμιόλιαι</term>; literally "one-and-a-half."</note>. For example, a ship falling between the size of a twenty-oared and a thirty-oared vessel was called a **one-and-a-half thirty-oared ship** <note>original Greek: <term>ἡμιόλιος τριακόντορος</term></note>; it was larger than a twenty-oared ship <note>original Greek: <term>εἰκόσορος</term></note>, but smaller than a thirty-oared one <note>original Greek: <term>τριακόντορος</term></note>. In the same way, a ship falling just short of the size of a trireme <note>A ship with three banks of oars.</note> was called a **one-and-a-half trireme** <note>original Greek: <term>τριήρης ἡμιολία</term></note>. For the "one-and-a-half" <note>original Greek: <term>τὸ ἡμιόλιον</term></note> contains a whole unit plus a half. Thus, the **trihemiolia** <note>original Greek: <term>τριημιολία</term></note> was equal to the size of a bireme <note>A ship with two banks of oars.</note> and somewhat larger besides.*

*Nor indeed was it necessary for exactly a half to be added, any more than in a musical semitone. For a musical tone is not divided perfectly in two. Therefore, just as one says a "one-and-a-half cubit" <note>original Greek: <term>ἡμιόλιος πῆχυς</term></note>, so by the same construction one says a "one-and-a-half trireme," but not for the same mathematical reason. For it ought rather to have been called a "one-and-a-half bireme," since it was of the size of a bireme, or slightly larger. But custom prevailed, so that it was named after the ship size it most nearly approached, rather than the one it not only equaled but exceeded by some portion. These ships—the twenty-oared, thirty-oared, and fifty-oared—were the only ones first in use among the Greeks...*

<summary>This page explains the naming conventions of ancient Greek ships, distinguishing between those named by oar count (like the pentekontoros) and those named by their banks of oars (like the trireme). It also details the "hemiolia" class of ships, which were intermediate sizes named by an "one-and-a-half" designation relative to larger vessels.</summary>
<keywords>naval architecture, trireme, bireme, hemiolia, Scaliger, Aeschylus, Strabo, scalmos, oar banks, Greek maritime history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Joseph Scaliger’s detailed exploration of ancient Greek naval terminology and ship construction. It follows from the previous discussion on how ships were named based on their oar count and the arrangement of their rowers.</meta>

[...they] existed until that time which has been determined here by **Eusebius** <note>Eusebius of Caesarea (c. 260–339 AD), a historian often cited for his chronological tables of ancient history.</note>, and their ships-and-a-half <note>original Greek: <term>ἡμιόλιαι</term> (*hēmioliai*). These were fast, light warships often used by pirates, characterized by having one and a half banks of oars.</note>.

*This type of invention belongs to the **Sidonians**. The Greeks did not attempt this kind of vessel before the Colchian expedition of **Jason**. Hence, ships of this sort were called "Sidonian" <note>original Greek: <term>σιδώνιαι</term> (*sidōniai*)</note> by the Greeks. **Euripides** says in his play "Helen":*

> *Go, and give to these men a Sidonian fifty-oared ship*
> *and masters of the oars.*

*We have said that these are the vessels which the Greeks call "long ships" <note>original Greek: <term>μακρὰς ναῦς</term> (*makras naus*)</note> and we call **Galleys**: such as the ship of **Jason**, which was a fifty-oared vessel <note>original Greek: <term>πεντηκόντορος</term> (*pentēkontoros*)</note>, as is evident from the number of the **Argonauts**. For the Heroes themselves were their own rowers <note>original Greek: <term>αὐτερέται</term> (*auteretai*), meaning the warriors also pulled the oars</note>, as everyone knows. From this, **Hercules** was called "Half-Rower Hercules" <note>original Latin: <term>Semiremex Hercules</term></note> by the poet **Serenus**; because he was indeed a rower among the Argonauts, but he was unskillful and unfit for that duty. Others count a greater number of Argonauts. Therefore, it was necessary for the **Argo** to be larger than a fifty-oared vessel. For this reason, **Theocritus**, in his poem "Hylas," called the Argo "thirty-banked" <note>original Greek: <term>τριακοντάζυγον</term> (*triakontazygon*)</note>; that is, a sixty-oared vessel <note>original Greek: <term>ἑξηκόντορον</term> (*hexēkontoron*)</note>, driven by thirty rowers on each side.*

*Among those ships that were driven by multiple banks of oars, the **Trireme** <note>original Greek: <term>Trieres</term></note> seems to have been the first; it was named, as we have advised, from the number of its decks, "according to the rows placed one above the other in height," as that ancient writer says, "closely packed": with one row leaning upon another according to their height. Thus, the lowest tier was closer to the water, the highest closer to the ship's hatches <note>original Latin: <term>foris</term></note>, and the middle was between the lowest and highest. The rowers of the lowest row were called **thalamitai** <note>literally "those in the hold" (thalamas)</note>, those of the middle **zygitai** <note>literally "those on the cross-beams" (zyga)</note>, and those of the highest **thranitai** <note>literally "those on the benches" (thranos)</note>. These same vessels are also called single-beat, double-beat, or triple-beat <note>original Greek: <term>μονόκροτα</term>, <term>δίκροτα</term>, <term>τρίκροτα</term> (*monokrota*, *dikrota*, *trikrota*)</note>. A **Bireme**, or a two-banked ship <note>original Greek: <term>διήρης</term> (*diērēs*)</note>, or...*

<meta>catchword: double-beat [δικρότȣ]</meta>
