--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues Joseph Scaliger’s critique of the French scholar Lazare de Baïf (Bayfius) regarding the construction and terminology of ancient galleys. It specifically addresses the arrangement of rowers in ships with multiple banks of oars.</meta>

**** 3

*Baïf displays the form of* **biremes** <note>original Greek: <term>διήρεις</term> (*diērēis*), ships with two banks of oars</note> *from ancient marbles, and admits that it is the true bireme of the ancients, in which the upper row is equipped with three rowers and the lower with two. Then he brings forward two other forms of biremes, in both of which each row consists of four oars. He believes it to be, as we have said, the true bireme. But (see his fickle opinion), even when the form of a true* **trireme** <note>a ship with three banks of oars</note> *from ancient Roman monuments is presented, he denies that it is a trireme. Certainly, if the one, as he himself admits, is a bireme because it is equipped with a double row of oars, then the other should be a trireme for the same reason, as it is provided with a triple row of oars. Conversely, if this one is not a trireme, then he was wrong to believe the other was a bireme.*

*Who would think such an absurd opinion could escape such a great man? He offers a ridiculous reason. Because, he says, if the* **thranites**, **zygians**, *and* **thalamites** <note>original Greek: <term>θρανῖται, ζυγῖται, θαλαμῖται</term> (*thranitai, zygitai, thalamitai*). In ancient Greek naval terminology, these refer to the rowers on the highest, middle, and lowest banks respectively.</note> *are named from a triple row of oars, how will they exist in a* **bireme**, *which has only two rows, or in* **quinqueremes, hexaremes,** *or* **septiremes** <note>original Greek: <term>πεντηρήεις, ἑξηρήεις, ἑπτηρήεις</term> (*pentērēis, hexērēis, heptērēis*). These are ships with five, six, or seven files of oars.</note> *which have more? Even a boy could counter this objection. For in a bireme, there are only the lowest and highest rowers, and none in the middle; for it is not necessary for the* **zygians** *to be present. But when there are more than two levels, the middle ones are always* **zygians**, *and the lowest and highest levels are always* **thalamites** *and* **thranites**.*

*If the majority of Romans had a first name, a family name, and an honorary name* <note>original Latin: <term>praenomen, nomen, & agnomen</term></note>, *it does not follow that everyone must have an honorary name. What new thing, then, does he bring to that objection? Something ridiculous and unworthy of such a great man. He divides the ship into three parts: from the prow to the mast; from the mast to the start of the* **stern ornaments** <note>original Greek: <term>ἀκροστόλιον</term> (*akrostolion*)</note>; *and from there to the top of the* **stern decoration** <note>original Latin: <term>aplustre</term>, the curved, fan-like wooden ornament at the rear of an ancient galley</note>. *He calls those rowers from the prow to the mast the* **thranites**. *From here...*

<meta>catchword: to (original Latin: ad)</meta>

<summary>Scaliger critiques Lazare de Baïf’s misunderstanding of ancient ship rowers, arguing that "thranite," "zygian," and "thalamite" refer to the vertical level of the oars regardless of the total number of banks.</summary>
<keywords>bireme, trireme, thranites, zygites, thalamites, Lazare de Baïf, naval architecture, Roman monuments, oar banks, ship terminology</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues Joseph Scaliger’s refutation of Lazare de Baïf regarding the seating of ancient rowers. The previous page ended mid-sentence with the catchword "Thra-", likely referring to the thranites (the highest bank of rowers). This page begins by placing the other classes of rowers—the zygitai and thalamitai—within the ship's structure to prove that banks were stacked vertically rather than arranged horizontally.</meta>

...at the start of the **bow-ornaments** <note>original Greek: <term>ἀκροστολίων</term> (*akrostoliōn*)</note> the **zygians** <note>original Greek: <term>ζευγῖται</term> (*zygitai*), the rowers on the middle tier</note>; from there, at the highest part of the **stern-ornament** <note>original: <term>aplustre</term></note>, the **thalamites** <note>original Greek: <term>θαλαμῖται</term> (*thalamitai*), the rowers on the lowest tier</note>. If I wanted to follow the example of those **boasters** <note>original: <term>Aretalogorum</term>, a term for storytellers or charlatans</note> who publish ten-volume sets instead of brief commentaries, neither time nor paper would suffice to refute such a childish fabrication. 

But setting aside such absurdities, the author of the **Tactics** <note>Likely referring to ancient military manuals by authors such as Aelian or Arrian</note> said: "by rows, **one above the other in height**" <note>original Greek: <term>κατ' στίχους τοὺς κατὰ τὸ ὕψος ἐπ' ἀλλήλοις</term></note>. He did **not** say, "according to length." What could better mock Baïf's fabrication? "**Rows of oars**" <note>original Greek: <term>στίχοι κωπῶν</term></note> is one thing, and "**oars**" <note>original Greek: <term>κῶπαι</term></note> another; likewise "according to height" is different from "according to length." **Vegetius** <note>A 4th-century Roman military theorist</note> says of **quinqueremes** <note>ships with five banks of oars</note>: "They are assigned five **levels** of rowers." If they are "levels," then they must be vertical, not horizontal. **Virgil** <note>From the *Aeneid*, Book V, describing a ship race</note> writes:

> ...the Dardanian youth propel it in a **triple row**,
> the oars rise up in a **triple rank**.

Truly, "rising ranks" of oars cannot be said of length. Length lies flat; height rises. **Lucan** <note>A 1st-century Roman poet</note> said the same of the **quadrireme** <note>a ship with four banks of oars</note> in Book III:

> And those which a fourfold **rising rank** of built-up rowers
> moves, and the oars rise in a triple rank.

We cannot take "built-up ranks of rowers" to mean stretched out horizontally. For that is not what "to build up" means. There is a famous passage in the excerpts of **Memnon** found in **Photius** <note>Photius was a 9th-century scholar who preserved summaries of lost ancient books, including Memnon's history of Heraclea</note>: "**There was one sixteen-banked ship** <note>original Greek: <term>Ἐκκαιδεκήρης</term> (*Ekkaidekerēs*)</note> **called the 'Lion-bearer'** <note>original Greek: <term>λεοντοφόρος</term> (*leontophoros*)</note>, **which was a marvel for its size and beauty. In this ship, one hundred men per row were rowing, so that there were 800 on one side, and 1,600 on both sides.**" An **octeres** <note>an eight-banked ship</note> is so named from eight rows of rowers on each side, with a hundred rowers in a row: so that there were sixteen hundred rowers in total. What would **Baïf** say here? That eight hundred men were stretched out in a single line? If not that, then what? But such was the prodigious mass of those ships. I return to the **triremes**, the standard and most common vessel.

<summary>Scaliger uses classical authorities like Vegetius, Virgil, and Memnon to argue that ancient galleys had rowers stacked vertically in tiers rather than arranged in a single long line. He ridicules Lazare de Baïf’s horizontal seating theory as a "childish fabrication" that ignores the literal meaning of "rising ranks."</summary>

<keywords>naval architecture, trireme, quadrireme, quinquereme, zygitai, thalamitai, Vegetius, Virgil, Lucan, Photius, rowers, ship construction, Joseph Scaliger, Lazare de Baïf</keywords>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the vertical arrangement of rowers on ancient ships. The previous page ended with a discussion of quinqueremes and levels, concluding with the catchword "Thra-" for the thranites, the topmost bank of rowers discussed here.</meta>

Logic convinces us that the oars of the **thranites** <note>original: <term>Thranitarū</term>; the rowers on the highest level of a trireme</note>, that is, of the highest rank, were longer than those of the **zygites** <note>the middle-level rowers</note>, and those of the zygites longer than those of the **thalamites** <note>the rowers on the lowest level, nearest the hull</note>. For the thalamites were closer to the water; hence their oars were **stunted** <note>original Greek: <term>κολοβοὶ</term> (*koloboi*)</note> and not long. **Lucan** <note>A Roman poet (39–65 AD) known for his epic on the Roman Civil War</note>, in his description of the **six-banked ship** <note>original Greek: <term>τῆς ἐξήρ.</term> (*tēs exēr.*)</note>, says: "With the topmost oars he seeks the sea from afar." By this, he understands the **thranite oars** <note>original Greek: <term>θρανιτικάς κώπας</term></note>, which necessarily had to be the longest of all. Consequently, the **thranites** <note>original Greek: <term>οἱ θρανῖται</term></note> received a higher wage than the thalamites.

The **Scholia on *The Frogs*** <note>Ancient explanatory notes on the comedy by Aristophanes</note> state: "The **thalamakes** <note>another term for the lowest rowers</note> received a small wage because they used stunted oars compared to the other (three) ranks of rowers, because they are closer to the water. There were three ranks of rowers: the lowest were the **thalamites**, the middle the **zygites**, and the highest the **thranites**."

Thus far, the account is correct. The remaining parts that follow have been stuffed into those excellent Scholia from the dregs of a later, inferior age: "The **thranite**, then, is the one at the stern; the **zygite** is in the middle; and the **thalamite** is at the bow." <note>The author rejects this later addition because it suggests a horizontal arrangement along the length of the ship rather than vertical stacking.</note> Regarding this distinguished "oracle," **Baïf** <note>Lazare de Baïf (1496–1547), a French humanist whose theories on ancient ships the author is refuting</note> reclined upon his blankets and sought sleep, from which he brought forth that dream of his concerning a ship to be divided lengthwise into three parts. That word (**three**) <note>original Greek: <term>τρεῖς</term></note> included in brackets should also be deleted.

These are the things which we have been able to say briefly, as the place and the constraints of time allowed, concerning **triremes** <note>ships with three banks of oars</note> and other ancient ships rising up in decks. There are two things at which I cannot marvel enough: first, how individual men could manage such long oars, when we see it done differently in our own **galleys** <note>In the 16th century, multiple men often pulled a single large oar, whereas the author notes ancient texts suggest one man per oar.</note>. For in that monstrous **eight-banked ship** <note>original: <term>octere</term></note> mentioned by **Memnon** <note>Memnon of Heraclea, a Greek historian</note>, only single men were assigned to the oars. And yet, the more rows of oars there were, the longer the oars of the highest rank necessarily had to be. The second point, which causes us no small wonder, is the sheer mass of the **six-banked ships** <note>original: <term>Hexerum</term></note>, **ten-banked ships** <note>original: <term>Decerum</term></note>, and as **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, Roman naturalist and author</note> writes, even...

<summary>This page argues that ancient rowers were stacked vertically, with the highest bank (thranites) requiring the longest oars and receiving higher pay. The author refutes Lazare de Baïf's theory of horizontal arrangement and expresses wonder at how single rowers could handle such massive oars on larger vessels.</summary>
<keywords>thranites, zygites, thalamites, naval architecture, trireme, oar length, Lazare de Baïf, Lucan, Memnon, ancient ships</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical argument regarding the vertical arrangement of rowers. *...of those which were constructed with 15, 20, 30, 40, and 50 ranks of oars. How, I ask, could they manage these in war? From where would such long timbers come to suffice for the oars of the **Thranites** <note>original: <term>Thranitarum</term>; the rowers stationed on the highest level of a multi-banked ship</note>? **Virgil** <note>The Roman poet (70–19 BC) who described the Battle of Actium in the *Aeneid*</note> also compares those ships which **Antony** <note>Mark Antony, the Roman general and ally of Cleopatra</note> commanded in the **Battle of Actium** <note>The decisive naval conflict in 31 BC between Antony and Octavian (Augustus)</note> to the **Cyclades** <note>A group of Greek islands; Virgil used this metaphor to emphasize the massive, island-like size of the ships</note>. **Dio** <note>Cassius Dio (c. 155–235 AD), a Roman historian who wrote a massive history of Rome</note> writes the same. If it were true, as **Bayfius** <note>Lazare de Baïf (1496–1547), a French scholar who famously attempted to reconstruct ancient ship designs</note> dreamed, that the oars were spread out horizontally rather than built up in height, then the greater the number of oars, the more maneuverable a **Trireme** <note>a ship with three banks of oars</note> would have been than a **Bireme** <note>two banks</note>, a **Quadrireme** <note>original: <term>Tetreres</term>; four banks</note> than a Trireme, and a **Quinquereme** <note>original: <term>Penteres</term>; five banks</note> than a Quadrireme; yet the opposite appears from **Florus** <note>A Roman historian of the 2nd century AD</note>, Book 4, concerning those "Cyclades" of Antony and **Augustus** <note>The first Roman Emperor, Antony’s rival</note> in the Actian fleet:*

*“We had no more than four hundred ships, and the enemy no fewer than two hundred; but their size made up for their number. For they were raised from six to nine ranks of oars, and additionally heightened with towers and decks, so that they moved like castles and cities—not without the groaning of the sea and the labor of the winds—which very **bulk** <note>original: <term>moles</term></note> was their destruction. Caesar's ships <note>referring to the fleet of Octavian/Augustus</note> had increased from triremes up to no more than six ranks. Therefore, being agile for everything that use required—for making attacks, retreats, and taking turns—they attacked those heavy ships, which were hindered in every way, several ships against one, and destroyed them at will with missiles, rams, and fires.”*

*I have transferred here from **Bayfius** the images of two **Biremes** and one **Trireme** mentioned here, which are entirely accurate likenesses, so that from them one might more easily grasp in the mind the **bulk** <note>original: <term>molem</term></note> and height of ships rising with many ranks, and [think/ponder...]* <note>The text ends mid-word on "cogi-"; the metadata catchword "tatione" completes the word as "cogitatione," meaning "consideration" or "thought."</note>

<meta>The text continues from the previous page, likely concluding the word "cogitatione" (by reflection or thought) from the catchword on the preceding leaf.</meta>

...you might attain by reflection. There is one thing that Scaliger <note>likely Joseph Justus Scaliger, a renowned 16th-century polymath and classical scholar</note> wonders at—how individual men could wield such long oars—which you may learn from Athenaeus, in his description of the ship built by Ptolemy Philopator <note>Ptolemy IV Philopator, Pharaoh of Egypt, known for constructing a massive "forty-banked" ship</note> with forty banks of oars. <note>original Greek: "κώπας δὲ θρανικὰς ὀκτὼ καὶ τριάκοντα πηχῶν τὰς μεγίστας, αἳ διὰ τὸ μόλυβδον ἔχειν ἐν τοῖς ἐγχειριδίοις καὶ γεγονέναι λίαν εἴσω βαρεῖαι κατὰ τὴν ζύγωσιν, εὐήρεις ὑπῆρχον ἐπὶ τῆς χρείας."</note> The longest oars in the uppermost rank of the <term>thranite<gloss>the highest level of rowers in a multi-level ancient ship</gloss></term> were thirty-eight <term>cubits<gloss>a unit of length based on the forearm, roughly 18 to 21 inches</gloss></term> long; these were easy to handle in practice because they had lead in the handles and were made very heavy on the inside near the rowlocks. Therefore, this weight served to make the use of the oars agile, just as in a balance scale where the <term>fulcrum<gloss>the pivot point of a lever; the original text uses the Greek-derived "hypomochlium"</gloss></term> is at the <term>rowlock<gloss>the support for the oar, called a "scalmus"</gloss></term>. Nor yet would I deny that it seems not absurd to me that two rowers were assigned to these longest oars. However, one can learn from the history of the Argonauts that individuals certainly pulled their own oars in a "long ship."

> Each man gives his strength to the oar, and gives his name to the rowing benches.
> From here, Telamon holds the sea to the left; from the higher side, 
> Alcides <note>The Greek name for Hercules</note> occupies the other sea; the rest of the youth
> Are divided.

But in a mass of such size and built with so many banks, I do not sufficiently understand how the fortieth bank did not rise much higher: so that the highest <term>thranite</term> with an oar of thirty-eight cubits, of which no small part was handled inside the ship, could strike the sea with their blades. To make this clearer, having set the <term>abacus<gloss>a counting board or table used for performing calculations</gloss></term>, I will perform a calculation according to my opinion. The height of a sitting man from the <term>sacrum<gloss>the bone at the base of the spine</gloss></term> to the crown of the head...

<summary>The author examines the mechanics of ancient "super-ships," specifically how rowers on Ptolemy Philopator's forty-banked vessel could handle 38-cubit oars using lead counterweights, while questioning the practical height of such a massive structure.</summary>
<keywords>Ptolemy Philopator, naval architecture, thranite, oars, ancient engineering, Athenaeus, Scaliger, Argonauts, mechanics, leverage</keywords>

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>The text continues the discussion of Ptolemy Philopator's massive ship, specifically calculating the physical space required for forty banks of oars. The catchword "nati" from the previous page likely completed the word "cogitatione" or "ratiocinati," indicating a shift to mathematical reasoning.</meta>

If you take a moderate estimate, a man's height while seated is no less than three Roman feet <note>A Roman foot was slightly shorter than a modern foot, roughly 11.6 inches or 29.6 cm.</note>; but to rise up and pull the oar with all your strength, this cannot happen if you sit on the floor with your legs stretched out; rather, some height of a seat is necessary. Therefore, let one foot be added to the established height, making four feet. By at least such an interval, the vertical distance between the rowing benches ought to be separated. 

Certainly, if you take four feet forty times, they amount to one hundred and sixty feet—an interval by which the <term>thalamite<gloss>the lowest tier of rowers in a multi-level galley, positioned closest to the waterline</gloss></term> rowers at the bottom would be separated from the <term>thranite<gloss>the highest tier of rowers in an ancient galley</gloss></term> who rowed at the very top. And therefore the total height, reaching from the hatches and the highest sides of the ship down to the sea, would be far greater still. 

Yet it is established that this ship, from the highest part of the stern to the sea, was only fifty-three <term>cubits<gloss>an ancient unit of length, approximately 18-21 inches</gloss></term> in height, and much less along the sides. Athenaeus himself reported a height of forty-eight <note>original: "octo & quadraginta"</note> from the <term>acrostolion<gloss>the decorative ornament at the prow or stern of a galley</gloss></term>; which amounts to only seventy-two feet. Thus, the ship would by no means have had even half the height that we calculated above <note>The author is pointing out a logical contradiction: 40 vertical levels of men would require 160 feet, but the ship was only about 72-80 feet high</note>. 

The *thalamite* rowers, being certainly closer to the water, used shorter oars, and therefore the inner part of the oar from the <term>rowlock<gloss>the pivot point on the side of the ship, called a "scalmus"</gloss></term> to the handle was smaller; and they themselves on the rowing benches were quite close to the sides of the ship. The higher each rank rose, the longer the oars needed to be, requiring a longer radius and a greater distance from the rowlocks to move them. Consequently, by this staggering or recession of the ranks, some space was provided for the lower rowers, so that the top of the head of a lower rower could, without hindrance, reach the height of the chest of the rank immediately above. This was especially the case when the oars were arranged in alternating ranks...

<summary>The author uses mathematical calculations to argue that the forty banks of oars on Ptolemy's ship could not have been stacked vertically, as the ship's recorded height was far too low to accommodate them.</summary>
<keywords>naval architecture, Ptolemy Philopator, thranite, thalamite, ancient ships, Athenaeus, Roman feet, cubits, rowing mechanics</keywords>

<meta>The text continues the technical analysis of the internal arrangement of rowers within Ptolemy Philopator’s massive galley. It follows the previous page's discussion on the vertical height and seat requirements, now shifting to the horizontal spacing of the oar-ports and the staggering of the rowers to prevent mechanical interference.</meta>

[...reasoned], so that they were not all extended in a straight line through their <term>oar-ports<gloss>holes in the side of a ship's hull through which oars are passed; literally 'pigeon-holes'</gloss></term>, as if hanging from a plumb line, which would cause them to be a mutual hindrance to one another. Instead, they were arranged alternately, so that they might be set into their positions in this manner as if into an empty part of the sea. It seems quite plausible that this was accurately observed in ships of many ranks, to the point where a higher and longer oar would no longer be an impediment to a lower and shorter one after the third or fourth rank. 

And indeed, the ship itself, spurred on by such great rowing power and by so many "naval feet," <note>The author uses "naval feet" to poetically describe the collective movement of the oarsmen acting as the ship's means of propulsion.</note> would run through the sea. However, one point seems to me to be the "palm of victory" <note>An idiom for the most important or decisive point of an argument.</note> in the solution of this puzzle: exactly how much horizontal interval there was between the rowers of the same rank. For once that matter is understood, it will clear away all the fog of uncertainty. 

I believe I have observed that the rowing benches were not separated by the same interval in all vessels; rather, in some places they were further apart, and in others closer together. I shall demonstrate this by the evidence of two ships. <term>Philopator's forty-banked ship<gloss>The "tessarakonteres," a giant galley built for Ptolemy IV Philopator of Egypt around 200 BCE, famously described by the historian Callixenus</gloss></term> was driven by four thousand rowers. This number, divided by the forty ranks, gives one hundred rowers per rank; and therefore, on each individual side, fifty rowers in a line. 

Now, this vessel was 280 <term>cubits<gloss>an ancient unit of length based on the forearm, roughly 18 to 21 inches</gloss></term> long, which amounts to 420 feet. From this length, let 60 feet be deducted on account of the curvature of the hull and the <term>keel<gloss>the central structural beam running along the bottom of the ship</gloss></term> at the prow and the stern. The remaining 360 feet, divided by the number of 40 rowers, <note>The author calculates using 40 rowers despite having estimated 50 per side previously, perhaps to account for the actual usable rowing space along the straight portion of the hull.</note> will give to each man a space of nine feet for working the handle of the oar. Since the movement of the body and arms in pulling the oars requires an <term>interscalmium<gloss>the horizontal distance between two thole-pins (oar-locks)</gloss></term> of only four or five feet, this intermediate space allows the rank—

<meta>The text continues the geometric analysis of Ptolemy’s massive vessel, specifically focusing on the vertical distance between the lowest and highest tiers of rowers. approaches the next lower gain. And so it continues in the following ranks, by fitting equal intervals in alternating turns, as shown above in the diagram of this trireme. But what exceeds all belief, and what I myself cannot wonder at enough, is this: it is certain that the <term>thalamites<gloss>the lowest tier of rowers in a galley, situated in the hold or "thalamus"</gloss></term> were separated from the <term>thranites<gloss>the highest tier of rowers, who sat on the "thranos" or top benches</gloss></term>—that is, the lowest rowers from the highest—in this <term>forty-banked ship<gloss>the "tessarakonteres," a massive galley built for Ptolemy IV Philopator of Egypt around 200 BCE</gloss></term> by a vertical height of less than forty feet. 

You shall learn this from the stated length of the oars. For let us conceive of a right-angled isosceles triangle, whose hypotenuse (the side opposite the right angle) is the length of the extended oar itself, and the vertical leg is the height from the <term>thole-pin<gloss>the wooden pin or oar-lock (scalmus) on the ship's side that acts as a fulcrum</gloss></term> to the sea. It is well established that if the oar is worked at too small an angle against the ship's side, the rowing would be too difficult and the oar less manageable. In this triangle, therefore, the square of one leg is half the square of the hypotenuse. 

Now, the total length of the oar is given as thirty-eight <term>cubits<gloss>an ancient unit of length based on the forearm; the author calculates this as 1.5 feet per cubit</gloss></term>, which equals fifty-seven feet. But the part of the oar from the thole-pin to the <term>handle<gloss>original: "manubrium"</gloss></term> must have been of some length so it could be moved comfortably, and it must have held a reasonable proportion to the remaining part extended outside the ship. Let us assume that inner part was only seven feet, to be as conservative as possible in our estimate. The remainder of the oar from the thole-pin to the sea would then be fifty feet. The square of fifty is 2,500; half of that is 1,250. The <term>root of this half<gloss>the side of the square</gloss></term> is less than thirty-six feet. 

Consequently, the individual ranks, including the thickness of the <term>bench<gloss>original: "transtri," the cross-beams or rowing seats</gloss></term> itself, would have been separated by an interval of barely one foot. It would be a sin to believe that these benches could have been occupied by rowers, unless perhaps you imagine them packed together like <note>The author uses a vivid culinary metaphor here: "haleces & salsamenta," referring to salted herrings or preserved fish tightly packed in a barrel.</note> herrings and salt-fish. Now, as for the remainder...

<meta>This page continues the technical and historical debate regarding the structural feasibility of massive ancient galleys, specifically comparing the famous forty-banked ship (tessarakonteres) of Ptolemy IV with the eight-banked ship (octeris) of Ptolemy Ceraunus.</meta>

...that the space above the <term>thranites<gloss>the highest tier of rowers in a galley</gloss></term>, extending to the openings of the ship, was divided and assigned for dining rooms, bed-chambers, and other uses, will not be doubted by anyone who applies their mind to examining the words of <term>Athenaeus<gloss>A Greek author (c. 200 CE) whose work "The Deipnosophists" provides the most detailed surviving description of the "forty-banked" ship</gloss></term> with a bit more care. Nor can we, by any reasoning, extricate ourselves from these difficulties and constraints into which the measurement of the oars has cast us, unless we concede this: that the lowest ranks were driven by fewer oars, and the highest by more; and furthermore, that we allow some space for the recession of the upper rowers, who would work their oars with longer handles. By this path, I see the result: that these massive structures were certainly established only for show, and were of no practical use. This is indeed most true. 

But in the excerpts of <term>Memnon<gloss>An ancient historian of Heraclea Pontica</gloss></term> found in <term>Photius<gloss>A 9th-century scholar and Patriarch of Constantinople who preserved summaries of many lost ancient books</gloss></term>, the <term>eight-banked ship<gloss>the "octeris," a large warship with eight rowers per vertical section</gloss></term> of Ptolemy Ceraunus <note>King of Macedon from 281 to 279 BCE</note> has a simpler explanation, even though the number of rowers is much larger—namely, one hundred per row <note>original: "centum nempe in versum"</note>. This number of rowers was two and a half times <note>original: "duplus sesquialter", a ratio of 5:2</note> that of the other. Therefore, the vertical distance between the rowing benches would have been a bit greater; for here, because of the multitude of rowers, the lower ones could be placed entirely beneath the upper ones. 

If, in this eight-banked ship, the distance between the benches in each rank and within the same row had been as great as it was in that forty-banked ship, the length of this eight-banked ship would easily have equaled 900 feet, and would have been more than double the length of the other; a thing which I believe could not occur to the mind of any sane person. For this reason, just as the length of this ship was indeed smaller than that of the forty-ranked vessel, so the distance between the lowest <term>thalamites<gloss>the rowers in the lowest, or "inner chamber" (thalamus) of the ship</gloss></term> and the highest thranites would not have been much less than forty feet, so that between each rank there were five...

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the evolution of seafaring, moving from the initial fear of the open ocean to the establishment of formal seasons and laws for safe navigation.</meta>

This is a custom that even today the inhabitants of the Mediterranean coast uphold: when a storm approaches, they retreat toward the shore. However, it is far better at such times to commit the ship to the high seas, where there is no back-and-forth churning of the waves—the very thing that causes ships to be battered and broken apart. 

Once people’s minds were hardened against every danger and they began to spend the night on the high seas, resting upon the ship’s decks and rowing benches, the opportune times for navigation were noted and defined by laws. This was done so that everyone would know definitely in which season of the year ships should be hauled ashore for safety, and when they should be launched back into the sea. 

Thus, in the <term>Acts of the Apostles<gloss>The fifth book of the New Testament, detailing the travels of the Apostles</gloss></term>, chapter 27, you see that the time for navigation was considered dangerous and unsafe: <note>original Greek: "ὄντος ἤδη ἐπισφαλοῦς τοῦ πλοὸς, διὰ τὸ καὶ τὴν νηστείαν ἤδη παρεληλυθέναι"</note> "since navigation was already full of danger," it says, "because the time of the fast had already passed." <note>The author specifies this refers to the Jewish Fast of Yom Kippur, occurring on the tenth day of the seventh month, which traditionally marked the beginning of the dangerous winter sailing season in the Mediterranean.</note> 

The <term>poet<gloss>Virgil, the Roman epic poet</gloss></term> had this same season of the year in mind when <term>Dido<gloss>The legendary Queen of Carthage</gloss></term> rebukes <term>Aeneas<gloss>The Trojan hero</gloss></term> as he prepares his flight:

> Why, do you even labor at your fleet under a winter star,
> And hasten to go through the deep in the midst of north winds,
> Cruel man? What? If you were not seeking foreign fields and
> Unknown homes, and if ancient Troy still stood,
> Would even Troy be sought by your fleets across such a wavy sea?
<note>original Latin: "Quin etiam hyberno moliris sidere classem..." from Virgil's Aeneid, Book 4.</note>

For from the third day of the <term>Ides of November<gloss>November 11th</gloss></term> until the sixth day of the <term>Ides of March<gloss>March 10th</gloss></term>, the seas were officially closed to travel. As <term>Pliny<gloss>Pliny the Elder, Roman author and naturalist</gloss></term> also wrote, *"Spring opens the seas to those who sail,"* at the beginning of which...

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from seasonal sailing to the development of celestial navigation, focusing on how early mariners divided the world and used the stars to guide their voyages beyond the Mediterranean.</meta>

The **West Winds** <note>original Latin: *Favonij*, the gentle winds of spring</note> soften the sky. And then, from that time forward, the world was also divided more accurately into its regions. For just as **surveyors** <gloss>land-measurers using a ten-foot rod, or *decempeda*</gloss> on land mark out the meridian and their central axis to divide fields with the rigor of iron, so those wretched **sea-wanderers** <note>original Greek: ἁλίπλαγκτοι (*haliplanktoi*)</note>, tossed by the surging waves, began to seek signs so they might be certain of which shores they were being carried toward and by which blast of wind. In this way, they could more successfully map the location of maritime regions. For this purpose, the heavens themselves were called into service, so that they might steer their course by the sight of the stars.

---

->— Happy is he who gave use to the slow stars,<-
->And granted the power to hold sea-courses with the sky as guide.<-

The greatest glory in this belonged to the **Phoenicians**, who surpassed the other inhabitants of the Mediterranean coast in their devotion to trade and the exporting of goods; likewise, they excelled in the glory of celestial science. By day, they followed the Sun and distinguished the regions of the South and North without difficulty. At night, however, they depended entirely on **Cynosura** <gloss>The "Dog’s Tail," known today as the Little Bear or Ursa Minor</gloss>, because it was closest to the celestial pole. The Greeks, on the other hand, content with a broader view, looked to **Helice** <gloss>The "Twister," known today as the Great Bear or Ursa Major</gloss>. This distinction is frequently emphasized by the ancient writers:

> There are two Bears: the Sidonians seek **Cynosura**,
> While the Greek keel marks **Helice**.

Relying on this confidence, the **Tyrians** and **Sidonians** <gloss>Inhabitants of the Phoenician city-states of Tyre and Sidon</gloss> dared not only to frequent all the shores of the Mediterranean and visit its furthest reaches, but even to establish a colony outside its limits in the Atlantic Ocean at **Gades** <gloss>Modern-day Cádiz, Spain</gloss>. Using the labor of these men, King **Solomon**, the wisest of humans, also sought gold from far away in... <meta>The page ends with the catchword "Ophiro," referring to the biblical land of Ophir.</meta>

<summary>This page describes the birth of celestial navigation, comparing the precise star-charting of the Phoenicians with the broader methods of the Greeks. It highlights how these skills allowed ancient sailors to venture beyond the Mediterranean to places like Cadiz and Ophir.</summary>
<keywords>celestial navigation, Phoenicians, Cynosura, Helice, Ursa Minor, Ursa Major, King Solomon, Gades, maritime history, surveying</keywords>

He resolved to seek **Ophir** <note>A biblical land, likely in East Africa or India, famous for its wealth of gold and sandalwood</note> by way of the **Ethiopian Sea** <note>The ancient name for the southern Atlantic and Indian Oceans</note>, and joined his fleet with the fleet of **Hiram, King of the Tyrians**. And this indeed occurred so long before the island of **Taprobane** <gloss>The ancient name for Sri Lanka</gloss> was even heard of by those in the Western regions through report; many centuries later, the freedman of **Annius Plocamus**, having been driven there by a storm, became a source of wonder to those people <note>Pliny the Elder records that during the reign of Claudius, a tax collector's freedman was blown off course to Sri Lanka, leading to the first formal Roman embassy from the island</note>. Therefore, this careful observation of the seasons lasted for many centuries, so that, religiously avoiding the cruelty of storms, they would sail only during those favorable months. 

But **pirates** first compelled men, through the danger of death, to rush into death itself and brave the wintry seas. Now, greed compels the same. Nor is this the case only in the **Mediterranean Sea**, whose distances can be crossed in a very short time. It has reached such a point that **Galerius** reached **Alexandria** from the **Strait of Sicily** on the seventh day, and **Bibulus** on the sixth—both of them being prefects. Indeed, in the following summer, **Valerius Marianus**, one of the senators of praetorian rank, reached his destination from **Puteoli** <note>A major Roman port near modern Naples</note> on the ninth day with the gentlest breeze, as **Pliny** says. The same author records that one can reach **Ostia** <note>The primary port of Rome</note> from **Gades** <note>Modern Cádiz, Spain</note> beyond the **Pillars of Hercules** <note>The Strait of Gibraltar</note> in seven days, from Hither Spain in four, from the Narbonnese Province in three, and from Africa in two.

*When, I say, men wander blind upon the waves, nor can they any longer remember their way in that vast abyss; but are tossed now here, now there, from their intended course by the force of the waves. Here, therefore, the sight of neither the sky nor the stars helps those wretched men in the slightest. There is certainly need of a far different guide <note>original: ξενάγωγω (*xenagogos*), a guide or conductor</note>, one which must be sought by them not from the heavens, but which should be present before their eyes and in their hands, always and as often as they please; from which, if nothing else, they may at least know toward which region of the world the ship directs its course: and although they may be driven astray from the straight path by the power of the waves, or the crash and fury of raging winds, it may nevertheless be immediately clear to them in which direction the stern has turned, and with what force it is being driven. Nor indeed were these things to be investigated with great labor, or sought further outside this globe. Rather, we only had to approach Mother Earth herself and make our vows to her; she, pregnant everywhere with the most noble magnet, brings it forth of her own accord from her bowels into the light, so that by its aid, as if by a praetorian edict <note>A formal order issued by a Roman magistrate; here the author suggests that the magnet gives humanity a legal or divine right to occupy the sea</note>, the human race might also be granted possession of the Sea itself. It attracts iron rings, and even imparts a power to the rings so that they are able to do this same thing which the stone does: to attract other rings. Thus, sometimes a very long chain of iron pieces and rings is hung from one another; for all of these, the power is suspended from that stone. <note>The author provides a Greek quotation—likely from Plato's *Ion*—describing magnetic induction, where the magnet's power flows through a series of objects.</note>

*Just as, he says, it happens in that stone which Euripides named the Magnet, and most people call the Herculean stone.* <note>The "Herculean stone" was a common ancient name for the loadstone, possibly referring to its "strength" or its discovery in Heraclea.</note> *For this stone not only attracts iron rings, but a very long series of iron rings may be joined together in a continuous weave: to all of which that power belongs entirely from that stone. Knowing this indeed is of little importance to us; but to have understood its cause, that at last would be to have unlocked the fountains of nature, upon which both the majesty and the practical use of maritime affairs entirely depend. And although that natural efficiency of the magnet in attracting iron has already been noted and observed for so many centuries by industrious men, yet to no one of them—not even in a dream <note>original: ἔτι κατ’ ὄναρ (*eti kat' onar*)</note>—did it occur that a far more noble cause of this efficiency lay beneath. He must have been a most sagacious initiate <term>initiate (mystem): one initiated into the sacred mysteries</term> of nature, by whose labor and industry that [secret] was first unearthed and investigated from the more secret shrines <term>shrines (adytis): the innermost sanctum of a temple</term> of nature. <meta>This page continues the discussion on the origins of the magnetic compass. The previous page ended with a mention of Campania (specifically the city of Amalfi), where tradition claims the mariner’s compass was perfected. The author now notes that while the specific inventor's name is lost to time, the glory belongs to the nation, comparing this to the ancient Phoenicians.</meta>

*...it is circulated under no specific title; whereas the inventors of even the simplest and most worthless things have their own titles and their own names. In a matter so illustrious as this, however, we must be content with only the name of the nation. And just as once the Phoenicians* <note>The Phoenicians were the premier mariners of the ancient Mediterranean, credited by the Greeks with inventing celestial navigation.</note> *were known as the inventors of many things—the heavens, the stars, navigation, and many other arts—where the glory is ascribed not to one man, but to the entire people. The same thing occurs here, so that it is known to us not who the author and inventor of such a divine thing was, but only from which nation he emerged. Scarcely four hundred years have passed since such a memorable invention, so suitable for maritime journeys, was unearthed and brought to light. For a needle enclosed in a small box* <note>original: "pyxidula" — the author uses this diminutive term for the early compass housing.</note> *constantly turns itself toward the same region by its own command and guidance, so that there is scarcely any need for observations of the sun or stars. Nay, indeed, the regions of the world are investigated much more accurately in this way, while they repeatedly look at only the little box which is before their feet and accurately observe its guidance.*

<note>This poetic interlude references the traditional celestial markers—Bootes (the Herdsman), Helice (the Great Bear), and Orion—which were the primary guides for sailors before the compass made it possible to navigate without a clear sky.</note>

*For with confidence in this instrument, the Italians first, and then even the Spanish, began to tempt the external seas and to visit more frequently the Canary Islands, located in the Ocean not far from the African shore. ...and other smaller parts of the circumference. How far the practice has progressed since it first began at that time, I could not easily say. This much, indeed, has been handed down to memory: that a certain Sea-Captain <note>original: "Navarchum"</note> (whose name is no better known than that of the man who first taught how to transfer the magnetic turning-force into the nautical needle) was the first of mortals to penetrate beyond Madeira to the opposite shore of America. Whether he did so by deliberate study or by chance, with the fates leading him, he is said to have possessed a rough, sketched-out knowledge of this matter. 

For this man—trained from his earliest youth in various and (as the times then allowed) long-distance voyages—was carried to America by a favorable wind. From there, having reversed his course, he landed his ship at Madeira, though several of his crew had been lost. He himself returned, but was so exhausted by the long journey and broken by his labors that he died at the home of Christopher Columbus, a Ligurian <note>Columbus was from Genoa, in the region of Liguria, Italy.</note> by birth, who at that time was making a living there by drawing hydrographic charts <note>Maps used by mariners for navigation at sea.</note>. The outlines of the route from the compass-box <note>original: "pyxide"</note> and the latitude of the place were indicated to Columbus himself. 

These things later served as an incentive to Columbus, so that he urged the undertaking of that expedition with such great zeal and with all his might <note>original: "manibus pedibusque obnixe" — a Latin idiom meaning "with hands and feet" or "striving with every effort."</note>. He left no stone unturned, appealing to his own Genoese people, to Henry VII of England, to Alfonso V of Portugal, and to Ferdinand and Isabella, the monarchs of Castile. At last, remaining most tenacious in his purpose, he obtained his wish from the monarchs of Castile. He was the first to discover those lands so long unknown to our world, and the immense riches taken from those wretched and most peaceful mortals into—

...the sea is at its widest) is three thousand two hundred [stadia]. From there, he explains the method of measurement:

<note>The following passage is translated from the author's inclusion of both the original Greek of Herodotus (Histories, 4.86) and his own Latin translation.</note>

*“These things,” he says, “were measured in this way. In the course of a long day, a ship generally covers at most seventy thousand orgyae <note>An *orgya* is an ancient Greek unit of length, roughly six feet, equivalent to the span of a man's outstretched arms or a modern fathom.</note>, and sixty thousand by night. Now, from the mouth of the sea to Phasis <note>The Phasis River (modern Rioni in Georgia) was considered the easternmost point of the Black Sea.</note> (for this is the longest part of the Pontus <note>The *Pontus Euxinus* is the ancient name for the Black Sea.</note>), it is a voyage of nine days and eight nights; these make one million, one hundred and ten thousand orgyae, which is eleven thousand one hundred stadia <note>A *stadium* was a standard Greek unit of distance, approximately 600 feet. The author is converting the travel time into a physical distance.</note>. From the land of the Sindicæ to Themiscyra on the Thermodon river <note>A region on the southern coast of the Black Sea, legendary home of the Amazons.</note> (for this is the widest part of the Pontus), it is a voyage of three days and two nights; these make three hundred and thirty thousand orgyae, or three thousand three hundred stadia. In this manner, therefore, the Pontus, the Bosphorus, and the Hellespont <note>The Dardanelles.</note> were measured by me.”*

See how this most accurate and trustworthy writer <note>The author refers to Herodotus, often called the "Father of History."</note> pledges his own word; he does not rely on the testimony of another. You see that he expands the distance covered during the day and contracts it at night: which you will easily understand if you consider that the forty-fifth parallel extends through the middle of the Pontus. Moreover, the span of the longest day is...

<meta>This page continues the author's analysis of ancient maritime measurements, specifically critiquing the accuracy of distance estimations made before the invention of the magnetic compass. of fifteen and a half hours, and of the shortest night, eight and a half hours. And although ships are driven sometimes more violently and sometimes more slowly according to the varying force of the winds, we see that Herodotus nevertheless followed a certain middle ground <note>Or "average speed."</note> in his estimation of these intervals. By a not dissimilar method, the distance between Rhodes and Alexandria was estimated at five thousand stadia, and that between Carthage and Lilybaeum <note>Modern-day Marsala, Sicily.</note> is, according to Strabo, <unclear>now</unclear> one thousand five hundred stadia, though according to Polybius it is only a thousand. There is no doubt these were estimated in such a way; there are infinite other examples of this kind among historians and geographers, yet experience proves that all of them were defined by those authors with too much uncertainty and contrary to the truth. Certainly, as long as the direction of the nautical compass <note>original: *pyxidis nauticæ*</note> was unknown to them, the method of guessing correctly eluded them all. For the compass was truly the thread of Ariadne <note>In Greek mythology, Ariadne gave Theseus a ball of thread to help him find his way out of the Labyrinth; the author uses this as a metaphor for the compass guiding sailors through the "maze" of the open sea.</note>, by which alone both the courses and the measurements of those courses are guided.

<margin>If the <term>bematistae</term> <note>original Greek: *βηματιϛαὶ*. These were "step-measurers" or professional surveyors in ancient Greece who measured distances by counting their paces.</note>,</margin> the measurers of the journey of Alexander the Great, namely Diognetus and Beton, had followed that method; and if afterwards the Roman <term>decempedatores</term> <note>Literally "ten-foot-pole men," these were Roman land surveyors who used a standard ten-foot measuring rod called a *decempeda*.</note>—Zenodoxus, Theodotus, and Polyclitus—who were sent by the authority of Julius Caesar to measure the entire world subject to the Roman Empire, had done the same, a great light would have been added to ancient geography by their labor. But those are only the wishes of those who hope for better things. --- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of ancient and early modern navigational charts. Having previously discussed the lack of a magnetic compass in antiquity, the author now addresses the geometric errors inherent in "plane charts" and the flaws in the data provided by ancient land-surveyors (bematistae), while introducing the concept of the rhumb line (loxodrome).</meta>

******* 2

[...they did not operate] conveniently, as long as they designed hydrographic charts <note>original: *tabulas hydrographicas*</note> on a plane in such a way that they established all parallels of latitude to be equal to one another. For in that case, it was necessary to make the parts of the smaller parallels equal to the corresponding parts of the larger ones. Consequently, when they sailed along a specific parallel, they assigned as much distance to each degree of longitude as is granted to a single degree on the great circle <note>The "great circle" here refers to the Equator; on a globe, degrees of longitude become physically shorter as one moves toward the poles, but early plane charts incorrectly treated them as uniform.</note>. 

While this error is intolerable, I am not certain whether Ptolemy himself, in his *Geography*, did not likewise stumble against the principles of geometry, since he derived the side opposite a right angle <note>The hypotenuse of a triangle.</note> from the squares of the other two sides on a globe just as one would on a flat plane. I pass over his most foul delusions in estimating the length of those sides. However, that error should not be attributed to him, but rather to the unskillful <term>bematistae</term> <note>original Greek: *Βηματίσταις*; professional "step-measurers" or surveyors in antiquity who calculated distances by counting their paces during travels.</note> of journeys, from whom he derived and transcribed his data, out of a grateful memory for posterity. 

Indeed, those shipmasters <note>original: *navarchæ*</note>, as long as they remained within the thirty-sixth parallel, deviated by barely a fifth of a degree from the true measurement; and the closer they approached the equinoctial line <note>The Equator.</note>, the less error was found in their conjectures. And it was here that they began to express the angles of <term>rhumb lines</term> <note>original: *loxodromiarum*; a path that crosses all meridians of longitude at the same angle, appearing as a straight line on a Mercator projection but a spiral on a globe.</note> through the calculation of right-angled triangles. In this, they were so far correct: that the segments of any rhumb line between equidistant parallels are equal. And conversely, that an equal distance between parallel circles intercepts equal segments of similar rhumb lines everywhere. These things, I say, although truly and legitimately noted by them—and by the hydrographers practicing that art, [derived] from the doctrine of plane triangles no [less than...] <note>The sentence is interrupted by the page break; the catchword "mis" suggests the next word is "minus" (less).</note>

<summary>The author critiques the geometric inaccuracy of treating the Earth as a plane in early navigation and notes that even Ptolemy relied on flawed data from ancient surveyors (bematistae). He introduces the mathematical properties of rhumb lines (loxodromes) as a more accurate way to calculate naval courses.</summary>

<keywords>hydrographic charts, plane charts, Ptolemy, bematistae, navigation errors, rhumb lines, loxodromes, maritime geometry</keywords>

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s historical account of navigation, moving from the errors of ancient geography to the 16th-century innovations of Pedro Nunes (Petrus Nonius). It specifically addresses the transition from "plane charts" to spherical geometry and the introduction of the loxodrome or rhumb line.</meta>

...[the measurements] had been accurately collected into certain tables made for this purpose, which Nonius later "brought back to the anvil" <note>An idiom meaning to refine, rework, or polish a scientific or literary work.</note>. However, these could not be legitimately demonstrated in this way, because they were built upon those flat and rectangular geographic charts. Thus, as the saying goes, "fortune stripped art bare" <note>original Greek: *ἡ τύχη τὴν τέχνην ἐγύμνησε*; a proverb suggesting that chance or circumstance revealed the limitations of the existing craft.</note>. On this slight occasion, as if by some spark of a nascent art around the year 1530, that most accurate man, <term>Petrus Nonius</term> <note>Pedro Nunes (1502–1578), a Portuguese mathematician and cosmographer who was the first to mathematically define the loxodrome.</note>, was stirred to seek this same doctrine from a somewhat higher source; and he appealed from <term>pseudography</term> <note>False or erroneous mapping; here referring to the distortions of flat charts.</note> to the Globe itself.

Through his great industry and the dexterity of intellect in which he excelled, he brought many things to light; and with many additions, he enriched the discoveries of those who came before him. Nor did he shrink from explaining in a specific book the properties of those lines which the courses of ships describe according to the guidance <note>original Greek: *ξεναγωγίαν* (xenagogian), meaning "guidance" or "conducting."</note> of the magnetic needle. After several years had passed, he described these same matters much more extensively in a second book, *On Rules and Instruments* <note>original Latin: *De Regulis & Instrumentis* (1537).</note>, and pursued the properties of these lines more accurately. 

Even if in these matters he not infrequently "missed the mark" <note>original Greek: *ἠστόχησε* (estochese).</note> and was engaged less successfully, casting himself into great difficulties, his chief error <note>original Latin: *Palmarium illud*</note> was that he cut up our <term>Loxodrome</term> <note>From the Greek *loxos* (oblique) and *dromos* (course); a line on a sphere that crosses all meridians at the same angle. On a flat Mercator map, it appears as a straight line, but on a globe, it is a spiral.</note>—which he, in the language of his own nation, calls a <term>Rhumb</term> <note>original: *Rumbum*; from the Portuguese *rumbo*.</note>—into fragments <note>original Greek: *τεμάχια* (temachia).</note> of spherical triangles and a sort of mince-meat. He treated this truly spiral <note>original Greek: *ἑλικοειδῆ* (helikoeide).</note> line, which is entirely unique in its nature, as a series of small parts. And from this came his first major mistake, so that he not infrequently stumbled here; nor did he bring this ship safely into harbor <note>A metaphor for failing to reach a complete or perfect scientific conclusion.</note>. Yet this fact is so far from detracting anything from his reputation that it even [aroused] <note>The text ends on the catchword "excita-", leading to "excitavit" (aroused/stirred) on the next page.</note> other great men to investigate these same things more accurately.

<summary>This page credits Pedro Nunes (Nonius) with moving navigation away from flawed flat charts toward spherical geometry, while also critiquing his mathematical method of treating spiral rhumb lines as a series of small triangles.</summary>

stirred [them]. These men, most skilled in **Mathematical** and **Geographical** matters, have scrutinized the properties and use of **loxodromes** <term>loxodrome</term> <note>Also called a rhumb line; a curve on a sphere that crosses all meridians at the same angle, allowing a navigator to maintain a constant compass heading.</note> with singular zeal. For these insightful men easily understood that an explanation of this was not only useful for maritime affairs, but exceedingly necessary; they were not unaware that just as far as they were from a complete and refined knowledge of this, so too was this very science far from its own perfection. And indeed, some have attempted with the greatest care to mark and express these lines on the globe itself; others on **hydrographic charts** <note>Navigational maps depicting coastlines, depths, and currents.</note> which, by their own analogy, might correspond more closely to the globe. In this matter, that most learned man and most diligent of Geographers, **Gerardus Mercator**, should be judged to have spent his efforts to great merit; and after him, several others in the Low Countries, and also our own **Stevinus** <note>Simon Stevin (1548–1620), a Flemish mathematician and engineer who worked on navigation and decimal fractions.</note>, in his great work. Also not a few in France. In Britain, **Edward Wright** <note>An English mathematician (1561–1615) who first explained the mathematical reasoning behind the Mercator projection.</note> was more diligent than the rest; then **Robert Hues**, and, as I hear, many others. But to speak frankly of the matter as it stands: a final touch <note>original: *Coronis*; a Greek-derived term for the finishing stroke or flourish at the end of a manuscript.</note> and a perfect mapping of the work seemed to be lacking, so that it might be established exactly how much each individual loxodrome and its parts varied in longitude, even down to the minute. And this was not to be done by some crude or mechanical method, but by that more accurate way which calculates every smallest particle. Therefore, this is what I have pursued: to finally exhibit everything now, solidly demonstrated from the very foundation and arranged in order. > He who first cut through the deep with a ship he had fashioned,

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the poetic reflection on navigation on the previous page to the scientific and natural history of the magnet (lodestone), which is the physical basis for the mariner's compass. It begins with a celebrated passage from Pliny the Elder and concludes with a technical table of corrections (errata) for astronomical or geographical tables.</meta>

<header>Pliny, Book 36, Chapter 16.</header>

As one departs from marbles to consider the remarkable natures of the remaining stones, who could doubt that the **Magnet** should be the first to occur <gloss>to the mind</gloss>? For what is more marvelous? Or in what part has the power of nature been greater? She had already given a voice to rocks—as we have seen with echoes—that answer man, or rather, even speak back. What is more sluggish than the rigid coldness of a stone? Yet, behold, nature has granted it senses and hands. What is more defiant than the hardness of iron? Yet it yields and submits to the stone's influence: for iron is drawn by the magnet stone, and that material which subdues all other things runs toward a kind of void; as it draws near, it stops, is held fast, and clings in a close embrace. Because of this, some call it <term>Sideritis</term> <note>original: "Sideritin", from the Greek 'sideros' meaning iron; thus, the 'iron-stone'.</note>, while others call it the <term>Heraclean stone</term> <note>original: "Heraclean", named after the city of Heraclea in Lydia, a famous source of lodestones.</note>. It was named the "Magnet" after its discoverer—as the author <term>Nicander</term> <note>Nicander of Colophon was a 2nd-century BC Greek poet and physician who wrote about natural history.</note> attests—who found it on <term>Mount Ida</term> <note>A mountain in Crete or possibly the one near ancient Troy; both were associated with early metalworking myths.</note>. Indeed, it is found in many places, such as in Spain. The story goes that he discovered it when the nails of his sandals and the iron tip of his staff stuck to the ground while he was tending his herds.

# WILLEBRORD SNELL'S
# TIPHYS <note>In Greek mythology, Tiphys was the pilot of the *Argo*, the ship of Jason and the Argonauts. Snellius uses this name to represent the master of navigation.</note>,
## OR
### ON
## NAVIGATION <note>original: *Histiodromia*, a word Snellius constructed from the Greek *histion* (sail) and *dromos* (course or running).</note>
### BOOK ONE.

### PROPOSITION I.

*Histiodromics is the science which describes the nature and properties of the line traced by a ship's course as it follows the direction of the magnetic needle.*

<note>A decorative initial N at the start of the main text features a large capital 'N' intertwined with ornate floral patterns and foliage, set within a square border.</note>
For the master of a ship directs his course toward the desired goal in such a way that he always follows the path of a certain kind of line <note>Snellius is referring to the rhumb line or "loxodrome," a path that maintains a constant angle with the meridians, which he will define mathematically in this book.</note>: and in this matter, he diligently observes the position of the magnetic needle and the angles it forms with the intended course. 
<margin>William Gilbert, Book 3, Chapter 4.</margin> <note>Gilbert was an English physician and scientist whose 1600 work *De Magnete* was the first systematic study of magnetism.</note>
* For it is common knowledge that a steel needle, which moves freely upon the small axle on which it rests, is touched by the south pole <note>original: *notio*</note> of a magnet stone <note>original: *magnetis lapidis*</note> at that part which faces north <note>original: *boream*</note>; and conversely, it is touched by the north pole <note>original: *boreo*</note> of that same stone at the part which is to point south <note>original: *notum*</note>; as it is commonly said...

<summary>Willebrord Snellius begins his treatise by defining "Histiodromics" (the science of navigation) as the study of a ship's path in relation to the magnetic needle. He cites William Gilbert to explain how a compass needle is magnetized to align with the north and south poles.</summary>
<keywords>Willebrord Snell, Tiphys, navigation, Histiodromia, magnetic needle, William Gilbert, compass, rhumb line, north pole, south pole</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Snellius is explaining the fundamental behavior of the magnetic needle (the compass). The previous page ended with the needle being "touched by the south pole of a magnet stone," and here he concludes that this process is well-known before moving into the phenomenon of magnetic variation.</meta>

<header>WILLEBRORD SNELL</header>

is already known, I believe. And in this way, it always extends itself between north and south. Yet, experience has long since proven that this position sometimes departs from the laws of nature, and this for some hidden and secret cause. For although the magnet, when it moves freely, seeks the poles of the world with its own poles by its innate power and faculty, and extends its own axis in a position parallel to the axis of the world—so that a properly magnetized <note>original: *tincta*, literally "dyed" or "dipped," referring to the needle being "seasoned" or "charged" by the lodestone.</note> needle directs itself according to the local meridians—it has nevertheless been discovered to decline from this meridian line at any given place by several degrees, sometimes from the north toward the east, and sometimes toward the west. 

And when the needle looks straight toward the North, they call it the "steel-pointing" <note>original Greek: *χαλυβόδειξιν* (*chalybodeixin*)</note>, as if it were the proper and legitimate position of the needle. However, when it varies, they refer to its inclination away from the meridian line as the "steel-leaning" <note>original Greek: *χαλυβόκλισιν* (*chalyboklisin*)</note>. Thus, on the island of **Corvo** <note>The westernmost island of the Azores.</note>, one of the Flemish Islands, located almost midway between Europe and America, the needle looks straight toward the north. For this reason, some writers derive the starting point for all meridians <note>The Prime Meridian, from which longitude was measured.</note> from this specific meridian. 

From this point, for those traveling toward America, the northern tip of the needle declines toward the west; conversely, for those seeking Europe or Africa, it shifts toward the east. Specifically, at Plymouth in England, the declination is 13 degrees and 24 minutes <note>original: *scr.* for *scrupula*, an old term for minutes of a degree.</note>; at London, it is 11 degrees and 30 minutes. Here with us in Amsterdam, it is 9 degrees and 30 minutes, as is commonly believed; and so it varies differently elsewhere. Indeed, you may see a summary table of these variations in the *Haven-Finding Art* <note>original: *Limeneuretica*, a work by the Dutch mathematician Simon Stevin published in 1599 which proposed navigating by magnetic declination.</note> by the most ingenious **Stevin**. The Englishman **William Gilbert** also attempted to investigate and uncover the causes of this variation with great subtlety in a specific volume dedicated to this subject <note>A reference to Gilbert’s seminal 1600 work, *De Magnete*, the first modern scientific study of magnetism.</note>.

<summary>Snellius explains magnetic declination—the tendency of a compass needle to deviate from true north—and cites the observations of Simon Stevin and William Gilbert. He notes that the variation is zero at the island of Corvo, which some used as a Prime Meridian.</summary>
<keywords>magnetic declination, magnetic variation, Corvo, Prime Meridian, Simon Stevin, William Gilbert, compass needle, navigation, Amsterdam, London, Plymouth</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Willebrord Snell (Snellius) has just introduced the terms for when a compass needle points exactly north (chalybodeixis) versus when it deviates (chalyboklisis). He now transitions into formal propositions regarding the "legitimate" versus "spurious" positions of the needle.</meta>

<header>THE DUTCH TIPHYS <note>original: *Tiphys Batavus*. Tiphys was the mythological pilot of the ship Argo; Snellius uses this name to frame his work as a guide for Dutch navigators.</note> BOOK I.</header>

### PROPOSITION II.

*The legitimate position of the magnetic needle is the local meridian* <note>The meridian is the imaginary line running north-south through a specific location.</note> *line.*

This position alone is legitimate; all others are illegitimate <note>original Greek: *νόθοι* (*nothoi*), meaning "bastard" or "spurious" positions.</note> and spurious. For that hidden and innate "mind" <note>Snell uses *mens* (mind/disposition) to describe the internal force or "soul" of the magnet that governs its movement.</note> within the magnet so agitates its mass that it extends its own poles and axis in a position parallel to the Earth's axis—unless it is pulled away from this position, even slightly, now this way and now that, by some natural fascination <note>In 17th-century science, "fascination" referred to a natural but invisible influence or attraction.</note>. In this way, the needle declines differently in different places. This inclination <note>Snell uses "inclination" here to mean what we now call magnetic declination or variation—the angle between true north and magnetic north.</note>, however, must be carefully corrected if we wish to follow its guidance with any profit.

### PROPOSITION III.

*The spurious position of the magnetic needle is corrected by finding the "steel-leaning"* <note>original: *chalyboclisi*. A term Snell coined from Greek to describe magnetic variation.</note>.

Since it is uncertain whether the needle looks straight toward the north and south, or whether it departs from that position in another direction, this can be detected from the rising and setting of the sun and stars, or from their equal altitudes on both sides <note>A method of finding the meridian by measuring the sun's position at the same height before and after noon.</note>, or finally by other methods; this is common practice at sea. From these observations, it is established whether the magnetic needle extends in such a way that it bisects the circumference <note>The arc of the horizon.</note> contained by both these lines in the plane of the horizon, or how much it departs from the center toward either side. From this, the legitimate position of the meridian and the aberration of the magnetic needle to one side or the other may be defined. But we have relegated those technical demonstrations to our lectures. See those for further study.

<header>WILLEBRORD SNELL</header>

### PROPOSITION IV.

*Navigation is either circular or Loxodromic.* <note>original: *Histiodromia*. From the Greek words for "sail" and "running," this refers to the science of setting a course.</note>

Nature has so arranged the course of sailing, in which one follows the lead of the magnetic needle, that it traces either a certain circle or a kind of helical line, which we call a Loxodrome <note>original: *Loxodromiam*, from the Greek for "oblique course." Today known as a rhumb line, this is a path that crosses all meridians at the same angle.</note> because of its slant.

---

### PROPOSITION V.

*A sailing course is made circular when it is directed straight toward the North and South, or the East and West.*

---

### PROPOSITION VI.

*A sailing course toward the North or South describes the circumference of a great circle.* <note>A "great circle" is any circle that circumnavigates the Earth and passes through its center, representing the shortest distance between two points on a sphere.</note>

For by this method, the ship always remains on the same meridian <note>The imaginary line of longitude running from pole to pole.</note> upon which it had stood from the beginning; and indeed, it is known to anyone who has even a slight taste of spherical geometry <note>original: *Sphærica*.</note> that meridians are great circles. Therefore:

*A course of fifteen maritime miles here changes the elevation of the pole by one degree.* <note>Snellius is calculating the Earth's circumference. "Elevation of the pole" refers to the ship's latitude.</note>

*How much maritime or nautical miles differ from land miles and hourly miles will be explained below in the eighth proposition.*

<meta>This page continues Willebrord Snell’s geometric analysis of navigation. Having established that sailing north or south follows a "great circle" (a meridian), he now examines the geometry of sailing due east or west.</meta>

<header>DUTCH TIPHYS, BOOK I.</header>

## PROPOSITION VII.

*A sailing course toward the east or west describes a great circle when under the equator, but outside of it, it always describes a circle parallel to the equator.*

For let there be a line cutting through the middle of the keel from the prow to the stern—which the Greeks call the *tropis* <note>original: τροπίδα (*tropida*), meaning the keel or the backbone of the ship.</note>—standing perpendicular to any meridian in any given place; in that case, the ship will face directly toward the east or the west. And if that segment of the meridian, with the keel fixed to it, is understood to be rotated around the poles of the world, then this line of the keel will form right angles with all the meridians that this segment meets. And in this way, it will describe the circumference of a circle. 

The same result will be achieved if the keel, advancing by its own proper motion, passes through all meridians perpendicularly—that is, facing east or west. For this line will coincide with the former. 

And therefore, when the beginning of the motion is under the equator <note>original: *æquinoctiali*. In early modern navigation, the "equinoctial line" is the equator, so named because the sun crosses it at the equinox.</note>, the ship will run along the equator until, having completed its circuit, it is returned again to the same point from which it began to move. Or if it begins to move from any place whatever outside the equator, and continues its rotation and movement until it returns to the same point, by this path it will entirely trace out the parallel <note>A line of latitude.</note> of that location.

Therefore

<meta>catchword: PRO-</meta>

<summary>Snellius explains that sailing due east or west follows the Earth's parallels of latitude. This movement only forms a "great circle" (the shortest path around the sphere) when the ship is on the equator.</summary>

<header>WILLEBRORD SNELL</header>

### PROPOSITION VIII.

->*Under the equator, fifteen maritime miles change one*<-
->*degree in longitude; in other parallels, even*<-
->*more.*<-

The measurement of the journey is of the utmost importance in maritime affairs; <note>original: "utramque facit paginam," literally "makes up both pages," a Latin idiom meaning a subject is of the highest importance or pervades the entire work.</note> for without this, all their <term>Hydrography</term> <note>The science of surveying and mapping the sea, its coasts, and hazards.</note> and their descriptions of ports, shores, and islands would be incomplete and full of errors. But this measurement cannot be profitably taken into account unless its relationship to the circumference of the earthly globe, or to some part of the circumference, is accurately known. For this reason, it has commonly happened that people divide a degree into parts and call them "miles," according to the custom of their own nation. 

The English, for instance, set as many miles in a degree of a great circle as there are minutes contained in it, namely sixty. In this matter, they followed the custom of the Germans and other nations, who assign fifteen miles to one degree, since an English mile makes up a fourth part of a German mile. Certain Portuguese, as <term>Nonius</term> <note>Pedro Nunes (1502–1578), a celebrated Portuguese mathematician and cosmographer.</note> testifies, assign 16 2/3 miles to one degree, while others assign 17 1/2. 

We have taught elsewhere that 19 "hourly miles" are contained in one degree, each of which contains 18,000 of our <term>Rhineland feet</term>. <note>The Rhineland foot (Rhijnlandse voet) was a widely used unit of length in the Netherlands, roughly 31.4 centimeters.</note> These I am accustomed to call "land miles." Alternatively, one might use 15 miles, so that 22,800 feet are assigned to each, which I then call "maritime miles," because the use of these does not deviate from nautical custom. I have added only one thing—which was the prize-winning stroke <note>original: "palmarium," referring to a "masterpiece" or "the best part."</note>—the exact method of measurement. But regarding this matter, see either my lectures or our <term>Dutch Eratosthenes</term>. <note>Snell is referring to his own book, *Eratosthenes Batavus* (1617), where he published his groundbreaking measurement of the Earth's circumference using triangulation.</note> 

Now, to determine how many miles are in one degree in any given—
