...yield to whatever parallel, I explain in the following theorem.

### PROPOSITION IX.

*Just as the radius is to the complement of a given parallel, so is the quantity of one degree on the equator to the quantity of one degree on the given parallel.*

<note>A circular diagram represents a sphere. A vertical axis is labeled 's' at the top and 'r' at the bottom. A horizontal diameter is labeled 'i' on the left, 'a' in the center, and 'e' on the right. A parallel chord 'uo' is drawn above the diameter, with 'u' on the left and 'o' on the right. The vertical axis intersects this chord at point 'y'. Point 'l' is on the left arc between 'u' and 'i'. An arc 'oe' is indicated on the right side between the chord and the diameter.</note>

How many miles correspond to one degree in each individual parallel is found by the proportion set forth here. For let *sr* be the axis of the world, *ie* the common intersection of the meridian and the <term>equinoctial</term> <note>The Equator, so called because when the sun crosses it, day and night are of equal length.</note>, and *uo* the intersection of the parallel and the meridian. Therefore, *ie* and *uo* will be the diameters of their respective circles, and *ae* and *yo* their radii. Thus, as *ae* is to *yo*, so is the entire circumference on diameter *ie* to the circumference described on *uo*. 

But as the whole is to the whole, so is a part to a similar part. Therefore, as the <term>total sine</term> <note>In the trigonometry of Snell's era, the "total sine" refers to the radius of the circle used for calculations, often standardized as 100,000 to avoid working with decimals.</note> *ae* is to *yo*—which is the sine of the <term>complement</term> <note>The angle needed to complete 90 degrees; here, it refers to the co-latitude.</note> of the arc *oe* (which is the measure of the latitude)—so is the quantity of one degree on the great circle (estimated at 15 marine miles) to the number of miles in the given parallel.

For example, if the size of one degree is sought under the parallel of 52 degrees and 10 minutes <note>Roughly the latitude of Holland.</note>, the complement of this is 37 degrees and 50 minutes, and its sine is 61,337. Therefore, let the calculation be: as 100,000 is to 61,337, so 15 is to 9 and 2005.5/10,000 for the quantity of miles contained in one degree under the given parallel. And so...

<summary>Snell provides a geometric proof and trigonometric formula for calculating the length of a degree of longitude at any given latitude, using 15 miles at the equator as his base.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Willebrord Snell’s mathematical demonstration from the previous page, where he established the ratio between degrees at the equator and degrees at specific parallels. Here, he moves from whole degrees to "minutes" (scrupuli), providing a direct formula for navigators to calculate small distances at any given latitude.</meta>

WILLEBRORD SNELL

### PROPOSITION X.

->*A fourth of the sine of the complement of a given parallel divided by the radius will give the length of one minute.*<-

---

<margin>Proof</margin> For since, by the preceding proposition, the ratio of the radius to the <term>sine of the complement</term> <note>original: "sinum complementi"; this is what we now call the "cosine."</note> of a given parallel is the same as the ratio of 15 miles <note>Snell uses the "German mile" or league, common in 17th-century Northern Europe, which was defined as 15 miles per degree of the Earth's circumference.</note> to the number of miles in one degree at that given parallel. And yet, since 1/4 of a mile is equal to 1/60th of a degree (and is therefore the measure of one <term>minute</term> <note>original: "scrupuli"; literally "a small sharp stone," used here as a technical term for a minute of arc, 1/60th of a degree.</note> on a <term>great circle</term> <note>The "greatest circle" refers to the Equator.</note>); it will also follow that as the radius is to the sine of the complement of the given parallel, so is 1/4 of a mile to the measure of a minute in that parallel. Or, by interpreting this with the terms transposed: as the radius is to one-fourth of the sine of the complement of the given parallel, so is 1 mile to the measure of one minute.

Let us use the same example as before: under the parallel of 52 degrees, 10 minutes. The sine of its complement is given as 61,337 <note>In the decimal-free trigonometry of the 1600s, the radius was often set at 100,000; thus 61,337 represents a cosine of 0.61337.</note>. Its fourth part, 15,334, divided by the radius, will give 15,334/100,000 of a mile—the distance of one minute in the said parallel. And indeed, this agrees with the previous <term>calculation</term> <note>original: "epilogismo"; a summary calculation or conclusion.</note>. For the sixtieth part of 9 1/5 is 9 1/5 / 60, or 15,334/100,000, exactly as we expressed above. And so, without any roundabout way, the distance of one minute in any parallel whatever can be shown.

<meta>catchword: PRO-</meta>

<summary>Snell provides a mathematical formula for calculating the length of one minute of arc at any latitude by taking one-fourth of the cosine (sine of the complement) and dividing it by the radius. This allows for precise navigation and distance measurement without complex calculations.</summary>
<keywords>trigonometry, latitude, nautical miles, minutes of arc, cosine, Snell's Law, geodesy, navigation</keywords>

<header>THE DUTCH TIPHYS, BOOK I.</header>
<sig>B</sig>

## PROPOSITION XI.

*As the radius is to four times the secant of a given parallel, so are any given miles to the number of minutes owed to them in that same parallel.*

This is the converse of both preceding propositions, if you consider its purpose. For in those, the miles corresponding to given degrees or minutes are sought. Here, on the contrary, from a given number of miles, the number of <term>minutes</term> <note>original: "scrupulorum"; Snell uses the classical term for "small units" to describe minutes of arc.</note> owed to them under a given parallel is investigated. For it was demonstrated in the ninth and tenth propositions that the radius is to the <term>sine of the complement</term> <note>original: "sinum complementi"; this refers to the cosine of the latitude.</note> of a given parallel, as the number of miles in any given part of a <term>great circle</term> <note>The "great circle" refers to the Equator.</note> is to the miles corresponding to a similar part in the given parallel. 

And therefore, inversely: as the sine of the complement of a given parallel is to the radius—that is, as the radius is to the <term>secant</term> <note>The secant is the reciprocal of the cosine (1/cos). Snell is using the mathematical property that Radius/Cosine = Secant/Radius.</note> of the given parallel—so are the miles in that same parallel to the miles owed to a similar arc on the <term>equator</term> <note>original: "æquinoctiali".</note>. Furthermore, since fifteen miles fulfill one degree <note>Snell is using the "German mile" or league, where 15 miles equal 1 degree (60 minutes) of the Earth's circumference.</note>; therefore four times these miles will show the number of minutes owed to them on the equator <note>If 15 miles = 60 minutes, then 1 mile = 4 minutes. Thus, multiplying miles by four converts them into equivalent minutes of arc on a great circle.</note>, and that same number of minutes also corresponds to a similar arc in the given parallel.

The matter will be made clearer by an example. Suppose I wish to find in the sixtieth parallel how many minutes correspond to 15 nautical miles. Let the calculation be: as 10,000,000 <note>The standard value used for the Radius in trigonometric tables of this era.</note> is to 80,000,000 (which is four times the secant of sixty degrees <note>The secant of 60° is 2.0. In Snell’s decimal-free notation with a radius of 10 million, the secant is 20,000,000. Four times this is 80,000,000.</note>), so 15 is to 120. I will say, therefore, that in the sixtieth parallel, 15 miles occupy 120 minutes, which are two whole degrees; this is entirely true. For in this parallel, the individual degrees are half the size of those contained in the great circle. This is because the sine of its complement—namely the sine of 30 degrees—is one half...

<meta>catchword: is the sine</meta>

<summary>Snell explains how to convert physical miles traveled at a specific latitude into minutes of longitude, using the secant of the latitude and a conversion factor of four minutes per mile.</summary>
<keywords>secant, longitude calculation, nautical miles, scrupula, trigonometry, parallel of latitude, Dutch Tiphys</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Snell’s mathematical demonstration of how to calculate longitudinal distance from miles traveled. <header>WILLEBRORD SNELL</header>

is the sine of the great circle. But if there should be more or fewer miles than 15 <note>Snell uses the "German mile" or league, which he calculates at 15 miles per degree of the Equator.</note>, the truth of the calculation remains the same: for miles are proportional to miles, and <term>minutes</term> <note>original: "scrupula"; literally "small stones," a term used for minutes of arc.</note> to minutes.

The island of Terceira, one of the Azores (or Flemish Isles), located at the 39th parallel, is 250 miles distant from Lisbon, the great trading port of Spain, which lies under nearly the same parallel. It is asked: how great is the interval of longitude? Four times the <term>secant</term> <note>The secant is a trigonometric function (1/cosine). Snell uses it to adjust for the narrowing of the Earth's circumference as one moves away from the Equator.</note> of the given parallel is 514,703. Therefore, let the ratio be as 100,000 is to 514,703, so 250 miles is to 1,286 3/4 minutes—which equals 21 degrees and 26 3/4 minutes. Thus, by so many degrees is Terceira further west than Lisbon.

## PROPOSITION XII.

*As the radius is to four times the sum of the secants included between the given parallels; so is the proportional part of the sum of the given miles assigned to those same parallels, to the sum of the minutes of longitude, as much as accrues to each of the said parallels from the given miles.*

This theorem is clear from the preceding one and derived from the same source. Suppose 10 miles are to be assigned to 20 parallels, separated by an interval of one minute each, starting from (and excluding) the 52nd degree of latitude. Thus, there are twenty circles here. Therefore, so that I may assign an equal part to each parallel, the distance will be 1/2 of a mile <note>10 miles divided by 20 intervals equals 0.5 miles per interval.</note>. And because a half-mile does not occupy an equal number of minutes across all of these parallels—but rather a greater number in the lower latitudes and a smaller number in the higher ones <note>Snell is describing the convergence of meridians toward the poles. A mile covers more "longitude" the further north you go.</note>—the conclusion must be reached for each one individually: as the radius is to four times the secant of 52 degrees and 1 minute, so is 1/2 to the minutes owed to it in this parallel. And again, as the radius is to four times...

# WILLEBRORD SNELL

So that from this, a fourth proportional may be given: 68 **minutes** <term>minutes</term> <note>original: "scrupula"; literally "small stones," used by Snell to mean minutes of arc.</note> and 28 1/2 **seconds**. This, therefore, is the total amount of space that the given miles would occupy if distributed equally across each of the 20 individual parallels; however, the number of minutes in each individual parallel is unequal. For in the first parallel—namely at 52 degrees, 1 minute—a half-mile occupies 3 minutes and 14 20/121 seconds; but in the last, it occupies 3 minutes and 16 10/121 seconds. Although this difference is small in such close proximity, it becomes quite large over a longer interval. Indeed, a half-nautical mile at the equator <note>original: "sub æquinoctiali."</note> occupies exactly 2 minutes.

And so, with a single multiplication after the secants have been added, you are finished with this task. But such frequent addition, which must be repeated anew for every calculation according to the proportion sought, involves so much boredom that it is hardly worth the effort to undergo these labors. The utility that might be hoped for here lies buried and, as it were, crushed under the very mass of labor and trouble. 

To remedy this—because the use of such calculations is so necessary and nearly constant in this work—I have constructed a **Table** <note>original: "Canonem"; Snell uses "Canon" in the sense of a mathematical rule or table, specifically referring to what we now call a table of "meridional parts."</note> forged from added secants, progressing continuously through every single minute. The use of this table will later be by far the most convenient for us in calculating rhumb lines <term>loxodromies</term> <note>A rhumb line (loxodrome) is a path with a constant compass bearing. On a globe, it spirals toward the poles.</note> and in estimating the distance of a changed longitude. 

I include the table itself here, the construction of which consists solely of addition: namely, the **radius** <note>In early modern trigonometry, the "radius" was often set as a large integer like 10,000,000 to avoid using decimals.</note> itself, 10,000,000, is added to the secant of one minute, 10,000,001, from which the sum 20,000,001 is produced; this, added to the secant of 2 minutes, 10,000,002, will give 30,000,003; and this number, combined with the secant of 3 minutes, 10,000,004, will produce 40,000,007; and so on in that order, proceeding through every single minute to the furthest limit of the quadrant. I have extended this table to the seventieth degree, which is sufficient for maritime use, which we are primarily considering here; and if you proceed beyond that, it should be done not only by single minutes, but by halves, thirds, and sixths of minutes...

<summary>Snellius explains that manual calculations of longitude are too tedious for practical use and introduces his "Canon" or table of accumulated secants, which simplifies navigation along rhumb lines.</summary>

# THE DUTCH TIPHYS, BOOK I.

13

indeed, one would even have to proceed to "inches" <note>original: "uncias"; here Snellius refers to tiny fractions of a second of arc, using the term for a twelfth of a foot to describe a twelfth of a second.</note> in some places; finally, when you approach the pole, calculations would have to be made even by seconds or thirds. But such labor would be both fruitless and useless. Nevertheless, we provide some specimen of this precision <note>original Greek: "ἀκριβείας"</note> in the lessons below. If anyone should like to imitate it, they have a place to spend their leisure time without harm. We, however, persist in our established purpose. And so that this calculation <term>calculus</term> could be drawn up more securely, and any error that might have crept in upon us more certainly detected, I have calculated the same thing twice: once from the tables of secants by Thomas Fincke, and again from the tables of Bartholomaeus Pitiscus. This was done so that if any flaw had perhaps slipped into the tables themselves through the negligence of the workers, it would have an easy correction through mutual comparison; for the values of Pitiscus are known to be expressed from the *Opus Palatinum* <note>The *Opus Palatinum de Triangulis* (1596) was a monumental work of trigonometric tables begun by Rheticus, a student of Copernicus.</note>, while those of Fincke are different from these and were calculated earlier.

I have placed the table itself at the end of the book, to which I gave the title *Rule of Parallels* <note>original: *Canonica Parallelorum*.</note>.

But concerning the construction and order, I must advise you of this: I began the addition from the radius <note>In 17th-century trigonometry, the "radius" refers to the "total sine" or the base value used for the sine of 90°, often set at 10,000,000 to avoid decimals.</note> itself for the reason that it pertains to the equinoctial <note>The equator.</note> itself. Because each single mile occupies four minutes <term>minutes</term> <note>original: "scrupula"; Snellius uses this term for minutes of arc.</note> there, four times the number of miles will define the number of minutes owed to them. And so the same analogy returns for us: as the radius is to four times the radius, so is the number of miles to the corresponding minutes.

In the notation of degrees and minutes, we have followed this order: we have prefixed "1" to the equinoctial circle as if it were the first principle of all parallels; the remaining parallels we have marked off in order from this one, so that one degree is the sixtieth parallel, the first of which is the equinoctial. Thus, the parallel which is drawn through the fifty-ninth minute is for us the sixtieth number, and the one through the sixtieth minute is the sixty-first, or 1 degree.

### PROPOSITION XIII.

*If the starting point of given parallels is taken inclusively from the equator, four times the number corresponding to the last parallel increased by one will fulfill the second term of the proportion; but if the starting point is from elsewhere, yet both latitudes are in the same hemisphere, four times the difference between the numbers for the given parallels increased by one; but if they are in different hemispheres, four times the sum of the parallels increased by one.*

This is the primary use of the presented tables: so that from them, the term of that proportion which we expressed in the 12th proposition may be taken. But the matter will be made clearer with examples.

For let it be asked: how many minutes <note>original: "scrupula"; Snellius uses the term "scruples" to refer to minutes of arc.</note> of longitude are occupied by 750 miles <term>miliaria</term>, traveling from the equator <note>original: "æquinoctiali"</note> to the parallel at 44 degrees 59 minutes inclusive, so that an equal portion of miles is assigned to each parallel? 

In this case, you must take the table number <term>numerus canonicus</term> found at the 45th degree, which is 30,311,462. For what we commonly call the parallel of 44 degrees 59 minutes becomes 45 degrees if you count the equator itself (as was done in the construction of these tables). This span will encompass 2,700 parallels <note>45 degrees multiplied by 60 minutes equals 2,700 minutes or "parallels" in Snellius's calculation.</note>. Furthermore, four times the assumed number is 121,245,848. Now, the number of given miles divided by the 2,700 parallels will give...

Four times this sum is 23,943.6124. The number of parallels, however, is 5,281—namely, 52 degrees 1 minute and 36 degrees 1 minute...

### PROPOSITION XIV.

#### *Given the degrees and minutes of the change in longitude, the required measure of miles distributed equally among the parallels lying between the given points will also be given.*

This is the converse of the preceding proposition, and here is the second use of our table <term>canonis</term> <note>The "canon" refers to the trigonometric and meridional parts tables Snellius constructed for this work.</note>; by both of which the entire utility of Rhumb Lines is most clearly illustrated. For example, from the equator to the parallel of 44 degrees 59 minutes inclusive: that place, increased by one unit, will give 45 degrees. Therefore, if 1 mile is assumed in each parallel, there will be 12,124 and 5,848/10,000 minutes for the change in longitude. Conversely, therefore, the ratio of minutes attributed by an equal space of the journey through all parallels...

### P R O P O S I T I O N XV.

> *A Rhumb Line <term>Loxodromia</term> <note>From the Greek 'loxos' (oblique) and 'dromos' (course). Snellius was a pioneer in defining these mathematically as paths of constant bearing.</note> is a spiral-shaped <note>original: "ἑλικοειδὴς" (helikoeidēs)</note> line on the surface of the terrestrial globe, which, everywhere it touches a straight line, forms equal angles with all the meridians drawn through those points of contact.*

If a ship is ever steered straight toward the north or south, it then runs along a meridian, and therefore along a great circle of the earth. However, I have taught above that when it seeks straight for either the east <note>original: "ortum" (the rising sun)</note> or the west <note>original: "occasum" (the setting sun)</note>, it then forms a right angle with all the meridians through which it is carried; and therefore it describes some parallel in its course. But

<meta>catchword: truly (original: "vero")</meta>

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues the definition of a Rhumb Line (Loxodrome) from Proposition XV, moving from the geometric definition to the practical application in navigation and how it differs from circular paths on a globe.</meta>

WILLEBRORD SNELL
<page-num>18</page-num>

Truly, when [the ship] holds any other course different from these two <note>Referring to the cardinal directions of North-South or East-West mentioned on the previous page.</note>, and adjusts itself to the position of the magnetic needle (which, however, should be understood as previously corrected according to the estimated **magnetic variation** <term>kalyboklisin</term> <note>original: "καλυβοκλίσιν". Snellius uses this Greek term to refer to the angle between true north and magnetic north, now called magnetic declination.</note>), it arranges itself so that the **keel** <term>tropis</term> <note>original: "τρόπις"</note> of the ship, which extends through the middle of the hull from the prow to the stern, forms a certain angle—and always the same angle—with all the meridians it intersects. Then indeed, as long as it holds that course, it describes a **spiral line** <note>original: "helicem lineam"</note>, like those we have expressed here on a plane according to that formula as closely as possible: namely *egbd* and *eioy*, where *efqkh* marks the equatorial line <note>original: "æquinoctialem lineam"</note> and *a* marks the pole of the earth. We shall discuss the mechanical method of drawing this both on a globe and on a plane in our lectures. This we call, from the "obliquity of the course," a **Loxodrome** <term>loxodromian</term> <note>original: "λοξοδρομίαν", from the Greek words for "oblique" and "running" or "course."</note> using the Greek term; which clearly, as we have said, can never be a circle. 

For circles oblique to any meridian—whether they be Great Circles or Small Circles—always encompass one angle after another, and that angle is varying and unequal to the rest. This can be seen in the circle of the **ecliptic** <note>The path of the Sun across the celestial sphere.</note> intersecting the **colures** <term>coluros</term> <note>The two principal meridians of the celestial sphere.</note>; for at its common intersection with the colure of the tropics, it forms right angles; but around the **nodes** <note>The points where the ecliptic crosses the celestial equator.</note> it forms only the complement of its maximum declination, namely 66 degrees and 29 minutes; whereas in intermediate locations, it makes all angles unequal to one another. In smaller circles, this variation is even more manifest. 

The Loxodrome, therefore, is a certain kind of **spiral-shaped** <term>helikoeidēs</term> <note>original: "ἑλικοειδὴς"</note> line of its own genus, whose length and position during the course itself the pilots, shipmasters, and other **naval personnel** <note>original: "navales pedes", a poetic way of referring to the crew or navigators who "tread" the sea.</note> suitable for this task accurately and diligently observe; they note it down separately, having estimated it most precisely by conjecture based on the speed or slowness of the vessel. For from here, the most certain indications of changed longitude as well as latitude (provided nothing has been erred in these conjectures) are deri- <margin>vari</margin> <note>The word continues on the next page: "derived".</note>

<summary>Snellius defines the loxodrome (rhumb line) as a spiral path of constant bearing that intersects all meridians at a fixed angle, distinguishing it from circular paths which have varying angles. He emphasizes that this constant-angle path is the fundamental line used by navigators to track their change in coordinates.</summary>

THE DUTCH TIPHYS, BOOK I.
<page-num>39</page-num>

...are accustomed to vary, so that from there they may recognize and define which shores they have reached, or how far they have traveled. Therefore, the accurate and constant measurement of this line, and the angle which it forms with the legitimate position of the **magnetic needle**—that is, with the true meridian of the place—constitute the whole of our task <note>original: "utramque... paginam nobis faciunt," literally "make up both pages for us," a Latin idiom meaning a matter is of primary importance or encompasses the whole subject.</note> in this art. For this reason alone, they devote such diligent labor to this matter. For they restore the displaced position of the magnetic needle with the greatest care they can through daily observations <note>Snellius is referring to correcting for magnetic declination, the difference between magnetic north and true north.</note>. And they explore the measurement of the course by casting a rope while using a momentary **hourglass** <term>clepsydra</term> <note>original: "clepsydra momentanea." Although a *clepsydra* is traditionally a water-clock, in 17th-century navigation this refers to the sandglass used to time the log-line.</note>, 
<margin>15</margin>almost in this manner: they cast from the stern a rope of a known length, ten or twenty **ten-foot rods** <term>decempedae</term> <note>original: "decempedarum." The *decempeda* was a Roman unit of length equal to ten feet.</note>, even estimating it as the leg of a right-angled triangle relative to the vertical line of the stern. At the end of this rope they tie a piece of wood or a small boat-shape, which floats transversely behind in the **furrow of the sea** <note>The wake left by the ship.</note> made by the ship's rapid motion. Thus, while they unwind the rope from a very easily turned reel, the wood remains fixed as if in the same spot; meanwhile, from the very beginning, they measure the space of time elapsed during the unwinding of the reel with a momentary hourglass. From this, they form a conjecture as to how much they progress each hour by such a process. More accurate masters of ships repeat this quite often, according to the varying force of the winds, so that by estimating as closely as possible through observations of this kind, they may approach the truth. And so, from the angle of the completed **Loxodrome** and its quantity, they afterwards enter into a calculation of the changed latitude and longitude. It was certainly of great importance to have the accounts of Loxodromes well explained. It is an ingenious invention, which nevertheless has proceeded only so far that its properties could be used only mechanically, and could not be explained by more accurate calculation. WILLEBRORD SNELL'S
<page-num>20</page-num>

...having been set forth <note>The text begins mid-sentence, continuing from the previous page's discussion on measuring speed and direction.</note> by geometric lines, we teach how to follow them, a matter which possesses singular utility combined with the greatest ease. For the mechanical practice of these lines—as it is performed on those nautical charts <note>original: "chartis hydrographicis." Snellius is likely critiquing "plane charts" which did not account for the Earth's curvature, leading to significant navigation errors.</note>—is both laborious and slippery; such charts nowhere represent the position of the terrestrial globe with exactness, and they deviate so immensely from the truth that it is not so much a wonder as a necessity that this mechanical method headlong casts its results into manifest error.

<note>A geometric diagram depicts a loxodromic spiral on a polar projection. The center of the circle is labeled A. A spiral curve winds outward from the center. Radial lines representing meridians extend from point A to the circumference, which are labeled with Greek letters delta (δ), epsilon (ε), zeta (ζ), eta (η), theta (θ), iota (ι), kappa (κ), lambda (λ), mu (μ). ### PROPOSITIO XVI.

->*No loxodrome enters into the poles of the earth.*<-

**T**his is a corollary derived from the preceding proposition. For since the form of the **loxodrome** <term>loxodromia</term> <note>From the Greek *loxos* (slanting) and *dromos* (course); a line that crosses all meridians at the same angle.</note> is judged entirely by the angle which it forms with the **meridian** of the place, and if this angle is removed the loxodrome no longer exists, the meridian itself takes the place of the matter; if it [the angle] is withdrawn, as if the legs...

<summary>Snellius critiques the inaccuracy of contemporary flat nautical charts and introduces Proposition XVI, proving that a rhumb line (loxodrome) mathematically spirals toward but never reaches the geographic poles.</summary>
<keywords>loxodrome, rhumb line, nautical charts, cartography, meridians, geometry, navigation errors, Willebrord Snellius</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Snellius's geometric argument from Proposition XVI, specifically addressing why a loxodrome—a line of constant bearing—can never actually reach the North or South Pole. He explores the definition of a "meridian" to show that at the very center of the poles, the mathematical and physical requirements for such a line disappear.</meta>

THE DUTCH TIPHYS BOOK I.
<page-num>21</page-num>

...when the sides of the angle are removed <note>Snellius is continuing his proof that as one approaches the pole, the geometric conditions required to define a rhumb line's angle relative to a meridian begin to fail.</note>, it is understood that no angle at all can be contained; so that both the reality and the name of the **loxodrome** <term>loxodromia: also known as a rhumb line, a path that crosses all meridians of longitude at the same angle</term> fall away. But indeed, both reason and demonstration convince us that beneath the poles and the hinges of the world on this globe of the Earth, there is no **meridian** <term>meridianus: a great circle passing through the celestial poles and the zenith of a particular location</term>. For in that point of the earth, no star, nor any other immobile point of the sky, either rises or sets, from which the notion of the meridian is solely derived, as the definition of the name proves. It is thus called *meridianus* by the Latins, and *mesembrinos* <note>original: μεσημβρινὸς</note> or *mesemerinos* <note>original: μεσημέρινος</note> by the Greeks, as if to say "mid-day," because it cuts the middle of the time interval between the rising and setting of the same point. That it is called a "meridian" only from the rising and setting of the sun, however, is derived only from common usage and from the **phenomenon** <note>original: φαινομένῳ; an observable event</note> striking everyone's eyes.

> ->*When the sun, highest in the middle of its circle,*<-
> ->*Will look back at as much as it sees remaining.*<-
<note>This is a quote from the Roman poet Lucan’s "Pharsalia," describing the moment of noon when the sun is at its zenith.</note>

And if this were also to take place there <note>at the poles</note>, then the meridians there would be entirely changeable and wandering, and varying every year; since for us the sun enters the **equinoctial points** <term>aequinoctialia puncta: the points where the sun crosses the celestial equator, marking the start of spring and autumn</term> now in the middle of the sky, then at rising, then at the bottom of the sky, at other times at setting; finally, always in different places. And thus, according to this or that bisection, the position of the meridian circle would be inconsistent. For sometimes their meridian would be the same as ours; at other times it would be that **hour circle** <term>circulus horarius: any great circle passing through the celestial poles</term> which marks the sixth hour of the morning for us; at other times another, and always varying. Therefore, since no meridian is given here, even the properties which are usually attributed to it will vanish along with it. And for that reason, the loxodrome, which takes its name and form from the intersection of the meridian, can be conceived as plainly non-existent in this place beneath the poles—as shown in the diagram of the preceding proposition. The loxodrome *eiou*, continued further through *m*, *n*, and *p*, and from there to *c*, will nonetheless never run into the pole *a*.

<sig>C 3</sig>
<meta>sed</meta>

<summary>Snellius argues that the definition of a meridian fails at the poles because stars do not rise or set there, and consequently, a loxodrome (rhumb line) can never actually reach the pole.</summary>

but having been driven around in infinite turns <note>original: "infinitis gyris"</note>, it will never reach that point.

### PROPOSITION XVII.

*A **loxodrome** <term>loxodromia: a rhumb line, or a path that maintains a constant compass bearing</term> is like the base of a plane right-angled triangle applied to the surface of a sphere, of which one leg is the distance of the parallels between which it is intercepted.*

<note>A geometric diagram showing a hemisphere with a loxodromic spiral (rhumb line) curving from the periphery towards the center point (a). Radiating lines of longitude connect the center to points along the outer arc. The spiral curve is marked with the letters R, q, p, n, l, h, g, f, d, c. The baseline of the hemisphere is marked with B on the left and letters e, z, x on the right. Additional points on the radial lines are marked u, y, s, r, and the Greek letter β.</note>

The inclination of the angle which the keel of a ship makes with the **meridian** <term>meridianus: a great circle of longitude passing through the poles</term>, once it deviates from the perpendicular, can be entirely varied; and therefore the **spirals** <term>helices: the curved paths formed by the loxodrome as it winds around the globe</term> of the loxodrome are infinite, forming one angle after another with the meridians. Here, however, we call the angle that is smaller than a right angle the "angle of inclination." Since, moreover, for each individual line there is one and the same inclination to all meridians, and through individual points—even those very close—

<meta>catchword: niffima (completing the word "vicinissima" or "closest" on the following page)</meta>

<summary>Snellius explains that a loxodrome (rhumb line) acts as the hypotenuse of a right-angled triangle when projected onto a sphere's surface. He defines the "angle of inclination" as the constant bearing a ship maintains relative to all meridians of longitude.</summary>
<keywords>loxodrome, rhumb line, geometry of navigation, angle of inclination, meridian, spirals, spherical geometry</keywords>

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>This page continues Snellius's geometric breakdown of the loxodrome, picking up from the previous page's explanation of how very close segments of latitude and longitude interact to form a path for a ship.</meta>

WILLEBRORD SNELLIUS
<page-num>23</page-num>
### THE DUTCH TIPHYS BOOK I.

—very close segments of a parallel circle perpendicular to the meridian can be mentally traced, you see that a right-angled triangle is formed here. Furthermore, both the **meridian** <term>meridianus: a circle of longitude passing through the North and South poles</term> and the **loxodrome** <term>loxodromia: a path with a constant compass bearing</term> can be divided into the smallest possible parts, so that their difference from straight lines can hardly be expressed or perceived by any calculation; and these tiny parts can be divided again into other smaller thousandths. And although the difference between a straight and a curved line cannot be eliminated by this mincing of sections—nor is any part of a curved line, however small, ever truly straight—nevertheless, for the sake of perception and practical use, the difference entirely vanishes. Therefore, this smallest segment of the loxodrome that we conceive here becomes the **base** <note>In Snellius's geometry, the "base" of this small triangle refers to the hypotenuse, or the actual path traveled by the ship.</note> of the right angle, while the segment of the meridian becomes one leg. 

It follows this exact formula, just as happens in a right-angled triangle where one leg is equal to the circumference of a cylinder: if the other leg is applied to the side of a right cylinder and then wrapped around it, the base of the triangle will trace a **cylindrical helix** <term>helix: a spiral curve, like the thread of a screw</term> upon its surface. Pappus explicitly teaches this in Book 8, Proposition 24: so that that cylindrical helix corresponds to our loxodromic helix, and that leg of the triangle coinciding with the side—or what is the same thing, parallel to the axis—corresponds here to the segment of the meridian. However, the description and explanation of that cylindrical spiral is quite a bit easier than this one of ours; because on a cylinder, the parallel circles are all equal, whereas here on a sphere, it is far otherwise <note>On a globe, circles of latitude (parallels) get smaller as you move from the equator toward the poles, which complicates the math of the spiral.</note>.

<margin>A better example of the loxodrome is found not in the cited passage of Pappus, nor in Guidubaldo del Monte <note>original: "Guidubaldo de Cochlea"</note> Prop. 1, nor in David Rivault <note>original: "Davide Rivalto"</note> in his *External Works of Archimedes* where he treats the helix; but from Pappus of Alexandria himself in the *Mathematical Collection* Book 4, Prop. 30, and David Rivault in his commentaries on Archimedes’ *On Spirals*, Definition 1. See the notes.</margin>

Let us therefore understand that in the loxodrome *ε-ι-ο-υ* the segments of the meridians *j-ι*, *v-o*, and *u-f* are equal, and the angles *ε-ι-j*, *ι-o-v*, and *o-u-f* are also equal; the segments of the parallel circles *ε-j*, *ι-v*, and *o-f* are indeed not similar, but only equal. Here, the base of the whole triangle would be *ε-ι-ο-υ*; furthermore, let the remaining leg *ε-χ* be understood as being composed of the segments *ε-j*, *ι-v*, and *o-f*.

## PROPOSITION XVIII.

*The segments of the same loxodrome intercepted between parallel circles separated by an equal interval are equal.*

<margin>
<unclear>Handwritten note in margin: 4 lines of illegible cursive script</unclear>
</margin>

AND this likewise follows from the explained nature of **loxodromes** <term>loxodromia: a curve on a sphere that intersects all meridians at the same angle; a ship's path on a constant compass heading</term>. For suppose there is some loxodrome that reaches all the way to the **equinoctial line** <term>aequinoctialis: the equator</term> starting from the first degree of latitude, whatever angle it may eventually form with the meridians. Now, if we imagine **parallel circles** <note>Lines of latitude</note> passing through every single minute of latitude here, there will be sixty segments of this loxodrome intercepted between them. Therefore, by drawing parallels through those very points of intersection, just as many tiny right-angled triangles will be formed. In these, one "leg"—namely, the segment of the **meridian** <term>meridianus: a line of longitude</term>—is equal to the leg of another triangle; and the angle formed by the loxodrome (acting as the base) and the meridian is also equal. Consequently, the triangles themselves are both similar and equal. 

Or, if even this is not precise enough, imagine parallels being drawn through every single **second** <note>original: "scrupula," a term for small divisions of a degree</note>, and 3,600 right-angled triangles will be created. Indeed, if you conceive of even smaller segments, the explanation becomes even easier and its affinity with plane triangles even clearer. One might imagine a single degree on the meridian cut not just into thirds or fourths, but even into thousandths or millionths of a **second** <note>original: "scrupula"</note>. Thus, in these most minute triangles, each leg will equal the others; and the bases, which are the segments of that loxodrome, will likewise be equal to one another. 

This also corresponds to the **cylindrical helix** <note>The spiral shape of a screw thread</note>, whose segments between parallel circles separated by an equal interval are entirely equal. But beyond that, they are also congruent because of that line's **uniformity of parts** <term>ὁμοιομέρειαν (homoiomereia): a geometric property where any part of a curve can be superimposed on any other part, typical of circles and helices</term>, as Apollonius demonstrated in the works of Proclus—which in our...

<summary>Snellius proves that if a loxodrome (a ship's constant course) crosses equal increments of latitude, each segment of that path is of equal length. He justifies this by breaking the curve into infinitesimal right-angled triangles and comparing it to a cylindrical helix.</summary>
<keywords>loxodrome, geometry of navigation, meridian, latitude, infinitesimal triangles, Apollonius, cylindrical helix, uniformity of parts</keywords>

...we do not reach it by the **helix** <term>helix: here used as a synonym for the loxodrome or rhumb line, which spirals toward the pole</term>. Therefore, a segment of that same helix between the **equator** <note>original: "aequinoctialem"</note> and the first parallel will be exactly the same length as a segment between 45 and 46 degrees, or 50 and 51, or 80 and 81, and so on for all possible parallels. We have expressed this in the diagram above with the lines *ji*, *vo*, and *fu*.

<margin>Proposition 16a</margin>
The Earth’s pole, however, is a point, not a parallel circle, and I have demonstrated above that it cannot be reached by any **loxodromic** <term>loxodromia: a path with a constant compass heading</term> course, because that helix would fail <note>The mathematical curve of a rhumb line infinitely circles the pole without ever actually touching the point of the pole itself</note> before it could reach it. Nevertheless, the segments of loxodromes starting from a point near the pole between any two parallels separated by any interval (such as one minute) are just as large as those between parallels near the equator separated by the same interval. However, it is so that this smallest segment of the loxodrome revolves there many more times before it completes its full length between the designated parallels.

->PROPOSITION XIX.<-

*Given the length and the angle of inclination of a loxodrome, to find the distance between the parallels.*

Let a loxodrome be presented whose length is 50 maritime or nautical miles, and whose angle of inclination is 56 degrees and 15 minutes. The change in latitude is sought. This entire problem can be explained through the doctrine of plane triangles according to the principles already demonstrated. For let us conceive of a right-angled triangle *aei*, where *a* is the start of the loxodrome, *ae* is the **meridian** <term>meridianus: a line of longitude used here to measure change in latitude</term> of that place, and *ai* is the loxodrome...

The point 'a' is the origin, 'e' is the top of the vertical segment, and 'i' is the top-left vertex. A horizontal line 'ei' connects them at the top. The hypotenuse 'ai' is marked with the number '50'. A horizontal line 'ou' passes through point 'a', with 'o' to the left and 'u' to the right. A vertical line extends downwards from 'a' to a point 'c'.</note>

...the distance is 50 miles, and the angle of inclination <note>original: "angulus inclinationis"; this refers to the ship's course or bearing relative to the meridian</note> is 56 degrees and 15 minutes. Since, therefore, by Proposition 17, the segment of the meridian *ae* acts as the leg of a triangle, and the loxodrome *ai* acts as the base <note>In the geometry of the time, the "base" often referred to the hypotenuse in this specific right-triangle construction</note> of the right angle *aei*, and the angle *aie* (the complement of the inclination) is given as 33 degrees 45 minutes: It will be that just as the radius 10,000,000 is to the sine of the angle *aie* (33 degrees 45 minutes), which is 5,555,702; so the loxodrome *ai* of 50 miles is to <margin>27 $\frac{77851}{100000}$</margin> 27 $\frac{77851}{100000}$. This many miles, therefore, will be the segment of the meridian *ae*. Furthermore, these are reduced to minutes <term>scrupula: minutes of a degree</term> by proportion in this way: 15 miles are 60 minutes—that is, 1 mile is worth 4 minutes—therefore 27 $\frac{77851}{100000}$ will give 111 $\frac{1140}{100000}$ minutes. And because of this, point *e*, or the parallel *ei*, will be separated to the south or to the north from the parallel *ai* by 1 degree and 51 minutes. For which reason, if point *a* is placed on the 51st parallel and *ae* is extended toward the north, the parallel *ei* will be drawn through 52 degrees 51 minutes. Conversely, if toward the south, it would now reach 49 degrees 9 minutes. And so on for the rest. Or, more elegantly and briefly in this manner:

->*As the radius is to four times the sine of the complement of the given loxodrome's inclination; so the miles of its length are to the minutes of the changed latitude.*<-

<margin>111 $\frac{1140}{100000}$</margin>
The matter is evident from the stated proportion. So as not to wander too far, I will use the same example: four times the sine of 33 degrees 45 minutes is 22,222,808; whence, according to the established terms 10,000,000, 22,222,808, and 50, you conclude the fourth term is 111 $\frac{1140}{100000}$, as above.

## PROPOSITION XXII.

<margin>* Containing the potential of the change in longitude.</margin>
*The other leg of the loxodromic triangle <note>A loxodromic triangle is a right-angled triangle used to model a path of constant bearing (a rhumb line) on a sphere.</note> is at once entire and imaginary; but it must be assigned equally through minute parts to individual parallels separated by equal intervals; for this reason it may be called the* \* <term>mēkodynamikon</term> <note>original: <term>μηκοδυναμικὸν</term>; a term coined by Snellius meaning "longitude-potential" or "the power of length."</note>.

For since, according to Proposition 17, the Rhumb Line <term>loxodromia: a path of constant bearing</term> acts as the base <note>here meaning the hypotenuse</note> of a triangle, of which one leg is a segment of the meridian equal to the distance between the parallels it intercepts, the remaining leg must also be sought by us here. That leg, however, does not exist as a single, whole piece on the spherical surface; this is because circles on a sphere are unequal, and those nearer the pole are smaller than those further away and closer to the equator.

In the case of a cylindrical helix <note>a spiral wrapped around a cylinder rather than a sphere</note> and its triangle, if the perpendicular side were divided into equal segments across the individual parallel circles, the horizontal leg of the right angle would be a circular segment proportional and equal to the others, as they are separated by equal segments of the perpendicular.

Here on the sphere, however, even if the segments of the meridian (which are like the segments of the perpendicular) are equal, and the rhumb lines (which are the bases subtended by the right angle) are equal, and therefore the remaining horizontal legs which must be attributed to the parallels ought to be equal; yet, because successive parallels (if you begin from the poles) are larger than those that came before, even the smallest segments, though they must necessarily be equal in length among themselves, will by no means be similar <note>meaning they do not represent the same number of degrees of longitude</note>. Rather, in the larger circles

<summary>Snellius introduces the concept of "longitudinal potential" (mēkodynamikon), explaining that because latitude circles shrink toward the poles, the horizontal component of a rhumb line must be calculated as a series of infinitesimal parts rather than a single line.</summary>
<keywords>rhumb line, loxodromic triangle, mēkodynamikon, longitude, latitude, spherical geometry, Willebrord Snellius, parallels, meridian</keywords>

--- Page 93 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical proof from the previous page, specifically detailing how to calculate the change in longitude (the "longitude-potential") by breaking down a ship's path into tiny, manageable segments between parallels of latitude.</meta>

<page-num>29</page-num>
<header>THE DUTCH TIPHYS BOOK I.</header>

...fewer; in smaller parallels, they will occupy more minutes. This, however, will be made entirely clearer by the proposed example. Let there be a loxodromic triangle <note>A right-angled triangle used to calculate a rhumb line—a path of constant bearing—on a sphere.</note> from the preceding proposition, *aei*, whose base *ai* <note>In this context, the "base" refers to the hypotenuse or the actual distance traveled along the rhumb line.</note> is 49 9487/10000 miles, and the other leg *ae*, which was the segment of the meridian <note>The distance measured due North or South.</note>, was given as 1 degree and 51 minutes, or 27 3/4 miles. Therefore, the remaining side, let us call it *ei*, will be found through the tables of tangents to be 41 1102/10000 miles. 

Indeed, this [side] is never found on the surface of a sphere under a single name or as one continuous line. Instead, it must be understood as being distributed into the smallest possible particles through each of the almost infinite parallels of latitude. The more triangles into which this distance is spread, the more suitable they will be for practical use. We consider it sufficient to draw parallels for every single minute from the equator to the seventieth parallel, and to outline just as many triangles; in this way, there is no need for greater assistance <note>original: συνεργείᾳ (*synergeia*), meaning mathematical effort or cooperative precision.</note>, as will be demonstrated below. 

And so, in the proposed example, there will be 111 parallels, as many as there are minutes from the start to the end of the journey. Therefore, just as many right-angled triangles should be conceived as can be formed between these parallels, such that one perpendicular leg is a single minute on the meridian, drawn through the common intersections of the rhumb line and the parallels. The remaining leg is that portion of the *mēkodynamikon* <note>A term coined by Snellius meaning "longitude-potential"; it represents the horizontal component of travel that eventually translates into change in longitude.</note> leg situated within the parallels. But, as I said, the smaller the distance between these parallels, the more accurate will be the estimation of the minutes distributed through each parallel, because these must provide us with the calculation of our longitude. Therefore, the *mēkodynamikon* leg is established through the doctrine of triangles in this manner:

## PROPOSITION XXIII.

*Loxodromic triangles are fewer by one than the number of parallels that are intercepted from the first to the last, inclusive.*

In the diagram provided, let *kx* be the first line, the equator <note>original: æquinoctialis</note>, and let *az* be a parallel of latitude drawn through the eleventh minute; let the proposed loxodrome <note>A rhumb line, or a path that maintains a constant compass heading, which appears as a spiral on a globe.</note> be *ar*. If, therefore, parallels are drawn through every single minute, as you see here—marked as *fp*, *bg*, *cd*, and the others—you will have eleven such lines. If you count the equator *kx* itself among these, there will be twelve parallels in total.

<note>A geometric diagram illustrates loxodromic triangles on a grid. A horizontal base line is labeled K, N, P, X, representing the equator. Above it, a series of parallel horizontal lines represents parallels of latitude, with the top parallel labeled 'z' and 'a'. A curved line representing a loxodrome, labeled 'ar', starts at 'a' and descends through intersection points marked with letters including e, u, i, o, l, m, f, b, c, g, d, ending at 'r'. To the left of the grid, additional points R, H, S, V and P, L, C, y, o are marked relative to the parallels and base line to show the geometric relationships used in the proof.</note>

However, there are only eleven triangles, because the vertex of the final triangle ends exactly at the parallel *az*, as you can see here <note>This is a classic "fencepost error" explanation: to create 11 segments or intervals, you need 12 markers.</note>. If you begin the calculation from somewhere other than the equator, the result will be the same, and the mathematical proportion remains consistent.

*Since, however, in the standard tables <note>original: canonicis</note> of parallels, the starting point is taken from the equator itself, and we commonly speak in a way that excludes it—such as when we say "the parallel at 1 degree and 7 minutes," <note>original: 1. gr. 7. scr. (scrupulum)</note> we mean the specific line passing through that minute—therefore, besides the equator, there would be 67 parallels drawn for every single minute. If the equator is added to these, there are 68 parallels, and thus 67 triangles. In this way, it may safely be allowed for you*

<meta>catchword: be allowed</meta>

<summary>Snellius explains the "interval principle" in navigation: when calculating a rhumb line across several parallels of latitude, the number of mathematical triangles involved is always one less than the total number of latitude lines (including the starting line).</summary>
<keywords>loxodrome, rhumb line, equator, latitude parallels, geometric proof, navigation tables, Willebrord Snell</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical proof from page 30, explaining why the number of minutes of latitude between two points equals the number of infinitesimal "loxodromic triangles" used to calculate the path of a ship sailing on a constant bearing.</meta>

<page-num>31</page-num>
<header>THE DUTCH TIPHYS BOOK I. 31</header>

it is permissible to take from the canonical tables <note>Standard mathematical or trigonometric tables used by navigators and astronomers of the period.</note> four times the number assigned to 1 degree 7 minutes <note>original: scr., abbreviation for scrupulum</note>. It will be the same if you take the starting point from somewhere other than the equator <note>original: æquinoctiali</note>.

---

### PROPOSITION XXIV.

*If the start of a loxodrome is taken from the equator, there will be as many loxodromic triangles as there are minutes by which the final parallel is distant from it.*

This is clear in the previous example; for when I assume there that the final parallel is *az*, distant by eleven minutes from the equator, the first line in the figure shown is the equator itself. However, in this common and customary way of counting parallels, the equator does not enter the count. This is because the "first parallel" is understood to be the one separated from the equator itself by an interval of one minute. Thus, it follows that the number of minutes, which serves as the index for these parallels, does not differ from the number of intercepted triangles <note>Snellius is solving a "fencepost problem": if you have 11 one-minute steps, you have 11 triangles, even if there are 12 lines involved including the starting line.</note>; rather, it is one and plainly the same.

### PROPOSITION XXV.

*In a similar latitude, the number of minutes of difference between the parallels is equal to the number of loxodromic triangles. In a dissimilar latitude, however, it is the number of their sum.*

<note>By "similar latitude," Snellius means two points on the same side of the equator (both North or both South). "Dissimilar" means one is North and one is South.</note>

Suppose now in the same diagram that *kb* is the parallel at 52 degrees, and *az* is the parallel at 52 degrees 11 minutes. Here, therefore, if you take both extremes inclusively, there are indeed twelve parallels—

<meta>The text breaks here; the catchword "cim" indicates the next page begins with "cim," completing the word "duodecim" (twelve).</meta>

<summary>Snellius establishes that the number of mathematical triangles used to calculate a rhumb line path is exactly equal to the number of minutes of latitude traveled. He explains how to count these intervals whether the ship is staying in one hemisphere or crossing the equator.</summary>

[eleven] <note>completing the word "undecim" from the previous page</note>, but there are only eleven loxodromic triangles, which is the number of the difference of minutes <note>original: scrupulorum</note> if you subtract 52 degrees 11 minutes. If the latitude is dissimilar, one being northern <note>original: borea</note> and the other southern <note>original: notia</note>, the sum of the minutes of both latitudes joined together will be the same as the number of intermediate triangles. For let *kx* again be the equator <note>original: æquinoctialis</note>, and *az* a northern parallel drawn through the eleventh minute; on the opposite side, let *ag* be a southern parallel through the fourth minute of southern latitude. Therefore, according to Proposition 23, there will be eleven triangles in the first case and four in the second; the sum is fifteen triangles, which is also the total number of minutes.

## PROPOSITION XXVI.

> *If the distance-power <term>mecodynamic <note>Greek: μηκοδυναμικὸν. A term coined by Snellius to describe the leg of the triangle representing the distance traveled along a specific bearing.</note></term> leg of a loxodromic triangle is divided by the difference of parallels spaced minute by minute in a similar latitude, how much of a part should be equally assigned to each individual triangle, from the largest inclusive to the smallest exclusive?*

In the same diagram, let the distance-power <term>mecodynamic</term> leg *kx* be 17 1/2 miles, with *k* on the equator and *a* at the 11th minute of latitude. Thus, through Proposition 23, there will be 11 loxodromic triangles here, and as many legs to be assigned to the individual parallels. Of these, the first is assigned to the equator, the second *fp* to the parallel at the first minute of latitude, the third *bg* to the parallel at the second minute, and finally the eleventh *ie* to the parallel at the tenth minute. This is done so that the last or eleventh parallel is excluded from this count, because the vertex of the newest triangle *iae* only reaches that far. Therefore, when the distance-power <term>mecodynamic</term> leg of 17 1/2 miles is divided by 11,

And thus this entire imaginary side is assigned in equal parts to each individual parallel, however many we may conceive. The result will be the same if you do not begin from the equator itself, but from any other parallel. The truth of this is also manifest from Propositions 23 and 25.

### PROPOSITION XXVII.

->*The distance-power <term>mecodynamic <note>Greek: μηκοδυναμικὸν. The leg of the loxodromic triangle representing the actual distance sailed along a constant bearing.</note></term> leg of a loxodrome crossing the equator <note>original: æquinoctialis</note> is to be distributed into its parts on both sides from the equator; in this case, the equator has a double portion, while the outermost parallels on both sides are excluded.*<-

Let this distance-power <term>mecodynamic</term> leg across the equator be *αβ*. Let *κχ* be equal to it. Therefore, the part *κκ* is to be assigned to the southern parallels <note>original: notis</note>, and *κχ* to the northern parallels <note>original: boreis</note>, according to the preceding proposition. And thus it happens that *κλ* and *κh*, as two small portions, are to be assigned to the equator, while each extreme [parallel] is here excluded by equal right.

## PROPOSITION XXX.

->*Given the change in latitude from a given parallel, along with the inclination of the loxodrome, the change in longitude will also be given.*<-

Just as we distinguished three cases in loxodromic triangles in the previous Theorems 26 and 27, we also judge it worth the effort to explain this and the following propositions with three distinct examples. Let the parallel from which the start of the <term>loxodrome <note>A path of constant bearing; on a sphere, it spirals toward the poles.</note></term> is drawn be the <term>equinoctial <note>The celestial equator or the Earth's equator.</note></term> itself; let the inclination <note>bearing</note> of the loxodrome be 56 degrees 15 minutes (inclination, however, as we defined it above, is the smaller angle contained by the loxodrome and the meridian, such as *arz* or *krl* in the diagram above); and let the change in latitude be 53 degrees 10 minutes. It is asked how much distance in longitude has been departed from the first location. 

First, by the corollary of Proposition 28, from the given inclination of the loxodrome, let the fractional mile portion to be assigned to each parallel from the equinoctial to 53 degrees 10 minutes (exclusive) be found: namely, if a quarter of the tangent of the inclination is divided by the radius, as you see here $\frac{37415}{100000}$ <note>The tangent of 56° 15' is approximately 1.4966; one quarter of this is 0.37415.</note>; then, this mile portion will be converted into the minutes <note>original: scrupula</note> owed to each parallel through Proposition 12. In this way, from the <term>canons of parallels <note>Mathematical tables, likely referring to the integration of secants used to calculate latitude-longitude relationships on a sphere, a precursor to the calculus used in Mercator projections.</note></term>, let the number be taken which is owed to 53 degrees 10 minutes, which is 37,800,747, and its quadruple, 151,202,988. ## PROPOSITION XXXI.

*Given the length of the Loxodrome with the angle of inclination, the change in longitude is given.*

The change in longitude, both above and always among geographers, is the distance between two meridians estimated in degrees and minutes. First, with the base and the oblique angle given, you must seek each leg <note>In the context of spherical trigonometry, the "legs" are the sides of the right-angled triangle formed on the globe's surface.</note>. It seems worthwhile to explain this, just like the preceding theorem, through three types of examples. Let it be first, then, that a ship has sailed from the equator <note>original: æquinoctiali.</note> for 100 miles, at an angle of inclination of the loxodrome <note>The angle of the ship's constant course relative to the meridians.</note> of 33 degrees and 45 minutes. From which, by the preceding proposition, 3 degrees, 42 minutes, and 47 seconds of change in longitude are given.

Second, let there be a navigation from the 47th parallel at an angle of inclination of the loxodrome of 33 degrees 45 minutes, for a distance of 100 miles. The quantity of the change in longitude is sought. Since by Proposition 18 the segments of the same loxodrome between equal parallels are equal, therefore by Proposition 19 the distance of the parallels is given as 332 and 50/111, or what is the same, 5 degrees 33 minutes. These, when counted from the 47th parallel, fall at 52 degrees 33 minutes. From here, the fractional part of the <term>mecodynamic <note>original: μηκοδυναμικὰ.</note></term> leg to be attributed to the individual parallels will be given by Proposition 28, namely 1670/10000. Then by Proposition 29, the minutes of change in longitude will be given. For the quadruple of the difference of the number in the <term>canonical tables <note>The "canons" or standard mathematical tables Snellius developed for these calculations.</note></term> of parallels, which pertains to 47 degrees and to 52 degrees 33 minutes, is 20,660,132; from which the proportion is found by Proposition 30. As 10,000 is to 20,660,132, so is 1670/10000 to 345 1/4 minutes. These are 5 degrees 45 minutes 7 seconds. Thus you see that with the same inclination and measure of this course, the change in longitude between these parallels is 2 degrees greater than near the equator <note>This demonstrates the convergence of the meridians: as you move toward the poles, the same eastward distance covers more degrees of longitude.</note>.

### P R O P O S I T I O XXXII.

->*Given the parallel along with the change in latitude and longitude, to find the inclination and distance of the loxodrome.*<-

In order for the inclination of the loxodrome to be found from the difference of longitude and latitude, it is absolutely necessary to record the starting latitude, and furthermore, toward which region one is departing from the given parallel—whether toward the poles or toward the equator <note>original: æquinoctialem.</note>. For depending on that starting point and direction, the inclination of the loxodrome can also vary. Let the first example be as follows: From the island of São Tomé <note>original: inſula Divi Thomæ.</note> directly on the equator to the mouth

# WILLEBRORD SNELL
<page-num>40</page-num>

The difference in longitude for the Silver River in Brazil, commonly called the *Rio de la Plata*, is established at 60 degrees, and its distance from the equinoctial line <note>The Equator.</note> is approximately 34 degrees. The question is: by what inclination of course, or loxodrome <note>The constant compass heading a ship follows to reach a destination.</note>, must one sail from the island of Saint Thomas <note>São Tomé.</note> to the mouth of the said river, and by what distance of that same course? Here, therefore, the base must be sought from the two legs of a triangle, along with the other oblique angle. For the difference in latitude is one leg of the entire loxodromic triangle; however, the remaining leg must be investigated from the difference in longitude. First, 34 degrees on the meridian will give 510 miles <note>Snellius uses the German/Dutch mile, where 1 degree equals 15 miles; 34 multiplied by 15 is 510.</note>. Then, from the difference in longitude of 60 degrees, the proportional part of the <term>mecodynamic leg <note>Snellius’s technical term for the longitudinal component of a rhumb line calculation on a sphere.</note></term> in miles to be attributed to the individual parallels will be given by the proposition as 41448/100000. This, multiplied by the number of parallels, 2040 (for that many loxodromic triangles lie between them according to Proposition 23), will give 845 5192/10000 miles. Therefore, by adding the squares of these legs—namely from 510 and 845 5192/10000— the base of 987 4191/10000 miles will also be given. And such will be the length of the said loxodrome. Finally, from the given legs, the magnitude of the angle of inclination will be given: *as the 510 miles of changed latitude are to the mecodynamic leg 845 5192/10000; so the radius is to the tangent of the angle of inclination, according to the proposition, from which the angle of the sought loxodrome itself is given as 58 degrees 54 minutes.*

Let there be another example, where both latitudes are of the same kind. The question is by which loxodrome one should strive to travel from *Faial*, one of the Flemish Islands <note>The Azores, frequently referred to as the Flemish Islands in the 17th century because of their early Flemish settlers.</note>, to the island near the Davis Strait, which they call *Bella* (as if "pretty") because of its Parian stone resembling Jasper <note>Likely referring to a location described by explorers in the Arctic or near Greenland.</note>. The latitude of both is North: the former is 38 degrees 50 minutes <note>original: scrupula (scruples), used here to mean minutes of a degree.</note>; the latter is 52 degrees 21 minutes. Let the difference in longitude be 16 degrees 12 minutes. Thus, the canonical numbers of the parallels <note>Reference to Snellius’s pre-calculated mathematical tables.</note> for each latitude will be 3699. 1192 and 2531. 2354. Whence, through Proposition 14, you conclude in this manner: 10600 45/111 minutes of longitude occupy 1 mile in each of these parallels; therefore, 5210 minutes yield 4911/10000 of a mile in each parallel.

And from these legs of the right-angled triangle, the base is given, which is the length of the loxodrome: 1381 3/22 miles. ### PROPOSITION XXXIII.

->*Given a parallel and the angle of inclination of the loxodrome,*<-
->*along with the variation of longitude, to find the quantity of*<-
->*the loxodrome and the variation of latitude.*<-

Suppose, therefore, a voyage is made from the island of São Tomé <note>original: "Divi Thomæ"</note> located under the <term>equinoctial <note>The Equator.</note></term> toward the mouth of the Silver River, or Rio de la Plata, as it is called in the Spanish language, under an angle of inclination of the loxodrome of 58 degrees 54 minutes, such that in this course a variation of longitude of 60 degrees is made. The quantity of the loxodrome along with the variation of latitude is sought. 

This must be concluded in the following manner: first, test how much ten, twenty, or thirty degrees of latitude under this inclination vary the longitude, a matter which has a very easy explanation from the corollary of the twenty-eighth proposition. For a quarter of the tangent of the given loxodromic inclination divided by the radius will give the miles of the <term>mecodynamic leg <note>Snellius’s technical term for the longitudinal distance adjusted for the Earth's curvature.</note></term> to be attributed equally to each of the individual parallels. 

For example, if an inclination of 58 degrees 54 minutes is given: for a quarter of its tangent, 165772, which is 41443, divided by the radius will give <sup>41443</sup>/<sub>100000</sub> of one mile in each parallel. If, therefore, you multiply by this number four times that value from the <term>canonical numbers <note>Values from Snellius's pre-calculated mathematical tables.</note></term> of the parallels which pertains to 10 degrees, the variation of longitude will be given—how much would occur during this course up to the parallel of the tenth degree from the Equator, from start to finish, by Proposition... <note>Snellius leaves the specific proposition number blank here, referring back to his earlier proofs on longitudinal variation.</note> namely 398,<sup>174</sup>/<sub>100000</sub> minutes. From this, a proportion of this kind shall be established: Just as the 398,<sup>174</sup>/<sub>100000</sub> minutes found are to the desired 3600 (the total [minutes in 60 degrees]...

Thus, the assumed canonical number of parallels for 10 degrees, 240.1940, [is] to the fourth number 2171,<sup>51549</sup>/<sub>100000</sub>, which you must search for entirely in the canonical tables of parallels; to which 34 degrees, 0 minutes <note>original: "scr." for scrupula</note>, and 19 seconds respond there. Wherefore, they will have been made proportional to the multipliers. Therefore, as 398,<sup>174</sup>/<sub>1000</sub> (namely the minutes of longitude found) is to its number 960,<sup>7760</sup>/<sub>100000</sub>, so are the proposed 3600 minutes to their own number, of which the quarter is the canonical value; and in the table, 2171,<sup>51549</sup>/<sub>100000</sub> points to the parallel of 34 degrees. From this, the length of the <term>loxodrome <note>A rhumb line, or a path of constant bearing.</note></term> itself will be found through the proposition of the loxodrome by the squares of the legs of the entire mecodynamic triangle <note>A reference to the Pythagorean theorem applied to nautical distance.</note>. The mecodynamic leg of longitude from the parallels of 34 degrees is given as 845,<sup>437</sup>/<sub>1000</sub>, and the miles of varied latitude are 510 <note>34 degrees multiplied by Snellius's value of 15 miles per degree equals 510 miles.</note>. From these, the length of the loxodrome itself is 987,<sup>35</sup>/<sub>100</sub> miles.

And from here you may most easily find how much the latitude changes continuously from the equator, if the same difference of longitude is assumed everywhere. For example, if it is asked how much the change in latitude is under a rhumb line inclination of 33 degrees 45 minutes and a continuous change in longitude of two degrees. Here first, just as before, you will conclude by proportion how much any parallel chosen changes under this inclination. The <term>mecodynamic leg <note>A term coined by Snellius to represent a mathematical constant for a specific sailing course relative to the earth's curvature.</note></term> in individual parallels is 18,702/100,000 miles; from there, if a change of latitude of only one degree is assumed, to which the canonical number 60.0030 corresponds, from four times that value the minutes of the changed longitude will be given by the proposition as 40 and 283/100,000. ### The Same [Problem] IN ANOTHER WAY & more easily.

The same problem will also be solved in another way, and indeed much more easily without any division, if you only consider this correctly: as the <term>radius <note>The standard length used in trigonometry to define functions like sines and tangents; here, it functions as a base value (often 10,000,000 in this era) for calculations.</note></term> is to the <term>tangent of the complement <note>original: "tangentem complementi"; what we now call the cotangent.</note></term> of the angle of a given inclination, so the <term>minutes <note>original: "scrupula"; meaning "small weights" or "scruples," used here to denote the 60 minutes that make up a degree.</note></term> of the proposed longitude are to the <term>canonical number <note>A standardized value from Snell's mathematical tables used to find a corresponding latitude.</note></term>.

*From which it is easy to explain how much is assigned to ten, a hundred, or even a thousand minutes.*

As an example, suppose the inclination of the <term>loxodromic angle <note>The constant compass bearing or rhumb line angle.</note></term> is the same as before, with the starting point derived from the <term>equinoctial <note>The Equator.</note></term> itself, namely 58 degrees 54 minutes. The tangent of its complement was 6,032,386. That number, divided by the radius, will give 6,032,386 / 1,000,000 as the canonical number belonging to a single minute; and 6,032,386 / 100,000 for 10 minutes, and 6,032,386 / 10,000 for a hundred, and so on for thousands. The reason for this is manifest from the construction and demonstration of the proposition. For since the canonical number of a given latitude, when multiplied by the <term>mecodynamic leg <note>original: "crus mecodynamicum"; a technical term Snell used for a specific trigonometric ratio—effectively the secant of the angle—that accounts for the stretching of the rhumb line across latitudes.</note></term> of the given rhumb line assigned to each parallel, yields a quarter of the minutes of the changed longitude: therefore, the quarter of the minutes of the changed longitude divided by that same mecodynamic leg will give the canonical number appropriate for its latitude. 

However, the mecodynamic leg for each parallel under a given inclination is a quarter of the tangent divided by the radius. Therefore: as the quarter of the tangent of the angle of inclination is to the radius, so is the quarter of the minutes of the changed longitude to the canonical number of the changed latitude. That is, if four times the first and third values are taken: as the tangent is to the radius, so the minutes of the changed longitude are to the canonical number of the changed latitude. But just as the tangent is to the radius; so

the radius to the <term>tangent of the complement <note>original: "tangentem complementi"; this is the cotangent of the angle.</note></term>; wherefore, by equal proportions, it will also be: 

*As the radius is to the tangent of the complement of the given angle of inclination, so are the minutes of the changed longitude to the <term>canonical number <note>A value from Snell's pre-calculated mathematical tables used to determine position on a sphere.</note></term> of the changed latitude.* 

That is, if the entire radius is understood to represent the <term>minutes <note>original: "scrupula"; literally "scruples," used here for the 60 minutes that make up a degree of arc.</note></term> of the changed longitude, then the canonical number (which would have resulted from the division of a fourth part of those same minutes by the quantity of the <term>mecodynamic leg <note>original: "cruris mecodynamici"; a technical term Snell used to describe a specific side of a right-angled triangle used in his sailing calculations, related to the secant of the latitude.</note></term>) would be the tangent of the complement of the given inclination. Therefore, if the changed longitude is assumed to be 1, 10, 100, or 1000 minutes, the mecodynamic leg corresponding to that same change will be given, from which the change in latitude will be found using the table of canonical numbers itself.

And so, from this, the solution to the proposed problem will be very easy without any division. For example, if the inclination is 58 degrees 54 minutes, and the change in longitude is 60 degrees—which is 3600 minutes—then it will be: as 10,000,000 <note>The base value for the radius in these trigonometric tables.</note> is to the tangent of the complement of 58 degrees 54 minutes—namely 6,032,386—so are the 3600 minutes of changed longitude to the canonical number of the <term>parallels <note>Lines of latitude.</note></term>, which is 2171. <gloss>658900/10000000</gloss>. Or, what amounts to the same thing (since the canonical numbers are only set out to 10,000), to 2171. 6589. In the canonical tables, this corresponds to a change in latitude of 34 degrees.

Here, the terms of the proportion will be the same as before: 10,000,000, the tangent of the complement 6,032,386, and 3600 minutes. From these, the canonical number 2171. 6589 is produced. To this must be added the canonical number for 24 degrees—namely 1484. 0097. Snell notes that while mathematically elegant, this specific problem is more difficult and less practically useful for sailors.</meta>
