<header>THE DUTCH TIPHYS BOOK I.</header>

...thus it will have been reached by that course and by that variation in longitude.

Finally, if the course had been set from the same parallel toward the equator <note>original: "equinoctialem"</note> with the same heading and change in longitude, then the <term>canonical number <note>A value from Snell’s pre-calculated mathematical tables used to determine position on a sphere.</note></term> for 24 degrees would be subtracted from the found canonical term 2171.6589; this will give a remainder of 687.5462, which corresponds to 11 degrees 23 minutes. And so, by this course from the 24th northern parallel, one would reach the 11 degrees 23 minutes southern parallel. This method is much swifter than the previous one; for while the former required the double labor of multiplication and division, here the whole business is completed by multiplication alone.

### PROPOSITION XXXIII.

->*Given the parallel, the variation in longitude, and the measure of the loxodrome; to find its inclination and the variation in latitude.*<-

Although both this and the preceding problem are of little importance for practical nautical use, I have decided to propose both and illustrate them with an example to demonstrate the integrity and perfection of our art as clearly as possible. This latest problem, however, involves somewhat more difficulty. Indeed, Robert Hues—who explains these problems we have proposed only for those seven <term>loxodromes <note>Lines on a sphere that cross all meridians at the same angle; also known as rhumb lines.</note></term> which are described on a spherical surface, and does so only mechanically and quite roughly <note>original: "ὁλοχερῶς"</note>—ascribes this praise to this latest challenge: "There is nothing," he says, "in this entire art which is more difficult to find than the Rhumb" (as he calls the loxodrome, following Nunes <note>original: "Nonium"; Pedro Nunes was a Portuguese mathematician who first defined the loxodrome.</note>) "from a given interval and difference in longitude, nor by any other means than..."

,, can be sought through the use of globes by a laborious and frequently repeated practice and many measurements. And since this practice is so long-winded and involves such great labor, it is less necessary, or even utterly useless, because the difference in longitude is investigated with difficulty, as we warned above. Would that our great boasters <note>Snell is likely referring to contemporary navigators or rival mathematicians who made grand claims without mathematical proof.</note> might provide the discovery of this, so that it might be possible at last to expect something from them besides naked words, vain promises, and empty hope. 

And so says he <note>Referring to Robert Hues, whom Snell quoted on the previous page.</note>; even though he himself exhibits or requires almost nothing in nearly everything besides a crude model of the construction <note>original: "factiomis typum"</note>, and one which, however suitable it may be for expressing the properties and characteristics of loxodromes <note>A loxodrome, or rhumb line, is a path with a constant compass bearing, which appears as a spiral on a globe but a straight line on a Mercator projection.</note>, has little or no weight in actual use. We shall here demonstrate the way of finding the inclinations of the loxodromes even down to the first minutes <note>original: "scrupula prima"</note> and their fractions as well, if anyone should so desire. 

Let us assume, then, that the start of the set course is from the equator itself <note>original: "æquinoctiali"</note>, such that the length of the loxodrome is 800 miles, and the change in longitude is 50 degrees 19 minutes. The question is the angle of inclination for this loxodrome, and the change in latitude for the same, resulting from this longitude and simultaneously the magnitude of the loxodrome. 

Here, before all else, a test must be made as to whether the navigation was done under the same parallel, or indeed if the course was set along some loxodrome. For if the miles of the proposed course, divided by the miles of one degree in the parallel from which the start was made, show the same quantity of longitude variation, then truly the matter speaks for itself: the course was set under that same parallel, and there has been no change in latitude. For by the eleventh proposition, as the radius is to four times the secant of the given parallel, so too is the number of miles to the minutes <note>original: "scrupula"</note> corresponding to them in that parallel. Therefore, if the journey is set under the equator, the number of miles quadrupled will give the minutes belonging to them. For example,

<sig>G 2</sig>
<meta>Catchword: 8260.</meta>

here 800 miles <note>original: "miliaria". In Snell's system, 1 Dutch mile equals 4 minutes of arc along a great circle.</note> would make 3,200 minutes <note>original: "scrupula"</note>, which are 53 degrees and 20 minutes. But the change in longitude <term>change in longitude: original "evariatio longitudinis", the difference in meridians between the start and end points</term> set forth is 50 degrees and 19 minutes. From this it is established that the course follows some <term>loxodrome: a path of constant compass bearing that crosses all meridians at the same angle</term> inclined along smaller parallels of latitude. It remains, therefore, that we aim for the desired target by trial and conjecture, first assuming any loxodrome—for instance, an inclination of 45 degrees—so that it may be clear whether the sought-after inclination reaches above or below this value. This is easily done. For as it is performed through the second procedure of the preceding proposition: as the Radius <note>The "Radius" in early trigonometry refers to the total sine (Sin 90°), used as the base of the proportion.</note> is to the tangent of the complement of the inclination <note>original: "tangentem complementi", which we now call the cotangent.</note>, so are the minutes of the change in longitude to the **canonical number** <term>canonical number: a value derived from tables used to calculate distances and positions on a sphere</term>; from which the change in latitude may also be known. 

In this case, however, the radius and the tangent of the complement are equal <note>Since the tangent of 45° and its complement 45° are both 1, the ratio is 1:1.</note>: thus the minutes of the given longitude will also be equal to the canonical number, which are here 3,019. Furthermore, these being reduced to the base of 10,000 (just as the canonical numbers are expressed), they become 3019.0000. To this corresponds 44 degrees and 52 1/2 minutes of latitude, and from there, under this inclination, the loxodromic miles are given as 867 and 722/11111, which is more than the given amount, for those are only 800. Therefore, that is evidence enough that the angle of inclination is greater than the assumed loxodrome. And conversely, if the found loxodrome were greater than the given one, then it would in truth be less than the one assumed. 

For which reason, another and indeed larger inclination of 60 degrees should be assumed. From here, by the same path through the second operation of the preceding proposition, the canonical number will be given as 1743.0205, to which 27 degrees and 52 16/100 minutes of change in longitude are assigned; and from there, the length of the loxodrome is 811 4100/10000 miles. This is still larger than the proposed 800. Therefore, I shall again assume a larger inclination by 10 degrees, namely of 70 degrees (for by this increase you will first enclose that inclination within ten degrees without very much labor), from which the canonical number will come out to be 1098.

whereupon, by cross-multiplication <note>original: *multiplicatione ad decussim*. This refers to a method of linear interpolation used to find an unknown value between two known test points.</note> being performed, the numbers 2 <sup>961556974</sup>/<sub>1000000000</sub> and 1 <sup>418095503</sup>/<sub>1000000000</sub> will be given. And because the signs are different <note>referring to the positive and negative results from the previous trial calculations</note>, let addition be performed. From this will be given the divisor 7 <sup>2821</sup>/<sub>100000</sub> and the dividend 4 <sup>379652477</sup>/<sub>1000000000</sub>. When this division has been completed and reduced to its thousandths, the quotient will be <sup>60143</sup>/<sub>100000</sub> for the miles of *changed longitude* <note>original: *evariatæ longitudinis*</note> in each parallel. Four times this value, 240572, is the tangent of the inclination according to the corollary of proposition 28. From this, the angle of the sought inclination itself is given as 67 degrees and 26 minutes <note>original: *scr.*, an abbreviation for *scrupula*, meaning minutes of a degree.</note>. Furthermore, from here will be given the **canonical number** <term>canonical number: a value Snell derives from his tables to relate longitude and latitude on a rhumb line</term> 1254.6360, and the *change in latitude* 20 degrees and 27 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>3</sub> minutes, and the length of the *loxodrome* <note>original: *loxodromiæ*, the rhumb line or path of constant bearing</note> as 799 <sup>116</sup>/<sub>1000</sub> miles. Since it ought to be 800, the inclination will therefore be a few minutes greater: namely, 67 degrees and 30 minutes exactly, which a repeated calculation will display as the result. The same path holds for the rest, if by chance the beginning of the loxodrome is not taken from the *equator* <note>original: *æquinoctiali*</note> itself. And so, let this be the end of this theory, and likewise of the first book.

<meta>This page marks the beginning of Book II. In Book I, Snellius established the mathematical foundations of loxodromic curves (rhumb lines). He now moves from theory to "praxis" (practice), focusing on how these mathematical models apply to actual navigation and the correction of errors in a ship's recorded course.</meta>

# WILLEBRORD SNELL
# THE ART OF SAILING <note>original: *Histiodromices*, a Greek-derived term for the study of a ship's course under sail.</note>
## BOOK TWO,
# ON ITS PRACTICE:
### OR, ON THE
### *Correction of Nautical Deviation.*

Thus far we have examined all the properties of rhumb lines <term>loxodromia: a path of constant bearing that crosses all meridians at the same angle; on a flat map it is a straight line, but on a globe it is a spiral</term>, and have contemplated them accurately, so that we may explain and follow their inclinations through degrees, minutes <note>original: *scrupula*, literally "scruples," used here to denote the 60 minutes of a degree</note>, and other such smallest particles. Their practical use follows; so that we may next see how much maritime practice grants us, and how much faith is to be placed in it. And first, indeed, we shall treat the number of rhumb lines commonly accepted by sailors.

### PROPOSITION I.

> *The principal rhumb lines in each quadrant are arranged so that seven fall between the meridian and the local parallel; these divide the right angle into eight equal parts, which are themselves further subdivided into halves and quarters.*

<note>A decorative woodcut initial letter 'L' depicts a landscape scene with a prominent column and buildings within a square frame.</note>
It is known from the principles of spherical geometry that the parallel of any location is cut at a right angle <note>original: *normaliter*, meaning perpendicularly</note> by its meridian; and by this intersection, the regions of the world are separated into four quadrants, as stated in Book 1, proposition...

<meta>Following the introduction to Book II, Snellius continues discussing the geometric representation of the four cardinal directions and how they are projected from a sphere onto a plane. He is moving from the general principles of the meridian and parallel to the specific division of the compass rose into "rhumbs."</meta>

# THE DUTCH TIPHYS, BOOK II.

...demonstrated in proposition 7. Namely, that north and south are designated by the meridian, while east and west are designated by the parallel. This is usually expressed on a plane by straight lines intersecting each other perpendicularly <note>original: *normaliter*, meaning at a 90-degree angle</note>; these lines act as tangents to these circles at their common intersection. So that if the meridian line of a given place is *eo*, and its primary vertical line is *ui*: then *e* faces north, *o* south, *i* east, and *u* west. Here, following the path of line *oe*, you will travel along the meridian and thereby describe a "great circle" <note>A great circle is the largest possible circle that can be drawn on a sphere, like the equator or a meridian; it represents the shortest distance between two points.</note> from south to north, or vice versa. If, however, you follow line *ui*, and point *a* is directly under the equinoctial <note>The celestial equator.</note>, then the continued line *ui* will designate the Earth's equator; or if it is elsewhere in the position of an oblique sphere <note>A view of the world where the poles are at an angle to the horizon, typical for anyone living between the equator and the poles.</note>, it will designate some circle parallel to it. And so each of these courses designates a circle for us, according to propositions six and seven of the first book; however, the intermediate angles in each quadrant [do] not

<note>A circular diagram representing a compass rose or the projection of celestial and terrestrial coordinates onto a flat plane. A vertical axis is labeled 'e' (North) at the top and 'o' (South) at the bottom. A horizontal axis is labeled 'u' (West) on the left and 'i' (East) on the right. The circle is divided into many radial segments representing different headings. The upper-right quadrant is marked with points 'a', 'y', 's', 'r', 'x', 'z', and Greek letters like 'β' and 'η'. Other quadrants feature various Greek and Roman letters marking points on the circumference (e.g., ε, ζ, η, θ, λ, μ, ν, ξ, π, ρ, σ, τ, υ, φ), illustrating the mathematical division of a ship's potential courses.</note>

likewise, according to proposition 15. Those skilled in maritime affairs commonly divide these for naval use into eight equal parts, so that the angles of inclination—starting from the meridian—are separated from one another by an interval of 11 degrees and 15 minutes <note>original: *scrupula*, literally "scruples," a term used in this period to denote the 60 minutes that make up a degree.</note>. As seen here in the sections *eay*, *yas*, *sar*, and so forth. And thus, in each quadrant, eight courses are established, so that the first is distant from the meridian by an interval of 11° 15'. Of these, the first seven are loxodromic lines <term>loxodromia: also known as a rhumb line; a path that crosses all meridians at the same angle, allowing a sailor to maintain a constant compass heading.</term>, while the eighth, which is drawn through *ui*, is the parallel of the place itself. The angle of inclination for the first loxodrome is *eay* at 11° 15'; for the second, *eas* at 22° 30'; for the third, *ear* at 33° 45'; for the fourth, 45° 0'; for the fifth, 56° 15'; and for the sixth, 67° 30'.

<meta>This page continues Snellius's technical discussion of the division of the compass (rhumbs) and introduces the mathematical tables used to calculate distances and latitude changes when sailing along these lines. It follows the definition of cardinal directions from page 55.</meta>

WILLEBRORD SNELL

...of the seventh [rhumb], 78 degrees 45 minutes <note>original: *scr.*, abbreviation for *scrupula*, meaning minutes of a degree</note>. However, the eighth course, which completes the ninetieth degree, is circular <note>original Greek: *κυκλοειδής* (kykloeidēs), meaning "circle-like." This refers to the path following a parallel of latitude rather than crossing meridians at an angle.</note>. And thus the view of the whole world is divided through four quadrants into thirty-two directions <note>original: *plagas*, referring to the 32 points of the compass or "winds."</note>. Sailors have their own terms by which they call each direction according to the custom of their nation; they express these nomenclatures in their individual native languages. For us, however, in this art it will be enough to indicate them by their order and succession: the first, second, third, and so on, just as I expressed a little earlier. See the common names and titles, and how we might achieve the same elegance through Greek terminology, in the schools <note>This likely refers to Snellius's lectures or standard academic curriculum at the University of Leiden.</note>.

But this eight-fold division of a single quadrant is not sufficient for those who are more precise; instead, it is further bisected by some, and even divided into four by the most accurate, so that there are 32 courses in each quadrant and 128 in the whole circle. However, they explain these only as quarters, halves, and three-quarters <note>original: *dodrantes*, a Roman term for three-fourths</note> of the directions, and they do not designate them by any new names, so that the complexity of the division does not burden the memory. There follows the arrangement and construction of the tables <note>original: *canonum*, referring here to mathematical tables or "canons" of navigation.</note> for these inclinations and the fractional parts of the inclinations.

*Ready-to-use <note>original Greek: *πρόχειροι* (procheiroi), meaning "at hand" or "handy."</note> loxodromic tables show, for the customary inclinations, the variation in latitude, as well as the length of the rhumb line itself and of the longitudinal leg <note>original: *cruris mecodynamici*; Snell uses the term "mecodynamic" to refer to the length of the meridian intercepted by the rhumb line.</note> assigned to it, expressed in miles in the corresponding column.*

<meta>This page continues Snellius’s mathematical exposition on how to calculate travel along a rhumb line. He is moving from general definitions to specific trigonometric rules (propositions) and introducing his "Handy Canons"—pre-calculated tables designed to save sailors from difficult manual calculations.</meta>

->THE DUTCH TIPHYS BOOK II.<-

### H

...when the total distance of the course between set parallels is found using the angle of the course’s inclination. For example, if an interval of latitude is 40 degrees and the inclination of the rhumb line <note>original: *loxodromiæ*. A rhumb line is a path with a constant compass bearing, which spirals toward the poles on a sphere.</note> is 60 degrees, it will follow that: *as the radius is to the secant of the angle of inclination, so the minutes of the change in latitude are to the miles of the proposed rhumb line.* <note>In modern trigonometry, this is expressed as: Distance = Latitude Change / Cosine(Bearing). Snellius uses the secant, which is the reciprocal of the cosine.</note> Namely, as 100,000 is to 50,000, so are 40 degrees (that is, 2,400 minutes <note>original: *scrupula*, literally "scruples," used here to mean minutes of a degree.</note>) to 1,200 miles, which is a very accurate quantity for the rhumb line.

Furthermore, the length of the mecodynamic leg <note>original: *cruris mecodynamici*. This is a term Snellius coined from the Greek words for "length" and "power," referring to the "departure" or the east-west distance gained relative to the change in latitude.</note> will be found through Proposition 27. *For as the radius is to the tangent of the given rhumb line, so the minutes of the changed latitude are to the miles of the mecodynamic leg.* For instance, just as the radius of 100,000 is to the tangent of 60 degrees (43,301); so are the minutes of the changed latitude to the mecodynamic miles, 1,039 $\frac{224}{1000}$. Conversely, if the miles of the rhumb course are given, the change of latitude in degrees and minutes will be given along with the length of the mecodynamic leg through Proposition 19 and [onward]. 

But so that those who are less accustomed to handling these matters may be relieved of the tedium of tables, and so that it may be more convenient for daily use, it seemed best to present these numbers calculated for the customary rhumb lines and their halves and quarters. Because these ought to be always ready and at hand for navigators <note>original: *navarchis*, referring to ship masters or captains.</note>, we have named them the *Handy Canons* <note>original: *Canonas πρόχειρος* (procheiros). Snellius is referencing Ptolemy’s famous "Handy Tables" (*Procheiroi Kanones*), signaling that these are practical reference charts for use at sea.</note>. The order and arrangement of these is as follows: the difference of the angle of inclination increases by quarters of one-eighth of a right angle. Since the primary rhumb lines are separated from each other by an interval of 11 degrees and 15 minutes, we present these canons separated by quarters of this interval—namely, 2 degrees, 48 minutes, and 45 seconds. Therefore, the first canon is titled $\frac{1}{4}$; the second, whose inclination is 5 degrees, 37 minutes, and 30 seconds, is titled $\frac{2}{4}$; the third is $\frac{3}{4}$, whose inclination is 8 degrees, 26 minutes, and 15 seconds; and the fourth is titled 1, or the first rhumb line, whose angle of inclination is 11 degrees, 15 [minutes].

We have arranged these tables so that we might express even the smallest fractions of a naval course that are noted in practice. The arrangement of all of them is identical, so that when you understand one, you understand them all. For our example, let us take the first Table, titled "1/4," on page 30. In the first column <note>original: *selide*. From the Greek *selis*, meaning a row or column of text.</note> you have the changes in latitude for every single degree; then from the tenth degree by tens, up to and including the eightieth degree. Opposite this, in the second column, are the rhumb miles <note>original: *milliaria loxodromica*. This is the actual distance traveled along the slanted path of the ship.</note> assigned to that course for one degree: namely 15.0180, which is 15 and $\frac{180}{10,000}$ miles. Indeed, we have calculated the fractions of miles to the ten-thousandth place. Then, in the third column, is the quantity of mecodynamic miles <note>original: *miliarium mecodynamicorum*. Snellius's term for "departure," the east-west distance gained. See previous page.</note> under its own heading: namely 7.3690, or 7 and $\frac{3690}{10,000}$ miles. The table continues in this way for what corresponds to two, three, ten, and twenty degrees. Then in the fourth, fifth, sixth, seventh, eighth, and ninth columns, it shows what is due for 1, 2, 3, 4, and 5 minutes <note>original: *scrupulis*. Literally "scruples," referring here to arc-minutes.</note>...

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for using Snellius's mathematical tables. On the previous page, Snellius introduced the layout of his data; here, he explains that he has moved the full tables to the end of the book and justifies why he uses standard 32-point compass bearings instead of infinite, precise angles, citing the practical limitations of 17th-century navigation.</meta>

THE DUTCH TIPHYS, BOOK II.

H 2

...and so on for the rest up to 60. The structure <note>original: *syntaxis*</note> of all of them is similar. Moreover, we have moved the tables themselves <note>original: *canonas*. In this context, "canons" refers to mathematical tables or rules of calculation.</note> to the end of the book and arranged them after the tables for parallels. Their use is quite easy once the basic principle <note>original: *archēn*</note> is understood.

### PROPOSITION III.

*From the inclination and magnitude of the standard rhumb lines <note>original: *loxodromiarum*. A rhumb line is a path of constant bearing on a sphere, which appears as a straight line on a Mercator projection.</note>, the amount of the change in latitude, and the length of the longitudinal leg <note>original: *μηκοδυναμικῆς* (mēkodynamikēs). This Greek term refers to the "power" or distance of longitude, essentially the East-West component of a ship's travel.</note> in miles, to readily <note>original: *προχείρως* (procheirōs)</note> present the remaining two from the tables when one value is given.*

These things concern practice and ease of use. For in the first book, we pursued those matters in great detail and at length, down to the smallest fractions. Now, we follow only those boundaries and angles that custom has accepted. Since these are comprised of a fixed number, we have assembled just as many tables, so that when one value is given, the rest are in view. On account of this prompt ease, we call these "Handy Tables" <note>original: *canones πρόχειροι* (procheiroi)</note>, because they ought to be always before one's eyes and in one's hands here. For although (as we have already said quite often) the number of rhumb lines is infinite, and their angle can be varied through every single minute and part of a minute; nevertheless, since neither the fallibility of the magnetic needle <note>original: *magneticæ acus*</note> nor the tossing of the sea allows for a precise estimation of angles in degrees and minutes: therefore, for practical use, it is judged sufficient—at least as much precision <note>original: *ἀκριβείαν* (akribeian)</note> as this matter permits—if each rhumb line is separated by an interval of 2 degrees and 48 3/4 minutes. For in this way, the entire quadrant <note>90 degrees of a circle.</note> is divided into thirty-two parts. And thus, for each one [we provide] its own proper tables...

<meta>catchword: de-</meta>

<summary>Snellius introduces his "Handy Tables" for navigation, explaining that he uses 32 standard compass points (rhumbs) because the instability of ships and compasses makes more precise measurements impractical.</summary>
<keywords>rhumb lines, loxodromes, Handy Tables, maritime navigation, compass points, latitude, longitude, mathematical precision, magnetic needle</keywords>

--- Page 124 ---
<meta>This page continues the practical instructions for using Snellius’s "Handy Tables" to solve navigation problems. The previous page introduced Proposition III, which focuses on finding latitude changes and longitudinal progress using the inclination (angle) and the distance traveled along a rhumb line.</meta>

->WILLEBRORD SNELLIUS<-

...we have assigned, according to the rules expressed in the preceding proposition. Therefore, their use will be readily available. For example, if it is asked regarding the angle of the seventh rhumb line <note>original: *loxodromiæ septimæ*. In the 32-point compass system, the 7th rhumb line or "point" is an angle of 78.75 degrees from the meridian.</note>, between parallels separated by 50 minutes <note>original: *scrupulis*. A "scruple" is a historical term for a minute of arc (1/60th of a degree).</note>, how much of the journey has been completed, and how much the mecodynamic leg <note>original: *crus mecodynamicum*. This is Snellius's term for the East-West distance covered, effectively the change in longitude relative to the distance traveled.</note> amounts to when estimated in miles. Look, therefore, in these tables under the seventh rhumb line for the fiftieth minute; there, in that row within the first column, the sought-after quantity of the rhumb line will be given as 64.0729 miles, or $64\frac{729}{10000}$; and the mecodynamic leg as $62\frac{8501}{10000}$.

If the specific number for the difference in latitude is not given under a single heading in the loxodromic tables, it must be introduced in parts. Suppose the quantity of the rhumb line and the mecodynamic leg is sought between parallels separated by 27 degrees and 35 minutes. The calculation <note>original: *inductio*</note> will be made in parts, in this manner:

| | rhumb distance | mecodynamic leg |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 20 degrees | 701. 6639 | 634. 2667 |
| 7 degrees | 245. 5824 | 222. 0038 |
| 35 minutes | 20. 4652 | 18. 5003 |
| **Sum** | 967. 7115 | 874. 8008 |

When these are added into one total sum, they yield $967\frac{7115}{10000}$ loxodromic miles, and $874\frac{8008}{10000}$ mecodynamic miles.

Conversely, if the inclination of the rhumb line and its quantity estimated in miles are given, the variation in latitude, along with the mecodynamic miles, will be provided by these handy <note>original: *prochîris*. From the Greek *procheiros*, meaning "ready at hand" or "convenient."</note> tables. Suppose a ship has sailed at a rhumb line inclination angle of $5 \frac{1}{2}$ <note>This refers to a bearing between the 5th and 6th compass points.</note>, and the length of the course is estimated at $967\frac{7115}{10000}$ miles. And suppose the distance of the parallels from the start to the end of the course is sought—that is, the variation in latitude. This process of subtraction...

# THE DUTCH TIPHYS, BOOK II.

The reverse process will be completed in parts, because the whole is not given at once. In this manner:

->Rhumb Distance<-
---
967. 7115 <note>The total distance from the previous page's sum.</note>
701. 6639  20 } degrees. <note>Subtracting the distance corresponding to 20 degrees of latitude change.</note>
---

245. 5824  7  } <note>Subtracting the distance corresponding to 7 degrees of latitude change.</note>
---
 20. 4652  35 } minutes <note>original: *scrupula*. The remainder matches the distance for 35 minutes.</note>

From here, the quantity of the mecodynamic leg <term>mecodynamic leg: Snellius’s term for the departure, or the East-West distance covered, which helps determine change in longitude.</term> will be given, as before. But if the quantity of the rhumb line <note>original: *loxodromia*</note> is not found exactly in the minutes themselves, there will be a need for a proportional part, as is customary, if perhaps the difference is of such importance that you do not wish to simply take the nearest neighboring minute. 

This will be of significant importance in more oblique courses <note>meaning courses that run closer to East-West than North-South.</note>, where a non-negligible part of the rhumb line is intercepted between parallels separated by only a single minute. Consequently, the part of the mecodynamic leg will also be quite large; because of this, the estimation of the changed longitude itself would stand apart from the correct <note>original: *legittima*</note> value: especially indeed, if it must be estimated in narrower parallels <note>parallels of latitude closer to the poles, where degrees of longitude represent smaller physical distances.</note>. In those regions, one or two miles would lead the longitude away from its correct position by no small amount. 

But these things are not to be investigated too scrupulously <note>Snellius makes a mathematical pun here: the word "scrupulously" shares a root with *scrupulum* (minute).</note> where it is not necessary; or on the contrary, they should not be held too negligently when they are of some importance. I will go before you with a single example. Under the 7 1/2 rhumb line <note>An angle of approximately 84.375 degrees from the meridian, very close to due East or West.</note>, the length of the course is 82 and 8375/10000 miles. Here, from the handy tables <note>original: *canonibus prochîris*</note>, the change of latitude <note>original: *evariatio latitudinis*</note> is given as 0 degrees, 32 minutes, in this manner:

82. 8375 } 35 minutes <note>The distance for 35 minutes of latitude on this rhumb.</note>
81. 6183 } <note>The distance for 32 minutes.</note>

WILLEBRORD SNELLIUS

And there remain 1 $\frac{2192}{25505}$ miles. From which the proportion: since 25,505 gives 60 seconds, therefore 1,2192 will give 29 seconds. I am accustomed to reducing these to 100th parts of a minute <note>original: *scrupula*. Early modern mathematicians often used decimal fractions of minutes for greater precision in calculation.</note>, namely as 25,505 is to 100, so 1,2192 is to 48. Therefore, the true variation of latitude will be 0 degrees 35 $\frac{48}{100}$ minutes. From this, the quantity of the mecodynamic leg <term>mecodynamic leg: Snellius’s term for the departure, the East-West distance covered along a rhumb line, which he uses to calculate change in longitude.</term> will be given.

->81. 2253 } 35 minutes<-
->1. 2183 } $\frac{48}{100}$<-
---
->82. 4436<-

You see, therefore, that more than 1 $\frac{1}{12}$ miles is added to the mecodynamic leg; which, just as they vary the longitude by 5 minutes at the equator itself, so at the sixtieth parallel they make nearly 10 minutes, and even more further beyond. <note>Snellius is noting how the convergence of meridians toward the poles means that the same East-West distance results in a much larger change in longitude as one moves away from the equator.</note> I thought it best to mention these things only in passing. According to the fairness of your judgment, you should estimate the importance of the matter in its proper place, and in moderation.

### NOTE.

The use of the mecodynamic leg is rare, or entirely nonexistent, in single courses where you wish to find the required longitude from individual segments, as sailors are daily accustomed to do at sea. For there, a segment of the mecodynamic leg is sufficient for finding the longitude corresponding to a single parallel—unless, while keeping the quantity of the mecodynamic leg the same, you introduce a different change in latitude from the one given. Or, when you want the longitude to consist of several diverse courses, and indeed calculated only once at the very end, there it will necessarily be used. Just as we stated those things generally in Book 1. But first, we must explain the formula for the nautical journal <note>original: *diarium nauticum*; the logbook used by sailors to track their daily progress.</note>, in which the estimated quantity of the courses

## PROPOSITION IV.

### *To organize a nautical travel ephemeris.*

Nautical logbooks <note>original: *Diaria nautica*. A daily record of a ship's progress.</note> are diligently and accurately written every day by skilled and careful masters of ships, and are divided into their pages and tables <note>original: *selides ac laterculos*. Snellius uses classical terms for the columns and rows of a data table.</note>, so that they may carefully record from them the length of the completed course, the variation <note>Referring to magnetic declination, the difference between true north and magnetic north.</note>, the inclination, the latitude observed from the sky, the blowing and force of the winds, the state of the weather, and any other matters which it is useful to note more accurately, such as the sighting of capes or islands. They do this in almost the same manner which you see partially expressed here as an example: so that each page has its own title at the head, and the tables correspond to the days of the months of each year in which these journeys were completed by them. For in this way, it is very easy for them to reread the distances of the completed journey—if perhaps a mistake was made anywhere—and to mark off and correct them on their hydrographic charts <term>hydrographic charts: Nautical maps used by sailors to navigate the oceans, showing coastlines, depths, and rhumb lines.</term>.

<meta>catchword: TABEL- (Table)</meta>

<summary>Snellius describes the standard practice and necessity of maintaining a detailed nautical logbook (ephemeris) to record daily navigation data, weather, and landmarks. This record-keeping allows mariners to cross-reference their calculations and correct their positions on maritime charts.</summary>
<keywords>logbook, navigation, ephemeris, nautical charts, latitude, magnetic variation, record keeping</keywords>

Literally "The Head of the End of the Earth," the westernmost point of Galicia, Spain.</note>, which extends furthest to the west on the northern coast. To Ptolemy, it was known as the *Nerium promontory*.

Snellius uses this term, derived from the Greek word for "page" or "column," to refer to these specific tables of navigational data.</note>; let those who wish add more, just as we advised at the beginning. We have proposed these only as a model <note>original: *ὡς ἐν τύπῳ* (hōs en typō)</note>, as they are the most necessary and should by no means be neglected. For in those longer sea voyages, on which they travel to both the East and West Indies, it will be most advantageous to also provide a specific chart for the magnetic needle's variation <note>original: *χαλυβόκλισι* (chalyboklisi). A Greek-derived term meaning "the bending of the steel," referring to the magnetic declination or the angle by which a compass needle deviates from true north.</note>. ...is determined, from which they can more easily form an estimate of the reasons of their course. For when they have found the same magnetic declination <note>original: *chalyboclisin*. Snellius uses this term to refer to the "leaning of the steel," or the variation of the compass needle from true north.</note> under the same parallel of latitude, they also become more certain of the position of the longitude and the meridian. But this subject has been handled by others; and it provided our countryman Stevin <note>Simon Stevin (1548–1620), a Flemish mathematician and engineer whose work on navigation was pioneering.</note> with the occasion for writing his *Haven-Finding Art* <note>original: *Limeneuretices*. From the Greek *limen* (harbor) and *heuriskein* (to find). This was Stevin's method for determining a ship's position using magnetic variation.</note>. See his entry in the work *Mathematical Memoirs* of the Illustrious Prince Maurice, which was formerly translated into Latin and published by us.

## PROPOSITION V.

->*A maritime course is simple, or composite.*<-

Let this be understood regarding all nautical courses examined according to the magnetic needle. For one either runs by the same path from the beginning to the end; or by several and various paths mixed among themselves. We have distinguished them in this way so that a proper order may be maintained in treating the improvement of navigation toward which we are striving.

## PROPOSITION VI.

->*A simple course is one that follows a single and the same path of its direction.*<-

As if one goes straight from north to south; or runs along the same parallel of latitude; or finally, if you direct the course according to one and the same rhumb line <note>original: *loxodromiam*. A loxodrome or rhumb line is a path with a constant bearing relative to true north, appearing as a straight line on a Mercator projection but as a spiral on a globe.</note>.

<summary>Snellius explains how magnetic variation assists in determining longitude and introduces the definitions of simple and composite maritime courses, citing Simon Stevin’s navigational theories.</summary>
<keywords>magnetic declination, Simon Stevin, Haven-Finding Art, Prince Maurice, maritime course, loxodrome, rhumb line, longitude</keywords>

## N O T E.

Just as we have accurately set forth in the first book how the change <note>original: *evariatio*. Referring to the difference in coordinates between two points.</note> in latitude or longitude may be found from the given distance of individual courses. And first indeed:

1. How, given the distance of a course from north to south, the change in latitude is given (for here there is no change in longitude), in Proposition 6. *Namely, 1 mile makes 4 minutes, and 15 miles make one degree.* <note>Snellius uses the German/Dutch league or mile, where 15 miles equals one degree of the Earth's circumference.</note>
2. How, given the distance of a course under the same parallel <note>i.e., traveling due east or west along a line of latitude.</note>, the change in longitude is given (for here there is no change in latitude), in Proposition 11. *Namely, As the radius is to four times the secant of the given parallel, so any number of given miles is to the number of minutes of the changed longitude.*
3. How, given the inclination <note>The angle of the course relative to the meridian.</note> and the distance of the <term>loxodromic</term> <note>A loxodrome, or rhumb line, is a path that crosses all meridians of longitude at the same angle; it appears as a straight line on a Mercator projection but a spiral on a globe.</note> course, the change in latitude and longitude is found; in Proposition 19 of the first book concerning the change of latitude, and in Proposition 30 concerning the change of longitude. *Namely, As the radius is to a quarter of the tangent of the loxodromy, so the canonical number <note>A mathematical value derived from the latitude, used here as a precursor to what navigators would later call "meridional parts."</note> assigned to the interval of the changed latitude is to the minutes of the changed longitude.*

This is the first method in loxodromics; the other is also very easy to explain <term>readily</term> <note>original: *προχείρως* (procheirōs). A Greek term meaning "at hand" or "conveniently."</note> through mathematical tables. And these things are indeed derived from the observation of the inclination of the <term>magnetic needle</term> <note>The compass.</note> and the estimation of the distance traveled; there follows the comparison of both with celestial observation.

### PROPOSITION VII.

->*Both the course and the estimated quantity of its direction and inclination are verified by the latitude of the place observed from the heavens, or are corrected according to it.*<-

When shipmasters on the tossing sea have most diligently observed the speed of their course and the position of its direction <note>original: *plagae*. This refers to the "quarter" or point of the compass.</note>—which is sometimes set upon the meridian itself, or, as is most frequent, forms some angle with the meridian—yet experience has proven that they are very often deceived in this estimation. The test of this error <note>original: *hallucinationis*. Snellius uses this term to describe the inherent inaccuracy or "illusion" of dead reckoning without celestial checks.</note> is the agreement or discrepancy between the parallel of latitude estimated from the course and the <term>loxodrome</term> <note>A loxodrome, or rhumb line, is a path with a constant compass bearing that spirals toward the poles.</note>, and the latitude of the place actually observed from the heavens. For when the observation, which they derive from the sun or the stars, shows a different parallel than the one they themselves deduce from the estimated path of the journey (whether circular or loxodromic), it is necessary that an error persists somewhere: namely, either in the length of the estimated journey, or in the direction of the course and the loxodromic angle, or finally in the celestial observation itself. 

But since these celestial observations can be repeated daily—and "day teaches day"—whereas the previous legs of a journey cannot be retraced, they have rightly chosen celestial observation as the standard to which the others must be adjusted, and as a less deceptive guide. Indeed, the agreement of all these factors ought to be so close that, given any two, the third is consistently determined: namely, so that from a given starting point and a given distance of the course, the latitude of the place is concluded; or from the inclination <note>The angle of the ship's path relative to the north-south meridian.</note> and the change <note>original: *e variatione*.</note> in latitude, the distance of the course itself; or from the change in latitude and the distance of the course, its inclination. But for these to agree perfectly <note>original: *ad amussim*. Literally "by the mason's rule," meaning with precision.</note>, it is hardly possible in such great lu- <note>The text breaks here. The catchword *brici-* on the following page suggests the word *lubricitate*, referring to the slipperiness or instability of the sea and navigational data.</note>

<sig>I 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: brici-</meta>

<summary>Snellius explains that mathematical dead reckoning is prone to error and must be corrected by daily celestial observations of the sun and stars. He argues that latitude found via the heavens is the most reliable "standard" for adjusting a ship's estimated course and distance.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 69 regarding the correction of navigation errors. Snellius is specifically addressing the "instability" or unreliability of dead reckoning (estimating a ship's position based on speed and heading) and how to reconcile discrepancies between estimated positions and celestial observations of latitude.</meta>

<page-num>70</page-num>
<header>WILLEBRORD SNELLIUS</header>

...instability <note>The text begins mid-word with "bricitate," completing the word "lubricitate" (instability/slipperiness) from the previous page.</note> can be expected. Therefore, a remedy for this error has been sought that deviates as little as possible from the truth. For this correction must be established either by adjusting the angle slightly, or by adjusting the quantity of the course, or by adjusting both. 

But varying the angle while keeping the observed distance and the latitude found from celestial observation the same is excessively dangerous in straighter <term>loxodromes</term> <note>Loxodromes, or rhumb lines, are paths with a constant compass bearing. "Straighter" loxodromes refer to those that closely follow a meridian (North-South).</note>; and in other cases as well, if the observation deviates significantly from the conjecture, as will be taught in the lessons. 

However, varying the quantity of the loxodromic course while keeping the angle the same in more oblique loxodromes is highly unreliable, as we shall demonstrate in that same place. Therefore, there remains only this one method which pursues a middle path between the two and is equally safe in either case. This method can be twofold: such that the error is equally attributed to both the angle of inclination and the estimated length of the course. 

It is, however, accepted and customary among shipmasters that while the variation in longitude <note>original: *longitudinis*. In this context, it refers to the distance traveled along the rhumb line rather than geographic longitude.</note> is retained, only the variation in latitude is changed. We, however, shall follow a method slightly different from this reasoning, which seems least unsafe or liable to danger. We shall examine the remaining matters in the lessons; and what those men attempt mechanically and with too much instability, we shall explain as accurately as possible through our tables.

### PROPOSITION VIII.

> *If, for one traveling straight toward the north or south, the estimate of the course differs from the change in the observed latitude, the end point of the course must be established on the observed parallel.*

A ship having departed from point *a* under the 50th parallel, with the compass needle showing the way, appeared to hold a course under the same meridian, which...

<page-num>71</page-num>
<header>THE DUTCH TIPHYS, BOOK II.</header>

<note>A schematic diagram illustrating points on a vertical axis of latitude. Points are labeled from top to bottom as 'o', 'e', 'i', and 'a'. A horizontal line intersects the axis at point 'e'. Dotted vertical lines extend above 'o' and below 'a'. This diagram visually represents the difference between a ship's estimated progress and its actual observed latitude along a meridian.</note>

Suppose a shipmaster estimates the ship’s course <note>original: *cursum navis*</note> to be 27 miles; however, to test the truth of his estimation, he decides to call celestial observations into council. From these, he might discover that at that very moment he is located under the 52nd parallel. Since 27 maritime miles are equal to 1 degree and 48 minutes <note>original: *scrupulis*. In early modern navigation, "scruples" refers to arc-minutes.</note>, it follows from this estimated quantity of the course that he should be at latitude 51 degrees and 48 minutes—let us say at point ***i***. Yet, according to the observations, he is at point ***e*** under the 52nd parallel, eight minutes further. Conversely, if the estimated latitude is greater than the observed latitude, the conjecture must be rejected and the position must be placed under the observed parallel. For instance, let the estimated course ***ao*** be 51 miles, or 3 degrees and 24 minutes; point ***o*** would therefore be at latitude 53 degrees and 24 minutes. But if the observation only allows for 53 degrees and 12 minutes, I will then assume the latitude to be that much lower and place the ship at point ***e***. In this way, you will correct your conjecture by the heavens.

Nor, for that matter, should this "divining" of the completed course seem to be instituted in vain or to no purpose, even though the measurement of this course (when you are traveling directly north or south) can be found from the heavens themselves. I say this because, through this comparison, I learn by experience over time how much weight should be given to other conjectures and the estimated journey. Indeed, if for several days and nights no clear sky shines forth and no power of observing the heavens is granted, one must certainly flee to this estimation as if to a **sacred anchor** <note>original: *sacram anchoram*. A classical idiom referring to a last resort or ultimate refuge.</note>; lest through imprudence you lose yourself and your possessions, since in such a case no more certain evidence is available anywhere. 

Furthermore, when this discrepancy creeps in—whether from an unexpected increase or decrease in the course's intensity, or from the hidden agitation and movement of the sea—even if you remain ignorant of the cause, you must nevertheless subscribe to the observation of the heavens and withdraw trust from your own conjectures. Certainly, for my part, when the estimated course...

if the variation in latitude <note>original: *latitudinis evariatione*. This refers to the difference in latitude measured between two points on a voyage.</note> is greater, and this happens for several continuous days, it is evident that this error lies in your conjecture and estimation; and that the speed of your course was hindered by the force of the sea. But indeed, when the quantity of the estimated course is less than what the observation of the changed latitude allows, you should not always bring this course back to that parallel on the same meridian.

For this reason, there is a **CAUTION**: If it happens frequently, and through almost continuous observations, that the quantity of the estimated course is found to be consistently less than the observed variation in latitude, it will be an indubitable sign that you have been swept away crosswise by the motion of the sea <note>Snellius is describing the effect of ocean currents, which move a ship off its intended path without the crew realizing it.</note>, even though the magnetic needle <note>The compass needle.</note> maintains that the course was set under one and the same meridian. And in that case, you shall prudently and circumspectly assume a <term>loxodrome</term> <note>From the Greek *loxos* (slant) and *dromos* (path). A loxodrome or rhumb line is a path with a constant compass bearing, which appears as a straight line on a Mercator projection but a spiral on a globe.</note> for your meridian, and also a loxodromic angle such as the quantity of the estimated courses requires. Nor, however, is it sufficiently clear by applying this correction whether your loxodrome inclines toward the east <note>original: *in ortum*</note> or toward the west <note>original: *in occasum*</note>—that is, to the right or to the left—by that hidden motion of the sea, until you learn from an encounter with islands or lands, or from the observations of those who frequent that sea, in which direction the masses of water are carried. For in those places, such movement is almost perpetual and established at certain intervals of time. Truly, such impulses of the sea stealthily sweep ships from the northern coast of America toward England; just as in the African gulf below Guinea, it drives and carries everything toward the east. And so it is in other places as well. 

Let there be an example of this. From the island of Bella <note>Commonly known as Belle Isle, located between Newfoundland and Labrador.</note>, as it is commonly called, situated at the 52nd parallel on the American coast, let a voyage be made straight to the south, as it seemed, and let that journey be rated by nautical estimation at 50 miles, which make a variation of latit- <note>The text cuts off mid-word, continuing to the next page.</note>

...of [latitude difference] 3 degrees 20 minutes, from which the latitude of the place is given as 48 degrees 40 minutes. However, let the altitude of the pole <note>In navigation, the altitude of the celestial pole (the angle of the North Star above the horizon) is equal to the observer's latitude.</note> observed from the heavens be 49 degrees 50 minutes. When I detect such an enormous discrepancy between the estimated and observed latitude, I will not immediately move to the observed parallel. Instead, I will again compare the estimated latitude with the latitude observed on the following day. If, here again, my conjecture deviates from the observation, and the difference of the estimated latitude is once again found to be greater than the observation, this will be sufficient indication that the ship has been stealthily swept aside by the hidden force of the sea, and that this course was not continued under one and the same meridian; rather, the journey was completed along some <term>loxodrome</term> <note>A rhumb line, or a path that crosses all meridians of longitude at the same angle.</note>.

And therefore, from the given difference in latitude according to observation and the estimated distance of the journey, I will investigate the angle of inclination <note>The "angle of inclination" refers to the ship's bearing or course angle relative to the meridian (North-South line).</note> through the proposition... of the first book: *for as the miles of the estimated course are to the [total] minutes of the changed latitude, so the radius <note>In early modern trigonometry, the "radius" (often set at 100,000) represents the total sine or the hypotenuse in a ratio.</note> is to the sine of the complement of the angle of inclination.* The miles of the course are given here as 50, and the minutes of the changed latitude as 32 1/2. From this comes the proportion: as 50 is to 32 1/2, so 100,000 is to the sine 65,003, namely 40 degrees 33 minutes. The complement of this, 49 degrees 27 minutes, is given as the sought angle of inclination.

And from here, now through proposition 30 of the first book, the change in the sought longitude will be given. Namely, as 10,000 is to quadruple the <term>canonical number</term> <note>A value derived from a mathematical table, likely related to the secants used to calculate distance on a sphere.</note> which corresponds to this interval of parallels from 49 degrees 50 minutes to 52 degrees—which is 824.8580—so the quadrant <note>Original: *quadrans*. Here referring to a proportional value in the calculation.</note> of the tangent of the angle of inclination 113,208 / 100,000 is to the minutes of the changed longitude, 196 44/100, which are 3 degrees 16 1/2 minutes. By such an interval, therefore, you have departed from your meridian. Yet it is not yet clear by any indication whether the hidden and unobservable force of the sea has stolen the ship from your meridian toward the east or indeed toward the west, from where you first departed. As if rocks or hidden stones and shallows are nearby, or some island...

...or finally the mainland, into whose vicinity you might be carried at night or in very foggy air; here truly, as the saying goes, *placed between the sacrifice and the flint* <note>original: "intra sacrum & saxa constitutus." A Latin idiom similar to "between a rock and a hard place," referring to a victim standing at the altar about to be struck by the flint knife.</note>, you would find yourself in the greatest peril. For who could imagine so much danger hidden in such a small interval, which can be avoided or turned away from by no means even by the most skilled pilot? From this it becomes so clear how important it is that in nautical logbooks it should also be accurately noted which winds return in an annual cycle on these or those shores, what the agitation of the sea and waves is, and for how much time it lasts there. Thus, taught by the season of the year and by experience, sailors may know into which region of the sea the wave is swept—whether to the east or indeed to the west. For often the sea itself, during clear intervals, even has its own periods of calm in certain places. I will explain the matter with a single example.

->The pattern of maritime agitation and movement between Brazil and<-
->Angola, located on the opposite African shore, under the 12th<-
->southern <term>Austrino</term> <note>Latin "Austrinus" refers to the south, derived from "Auster," the south wind.</note> parallel, is as follows:<-

From the twentieth day of April to the twentieth of the month of July, the waves of the sea are carried toward the northwest <note>original: "Zephyroboream," a compound of the west and north winds; in Dutch, "Noort-West."</note>.

From the 20th of July to the 20th of October, they are not moved very much toward any region.

From the 20th of October to the 20th of January, the waves of the sea are carried toward the southwest <note>original: "Austrozephyrum," a compound of the south and west winds; in Dutch, "Zuyt-West."</note>.

From the 20th of January to the 20th of April, they are again moved by no motion.

And these intervals must be observed carefully and diligently by the masters of ships when they seek a shortcut for their journey. Ignorance or negligence of these things has given many over to death—partly by disease, partly by hunger—as provisions fail or as diseases consume their limbs during a long, unintended delay.

### PROPOSITION IX.

> *If the magnetic needle shows the direction of the course to be steered toward the east or toward the west, and observation confirms the same, there will be no room for correction.*

For here, observation will agree with the estimation and conjecture. And thus, no argument for correction, which is derived solely from the discrepancy between them, will be available to us here. Indeed, the nature of this course is so slippery that, even when you direct the prow straight toward the east or west and observation confirms you are on the same parallel, pilots who frequent the East Indies constantly report that the opposite happens due to the hidden agitation of the sea. For such is the pattern of the winds there that, like the trade winds <note>original: "etesiae," a classical term for seasonal winds.</note> (they themselves call them <term>monsoons</term> <note>original: "massonas," a Latinized form of the Portuguese "monção," referring to the seasonal wind shifts in the Indian Ocean.</note>), they blow back and forth for several continuous months during a certain season of the year: truly, the agitation of the sea is subject to its own movements, just as was said by us in the preceding proposition. And so it happens that when, with sails spread, toward the ea-

Thus, in former times, a certain man intending to visit the island of Saint Helena <note>An essential stop for fresh water and supplies in the South Atlantic for ships returning from the East Indies.</note> in the middle of the sea, after he had reached its parallel—namely, 16 degrees of southern latitude—and from there sought the island itself directly along that parallel, seemed about to reach it shortly with a favorable wind. At last, however, he was found to be more than two hundred miles away toward the west, and to be in the vicinity of America and Brazil: carried back over such a great distance by the hidden movement of the sea, while the winds struggled against it in vain. Wherefore, in such a case, this kind of navigation is very often dangerous, where no proof of your error appears, nor will it be clear how you may extricate yourself from the situation. And thus it seems not only useful but even necessary to angle the course back and forth <note>original: "cursum ultro citroque obliquare" — zigzagging or tacking to verify position.</note>, so that there may be room for correction. Moreover, when observation proves that you have not held the same parallel that you thought, your estimated course must be corrected according to the observations, just as will be explained hereafter.

## PROPOSITION X.

For example, if a ship’s master thinks the course was completed from *a* to *e* along the same parallel, and estimates it at 60 miles at a latitude of 52 2/3 degrees, but then, after performing an observation—having previously placed the ship's location at *e*—he finds himself 25 minutes <note>original: "scrupula" — literally "scruples," used here for minutes of a degree.</note> further north. For thus it is clear that I do not only vary the distance of the course (since I take the hypotenuse *ai* instead of the side of the right angle *ae*), but I also make a departure from the intended compass direction; for instead of the right angle *oae*, I take the angle of inclination *oai*, which is less than a right angle. the principles of correction, being less slippery <note>Meaning more reliable or less prone to error.</note>, were proposed by us above.

But there is a CAUTION to observe: if, while you are sailing along the same parallel <note>A line of latitude, sailing due East or West.</note>, you should discover through frequent and nearly continuous observations that you are being drawn toward the same region (namely, either to the south or to the north) away from the parallel you had intended to follow—provided that the estimate of the journey completed has been made with care—then indeed the latitude should be judged according to the observations made from the heavens. However, the miles of the journey actually completed should be taken as the quantity of the loxodrome <note>original: "loxodromia" — a rhumb line, or the actual diagonal path the ship took across the globe.</note>, and from there the variation in longitude ought to be investigated. 

For example, if in the same diagram the position of the magnetic needle indicates that I have directed my course in a straight line from I to O, from west to east, starting from Belle-Île <note>original: "insula Bella"; likely Belle-Île-en-Mer, though Snellius places it here at 52 degrees.</note> under the parallel of 52 degrees, and the distance of the estimated course is sixty miles; but an observation made from the heavens indicates that I am positioned under the parallel of 51 degrees 35 minutes, and I continuously discover that I am declining to the south from the estimated parallel, that will be sufficient evidence that the ship is being drawn away from its straight course to the side by a hidden motion of the sea <note>A sea current.</note>. Therefore, in this case, the 60 miles of the journey will stand for the length of the loxodrome. And from this, and from the given variation in latitude of 25 minutes, the angle of inclination <note>The bearing or heading of the actual path relative to the meridian.</note> will be given as 84 degrees 1 1/3 minutes, according to the 21st proposition of the first book. And from there, by the [same] proposition, the mecodynamic leg <note>original: "crus mecodynamicum" — a term coined by Snellius meaning the "distance-power" or the actual distance run along the loxodrome.</note> and the variation in longitude is 385 1/2 minutes, which are 6 degrees 25 1/2 minutes. 

I have assumed a small amount, since the delusion that arises from the motion of the sea in terms of latitude is so great there that after completing 24, 25, or 30 miles, you might easily have varied your latitude by an entire degree; and in 60 miles, by more than two degrees. If we assume it in this way, that after 60 miles are completed, the latitude has varied by 1 degree 30 minutes contrary to expectation, the angle of inclination will become 67 degrees 59 minutes, and the variation in longitude 5 degrees 55 minutes; whereas under the same 52-degree parallel, that estimated length of the journey would give a longitude

<summary>Snellius provides a "Caution" for navigators, explaining how to calculate their true position when a hidden current pushes them off their intended parallel of latitude. He demonstrates how to treat the estimated distance as the hypotenuse (loxodrome) of a triangle to solve for the true change in longitude.</summary>

It is of such great importance that these corrective measures <note>original: "medicas manus," literally "healing hands."</note> be applied to this business in their proper place and order. You will easily compare the rest of your findings according to this standard.

And thus far we have discussed the correction of courses which seemed to be set upon a certain circle as indicated by the magnetic needle <note>The compass.</note>; it remains that we next move on to the estimated courses of the loxodromes <note>original: "loxodromiarum" — a <term>loxodrome</term> is a rhumb line, a path with a constant compass bearing that spirals toward the poles.</note> and the comparison of those courses with the observed latitude.

### PROPOSITION XI.

*If, having followed some loxodrome and its distance according to your own estimation, you thereafter discover a variation in latitude different from the one observed, you shall transfer the latitude to the observed parallel while retaining the mesodynamic leg <note>original: "crure mesodynamico." In Snellius’s geometry, this refers to the actual path traveled—the hypotenuse of the navigational triangle.</note> according to the estimated course, and from this you will investigate the difference in longitude.*

The standard for increasing or decreasing the distance of the course is the latitude observed from the heavens; for this, as has been said, can be corrected, or confirmed and refuted by subsequent observations. The other factors <note>Such as the pilot's estimation of speed or current.</note>, however, can neither be repeated nor checked again. In all of these matters, if no error has been made, there ought to be the closest agreement among all the data; but if indeed some difference exists between them, only the latitude is provided as a light to guide us in the correction of the rest.

For instance, if from point *a* I have begun a course on the fifth loxodrome <note>The fifth rhumb line, or a specific compass point.</note>, just as the observed position of the magnetic needle seemed to dictate to me, and the estimated length of the journey is 77 miles, starting from the 50th parallel toward the north, but the observed latitude is 52
<meta>catchword: degrees 20 minutes</meta>

To make this matter more clear, we will compare both methods here against one another. Let us take the same example as before: from the 52nd parallel, on the fifth <term>loxodrome</term> <note>original: "loxodromia." A loxodrome or rhumb line is a path with a constant compass bearing, which appears as a straight line on a Mercator projection but a spiral on a globe.</note>, a distance of 77 miles is given. From this, by the handy tables <note>original Greek: πρόχειρος (*procheiros*), meaning a ready-reference or manual table.</note>, the variation of the conjectured [estimated] latitude is already given as 2 degrees and 40 minutes, and the <term>mecodynamic leg</term> <note>original: "crus mecodynamicum." Snellius uses this term to describe the distance traveled along the rhumb line, which serves as the hypotenuse in his navigational triangles.</note> is 59 and 1643/10,000 miles. 

In order to find the variation in longitude according to this estimated (but not true) difference in latitude, we show two paths, the first of which uses the proportional part of the mecodynamic leg from the variation of latitude, according to the proposition in the first book. Here, the difference in latitude is 2 degrees and 40 minutes. It will be thus: as 10,000 is to the quadruple of the canonical number of parallels lying between 50 degrees and 52 degrees 40 minutes (namely 1024.5416), so is the proportional part of the mecodynamic leg (59 and 1643/10,000) belonging to the intervening parallels—which here are 2 degrees 40 minutes, or 160 minutes—namely 15.8542 <note>original: 158542/10000</note>, to the minutes of the varied longitude due to the quantity of this loxodrome: 383 and 1/3, which are 6 degrees 23 and 1/3 minutes. 

Therefore, the result here is greater than it should rightly be. For we previously found from the same data and the observed latitude difference of 2 degrees 20 minutes, only 6 degrees 21 and 3/10 minutes. The difference that comes between the two calculations is only 1 and 11/100 minutes <note>original: "scrup. 1 11/10," likely a printing error for 1.11 or 1 11/100.</note>, and indeed this is in a course of 77 miles under the fifth loxodrome, where the observed latitude differed from the estimated latitude by a third of a degree [20 minutes]. Such a difference, amidst the great tossing of the waves and the inconstancy of the sea, ought not to be of great concern to us. 

This is even more true because in estimating individual courses, <term>navigators</term> <note>original: "naucleri," referring to the ship's masters or pilots.</note> must necessarily follow this method so that they may be confident in their accounts; for as long as an observation has not yet been taken from the heavens during individual courses, the position of longitude must be recorded.

And indeed, it is all the more advantageous to retain this practice because this commonly used path is completed by multiplication alone. For the mecodynamic leg owed to the individual parallels is first...

Snellius uses this metaphor to explain that he is substituting a simpler, more "sacrificable" (manageable) mathematical calculation for a more difficult one, as they are close enough in value to serve the same purpose.</note> in this place, as the tragic poets are accustomed to do—not only because the sacrifice is more easily performed with it, but also because they do not differ very much from one another. Let us grant that while the former was established with strict reason, the latter was substituted for it for the sake of ease and similarity. For this reason, let what has been said so far regarding the correction of simple loxodromes be sufficient. 

Next follows the correction of composite loxodromes, which, in the variation of latitude and the quantity of the mecodynamic leg, follows the individual laws for each part separately. But taken together, it is governed by certain rules of its own, in which care must be taken lest an error be committed.

<meta>This page transitions from the abstract mathematical calculations of the previous page to the practical realities of navigation. Having established how to calculate change in longitude on a single heading, Snellius now introduces the "composite course," acknowledging that real-world journeys are rarely straight lines due to geography and wind.</meta>

<page-num>83</page-num>
<header>THE DUTCH TIPHYS BOOK II.</header>

## PROPOSITION XII.

*A composite course occurs when several headings are continued in succession before an opportunity arises to make a correction based on observations.*

It happens quite often—indeed, almost always—that the course between one observation of altitude <note>Navigators determined their latitude by measuring the "altitude" (the angle above the horizon) of the sun or the North Star.</note> and the next is not of a single type. Instead, that interval of travel is composed of various segments of different headings. At one moment, the ship may turn toward the south; then, in turn, it must be angled toward the north, following one <term>loxodrome</term> <note>original: "loxodromia." This is a rhumb line, or a path that crosses all meridians at the same angle, maintaining a constant compass bearing.</note> after another, or perhaps directed along a specific parallel of latitude. For the course of a planned journey is often "jointed" and "hamstrung" <note>original: "articulatim delumbatur." Snellius uses a vivid anatomical metaphor to describe how a straight path is broken into segments by geographic obstacles.</note> by the edges of landmasses jutting out into the sea, causing the path to bend and break through various directions.

For example, if you were to plan a voyage from here in Holland, at the 52nd parallel, to Cadiz <note>original: "Gades."</note> and the furthest reaches of Spain: first, you would have to steer westward along roughly the fifth rhumb line from the south until you reach the high and most distant Gallic promontory, called *Gobaeum* by Ptolemy <note>Likely the Pointe du Raz or Cape Finistère in Brittany, France.</note>. Before this promontory lies the island our sailors call Ushant <note>original: "Eyfant." Known today in French as Ouessant.</note> at latitude 48 1/2°. Even this initial course must be broken into several segments along the way. From there, the path must be bent more directly toward the south until you arrive opposite Cape St. Vincent <note>original: "Cabo Sant Vincent."</note>, which Ptolemy called the "Sacred Promontory," at latitude 37 1/2°. Finally, from there, the course must be turned toward the east along roughly the seventh rhumb line to reach Cadiz, at latitude 36 1/4°, near the Strait of Hercules <note>The Strait of Gibraltar.</note> (through which the Mediterranean Sea pours itself out into the Ocean). This, of course, assumes that a favorable wind follows the travelers. Indeed, even in the open sea, where the ships have already gained the deep and no [land] appears...

<meta>The catchword "parent" at the bottom right indicates the first word of the next page starts a new sentence or thought, likely "apparent" (appear).</meta>

<summary>Snellius explains that real-world navigation requires "composite courses" consisting of multiple different headings to navigate around landmasses, using a voyage from Holland to Cadiz as a practical example.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "composite course" (cursum compositum). While the previous page introduced the concept as a necessity caused by geographic obstacles like headlands, this page explains how weather—specifically storms and adverse winds—forces a navigator to break a straight path into a series of staggered segments or "tacks."</meta>

<page-num>84</page-num>
<header>WILLEBRORD SNELL</header>

land, or reefs, or shallows, or hidden rocks appear or are feared, it quite often happens then too, that as a greater storm breaks in, <note>original Greek: "μὴ δυνάμενοι ἀντοφθαλμεῖν τῷ ἀνέμῳ"</note> **being unable to face the wind**—since they cannot strive against it—they turn the path of the sails as the winds demand, just as that line of the Poet says:

> *Neither are we able to struggle against it nor even to hold our course*
> *sufficiently; since fortune overcomes us, let us follow.*
> *And where she calls, let us turn our path.* <note>Snellius quotes Virgil’s *Aeneid*, Book 5, lines 21–23. In the poem, the pilot Palinurus realizes the wind is too strong to fight and advises Aeneas to stop struggling and go where the wind blows them.</note>

For here, as more violent gales rush in, <note>original Greek: "ἐπιδόντες φέρονται"</note> **giving themselves up, they are carried along**, and they surrender themselves to the will of the wind. Otherwise, even when not yielding to the most adverse blasts of the winds so as to be driven backward, they turn the prow now this way, now that, by a slanting of the course, in order to advance the intended journey in some part. And from this come those short turns of 2, 3, 4, or 5 miles—more or fewer—by which the direction of the primary course is disjointed and broken. This course, therefore, fused from a mixture of various types—namely two, three, or even more—is what we now call a **composite course**.

## PROPOSITION XIII.

*If, in a composite course, all segments continuously turn from a shared parallel toward the north or south, they will increase the variation in latitude; if any of them are reflected in the opposite direction, they will decrease it in proportion.*

Since every course—except for the eighth <note>The "eighth rhumb" refers to a course due East or West. Since these paths follow a parallel of latitude exactly, they are the only ones that do not change a ship's distance from the equator.</note>, which remains under the same parallel—induces a change of latitude and parallels; whether it is stretched from the south toward the north
