<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of how latitude changes during a "composite course"—a journey made of several different legs or tacks. Snellius is explaining that while individual legs have predictable latitude changes, the total change of a complex journey depends on whether the legs move consistently in one direction or double back toward the starting latitude.</meta>

<page-num>85</page-num>
<header>THE DUTCH TIPHYS BOOK II.</header>

the latitude is increased in a straight line, or conversely, decreased from north to south, and this indeed applies to each segment individually. In joined and composite courses, however, the matter behaves a little differently. In those cases, the variation of latitude can remain the same, or even be increased, and sometimes it can even be decreased. 

For example, if from the 52nd parallel any course brings you to the 50th, and from there you turn the course straight east or west; now, since you are traveling continuously on the same parallel, no greater variation in latitude will be introduced from this point. But if you then turn further south from there to reach the 48th parallel, the variation in latitude will be increased by that much, for the starting point and this end point are 4 degrees apart. However, if from the 50th parallel you had turned back toward the north until you reached the parallel of 50 degrees and 45 minutes, here the variation in latitude would have been decreased <note>Because you are moving back toward your starting latitude of 52, the total "variation" or distance from the start is reduced.</note>. 

Let the following be an example: from the mouth of the Maas River <note>original: "Mosae fluminis"</note>, situated at the 51st parallel, one has sailed toward the headlands of England. Then:

| | |
| :--- | :--- |
| To the **Shingles** <note>original: "Singels"</note> by the **South-West by West** wind <note>original: "Zuyt-VVest ten VVeſten"</note> | 33 miles |
| From the **Shingles** to **Beachy Head** <note>original: "Beveſier"</note> **West-South-West** <note>original: "VVest-Zuyt-VVeft"</note> | 6 miles |
| From **Beachy Head** to **The Lizard** <note>original: "Lezert"</note> **West by South** <note>original: "VVest ten Zuyden"</note> | 54 1/2 miles |
| From **The Lizard** to **Cape Finisterre** <note>original: "Caput Finiſterre"</note> **South-South-West** <note>original: "Zuyd-Zuyd-VVeft"</note> | 113 1/2 miles |

The first course is the 5th **rhumb line** <term>loxodromia: a path that follows a single constant compass bearing, crossing all meridians at the same angle</term> from the south toward the west, as appears below in the diagram of the winds; and this is designated by the path *ae*.

The second course is the 6th rhumb line from the south toward the west; and this is designated by the path *ei*.

The third is the 7th rhumb line from the south toward the west, and this is expressed by the path *oe*.

The fourth is the 2nd rhumb line from the south toward the west, and this is expressed by the path *ou*.

<meta>This page continues the practical application of Snellius's navigation theories. <page-num>86</page-num>
<header>WILLEBRORD SNELLIUS</header>

<note>A geometric diagram is located on the right side of the upper text block. It shows a series of intersecting lines and dashed vertical lines, labeled with characters including m, l, r, s, e, o, n, y, and x. It represents rhumb lines—the paths of constant bearing—or navigational vectors plotted relative to a coordinate grid.</note>

You see, therefore, that all the <term>rhumb lines</term> <note>original: "loxodromias," paths that cross all meridians of longitude at the same angle</note> incline toward the south from the parallel where they began. Thus, they move further away from the 52nd parallel where the journey started by decreasing the latitude. The variation of latitude for each leg will be found through the proposition... of the first book, or by the <term>handy tables</term> <note>original: "canonas πρόχειρος" (kanonas procheiros), a reference to ready-reckoning tables like those used by Ptolemy or for trigonometric shortcuts</note>, for each leg as follows:

| Leg Number | Course Name | Miles | Lat. Var. (Deg. Min.) |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 5 | **South-West by South** <note>Dutch: Zuyt-Vvest ten Zuyten</note> | 33 | 1. 13 $\frac{82}{100}$ |
| 6 | **West-South-West** | 6 | 0. 9 $\frac{19}{100}$ |
| 7 | **West by South** | 54 $\frac{1}{2}$ | 0. 42 $\frac{53}{100}$ |
| 2 | **South-South-West** | 113 $\frac{1}{2}$ | 6. 59 $\frac{44}{100}$ |
| | | *Total* | 9. 4 $\frac{49}{100}$ |

Therefore, these individual variations must be added together, which total 9 degrees and 4 $\frac{49}{100}$ minutes. And because the stated courses turn toward the south, they must be subtracted from the initial latitude of 52 degrees. From this, according to these estimates, the latitude of **Cape Finisterre** is given as 40 degrees and 55 $\frac{51}{100}$ minutes. There was no need for such meticulousness as to seek even the hundredth parts of the minutes; I could have stopped at the minutes themselves and assumed the value closest to the truth. For the inconstancy of the sea is so great that these estimates of journeys do not even [reach the precision of one...]

<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration of how to calculate a ship's position when sailing a "composite course"—a journey made of several different legs. Snellius is specifically discussing "prostaphaeresis," a term for the corrections or adjustments added to or subtracted from a base measurement to find the true position.</meta>

<page-num>87</page-num>
<header>THE DUTCH TIPHYS BOOK II.</header>

can assign the truth to us within one or two <term>scruples</term> <note>original: "scrupulum," a small unit of measure, here referring to a minute (1/60th) of a degree</note>, as will be shown in proposition . . . . through the examples and observations brought forward. But if any of these courses were turned toward the <term>North</term> <note>original: "Boream," the North wind</note>, it would subtract from the total variation in latitude in proportion. Indeed, it could even push the latitude northward beyond the starting parallel. For instance, if this latest <term>rhumb line</term> <note>original: "loxodromia," a path of constant bearing</note> from *Lefert* <note>a point on the journey</note> to *Cape Finisterre*, which is **South-South-West**, were changed to be from *Lefert* **North-North-West** for 113 1/2 miles, the variation of latitude in this course would be given as 6 degrees and 59 44/100 minutes as before, but turned toward the North from its beginning. Therefore, the three previous latitudes would need to be added—namely 1 degree 13 11/100 minutes, 9 12/100 minutes, and 42 33/100 minutes—the sum of which is 2 degrees and 5 minutes. This is how far one has gone toward the <term>south</term> <note>original: "meridiem"</note> from the start of all the courses at the mouth of the <term>Meuse</term> <note>original: "Mosae ostio," the river in the Netherlands where the voyage began</note>. But from there, the course turns back toward the north by 6 degrees 59 44/100 minutes. Therefore, if that smaller variation is subtracted from this larger one, the remaining variation is 4 degrees 54 minutes; but it must be counted North from the 52nd parallel, and it will fall on the 56th degree and 54th minute. The reasoning for the remaining cases will be similar.

### P R O P O S I T I O N XIV.

> *If in a composite course all legs continually turn from a common*
> *meridian toward the east or west, they will increase the*
> *variation in longitude; if any of them are reflected in the opposite*
> *direction, they will decrease it in proportion.*

Just as we explained the <term>prostaphaeresis</term> <note>from the Greek word for addition and subtraction; a correction value used to find a true position from a mean or starting point</note> of the changed latitude in Proposition 13, so here we shall pursue the prostaphaeresis of longitude. Both are used in practice and are often mixed together in composite courses. The variation of longitude, and its increase or decrease, is here not num-

<meta>The catchword "mera-" indicates the next word is likely "merator" or "meratur," continuing the discussion of numerical calculation.</meta>

<summary>Snellius explains how to calculate the net change in latitude when a voyage includes both northward and southward legs, demonstrating that opposing directions cancel each other out. He then introduces Proposition 14, which applies the same logic of "prostaphaeresis" (adjustments) to calculating longitude.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how to calculate a ship's position during a complex journey. While the previous page focused on latitude (North-South distance), Snellius now shifts to the calculation of longitude (East-West distance) and how individual legs of a journey either add to or subtract from the total longitudinal change.</meta>

# WILLEBRORD SNELLIUS
<page-num>88</page-num>

we measure from the prime meridian; but rather from the meridian of that place from which you begin the journey or the calculation, to that place toward which you are traveling—whether that path turns away from there toward the east or toward the west. For example, if you depart from point *a* of the meridian *ay* (as shown in the diagram of the preceding proposition) toward the west on course *ae*; and the following leg turns again from the endpoint *e* toward *i*; and a third from *i* to *o*; and a fourth from *o* to *u*; these courses will continually increase the change in longitude, for they all extend further westward beyond the most recent meridian. 

But if the latest course is turned back from *o* toward *n*, as in course *on*, it will subtract from the change already made as much as its own magnitude requires. Thus, in this case, *ln* subtracted from *ly* leaves *ny* as the change in longitude between the beginning of the journey *a* and this endpoint *n*. Furthermore, when you sail in a straight line toward the <term>South</term> <note>original: "Austrum"</note> or <term>North</term> <note>original: "Boream"</note> along the same meridian, it is manifest that nothing is added to or taken away from the change in longitude.

Now, let the courses, the inclination of those courses, and the distances be assumed as they were in the preceding proposition, and let us seek the change in longitude for each. This will be achieved through the proposition [number omitted]... if the quadruple of the difference of the nearest parallels is multiplied by the quadrant of the tangent of the inclination, or the <term>mecodynamic number</term> <note>original: "mecodynamicum"; a term coined by Snellius relating to the mathematical power or factor used to calculate longitudinal change along a rhumb line</note> assigned to each individual <term>minute</term> <note>original: "scrupulo"; literally a 'scruple,' used here to mean 1/60th of a degree</note>. 

I here submit a table, in which I have expressed the following: in the second column, the order of the <term>rhumb lines</term> <note>original: "loxodromiarum"; paths of constant bearing that cross all meridians at the same angle</note>; in the third, the names of the winds and the direction of the course; in the fourth, the estimated miles; in the fifth, the change in latitude or the parallel where each leg ends; in the sixth, the canonical quadruples belonging to the intervening parallels; and in the seventh, the change in longitude for each leg. In the first column, however, I have placed the canonical numbers that act as multipliers, or the quadrants of the tangent of the inclinations, as you see here.

In recording the latitude here, I have also pursued the small fractions, so that by this example I might show how little they matter to the <term>rhumb line</term> <note>original: "rhombum," the path of a ship maintainng a constant compass bearing</note>, unless by chance the courses are extremely oblique, such as rhumb 7 1/2 or 7 3/4 <note>Rhumb points represent angles of 11.25 degrees each; 7 1/2 rhumbs would be nearly perpendicular to the meridian, i.e., nearly due East or West</note>, or if you are operating in the very small parallels near and beyond the Arctic Circle. ...perfluous <note>The text begins with the end of a word from the previous page, likely "superfluous."</note>, which can rightly be attributed either to a conjecture in the length of the journey or to an error in observing the pole’s altitude <note>The "pole's altitude" refers to the angle of the North Star or the Sun above the horizon, used to determine latitude.</note>. Thus, in that *minute reasoning* <note>original: "λεπτολογίᾳ" (leptologia), a Greek term for overly subtle or hair-splitting logic</note>, it can easily be removed from the table without any loss of the work at hand.

If the last course had been *South-South-East* instead of *South-South-West*—that is, if *on* <note>A point on the author's geometric diagram</note> had been used instead of *ou*—then the final longitudinal variation of 4 degrees 3 <term>minutes</term> <note>original: "scrupula," literally "scruples," the standard term for minutes of a degree at this time</note> would have to be subtracted from the rest, namely from 2 degrees 46 minutes, and the longitudinal variation *ny* of only 4 degrees 43 minutes will remain.

Finally, if one had sailed straight south from point *o* to point *l*, it is manifest that there is no other longitudinal variation at point *l* than what had been at point *o* previously: namely, 8 degrees 46 minutes. What follows is a comparison of the conjectured variation of the composite course with that which is observed from the heavens.

---

## PROPOSITION XV.

> *If, in a composite course, the estimated latitude differs from the observed latitude, then, while retaining the longitudinal variation, you shall transfer the latitude to the observed parallel.*

Simple courses <note>A "simple course" is a single leg of a journey on one constant bearing.</note> have had their own rules, according to which they were governed by observations made from the heavens, and some of them would be corrected if too great an irregularity occurred. In composite courses <note>A "composite course" involves multiple changes in direction (tacking or changing legs).</note>, however, the method of correcting individual legs is more difficult and more intricate. Therefore, unless another evident cause—which we shall explain later—suggests otherwise, we shall retain the longitudinal variation and, setting aside the difference in latitude between the conjectured and the observed, we shall pass to the <term>observed parallel</term> <note>The specific line of latitude confirmed by celestial observation (using a sextant or astrolabe) rather than estimated by speed and direction.</note>. The matter will be clearer with an example. For let the courses be the same as those above:

<summary>Snellius explains how navigators should adjust their calculations when their estimated position differs from celestial observations, suggesting that latitude should be corrected to the "observed parallel" while maintaining the calculated longitudinal change. This highlights the ongoing 17th-century struggle to reconcile dead reckoning with astronomical data.</summary>
<keywords>navigation, longitude, latitude, dead reckoning, celestial observation, composite course, Snellius, minutes, parallels</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of reconciling navigational calculations with astronomical observations. <header>THE DUTCH TIPHYS BOOK II.</header>

...were expressed in Proposition 12, from the mouth of the River Maas <note>original: "Mosa"</note> under the fiftieth parallel to the promontory of Spain called *Cape Finisterre* <note>original: "Cabofinnis terræ"</note>. At that location, an observation was made from the heavens and the latitude was found to be 42 degrees 42 <term>minutes</term> <note>original: "scrupula," literally "scruples," the standard term for minutes of a degree at this time</note>. And yet, from the <term>rhumb lines</term> <note>original: "loxodromia," a path of constant bearing</note> and the account of the journey, the latitude previously given to us was 40 degrees 55 minutes, so that the difference between the latter and the former is 1 degree 47 minutes. I would attribute this to an error <note>original: "hallucinationi," implying a wandering or miscalculation</note> of the rhumb lines, so that it seems necessary that their inclinations were actually different; and that in the observation of the <term>magnetic declination</term> <note>original: "chalyboclisis," a term Snellius coined from the Greek words for "iron" and "inclination" to describe the compass needle's deviation from true north</note>, or by some other occasion, a slight error was committed. Nor is it at all strange or unheard of, in a journey of such length and over an interval of so many days, when in the meantime no observation is made from the heavens, that such a great difference from the true latitude should intervene—which will also be made clear below by another example. For we do not present these things as fictions, but as facts extracted from the journals of sailors. Furthermore, since no assistance is granted for correcting the longitude, I shall retain the value found previously, which for us between the Maas and the said promontory was 12 degrees 46 minutes. And thus, I find it necessary to establish the limit of the completed journey at that point where the meridian and the common parallel intersect.

And yet, as we have said, there is a **CAUTION**: if that difference is enormous, there is need for such a correction by which the angles at which the rhumb lines are inclined to the meridian are changed. That is to say, different rhumb lines must be assumed than those which the magnetic needle indicated during the course itself, because this may have been caused by the hidden force of the sea, by which the ship was stealthily carried away crosswise from its intended path. For example, the waves of the sea flow with such a violent, yet unobservable force near the *Davis Strait* <note>original: "fretum Davis"</note> from the north toward the south, that those who direct their course eastward from there—as shown by the magnetic needle, even when properly corrected according to its magnetic declination—perpetually discover that they have not held that course, but instead of a parallel...

<meta>This page continues Snellius's technical explanation of how to reconcile the estimated position of a ship (dead reckoning) with its actual position as determined by astronomical observations. <header>WILLEBRORDI SNELLII</header>

...that this <term>rhumb line</term> <note>original: "loxodromiam," a path of constant bearing</note> had traversed the fourth point between the East and the South (called by our people the *Southeast direction* <note>original: "Zuyt Oost streeck"</note>). How this might be corrected in this simple course—when it begins and ends with an observation—I have explained above. Therefore, once that angle of the misleading rhumb line is known, I will adjust all the others to it. For since you are well aware that the observed course here is different from the true one, and that the movement of the sea is carried from the North to the South; if you, therefore, travel toward the north under the same meridian, it follows that your journey is less than you would estimate by your conjecture. If you travel toward the south, it is much greater. Thus, once you have thoroughly understood the cause, you will cease to wonder why the observed latitude there was less than estimated, and conversely why it is greater here. 

Between the meridian and the parallel, the angles of the rhumb lines and the distance of the course must be adjusted in such a way as diligent observation and a careful estimate of the journey completed shall prove, according to the rules established for simple courses, where we assign individual observations to individual legs of the journey and correct them as necessary. 

Moreover, since in compound courses—where observation and correction are only applied after several continuous legs of travel—the matter involves more difficulty; it is necessary that you know which rhumb line must be substituted for another in each leg, and so come prepared and instructed. Thus, in the example we just reviewed, although you seemed to be going toward the East, you should not record *East* <note>original: "Oost"</note> in your <term>logbook</term> <note>original: "ephemerin tuam nauticam"</note>, but as we said, *Southeast* <note>original: "Zuydtoost"</note> for so many miles; and likewise for the rest according to the same standard. 

But if you should come upon some sea unprepared, and be carried into it contrary to your expectations—having never even heard of its currents or force—you will need to apply your mind to this matter with the greatest care and diligence, and compare your estimates with the observations, from which the correc-

->THE DUTCH TIPHYS, BOOK II.<-

...once the correction of the <term>rhumb lines</term> <note>original: "loxodromiarum," lines that cross all meridians at the same angle, representing a constant compass heading</note> is made, you may determine something sound regarding your progress or your shortfall; for this is the duty of the ship and of the industrious master <note>original: "naucleri," the ship's pilot or captain</note>: to whose trust not only these goods, however precious, but even the whole ship—in which the life and safety of so many men are contained—is entrusted.

It has seemed appropriate to attach to this theorem a very clear example of a longer journey and the method of calculation, from which our nautical practice may be thoroughly understood; and to transfer here all that we applied above in organizing a ship's log <note>original: "nautico diario"</note>, and to reduce it to the tables <note>original: "abacos"</note> of a nautical summary <note>original: "epilogismi"</note>. 

Here I must warn you at the very threshold <note>that is, at the start of the explanation</note> that the angles of the rhumb lines and the distance of the courses have been corrected by the most skilled masters—who traveled along and were by no means inexperienced in these difficulties—according to their custom, just as they, taught by experience, believed most closely approached the truth. And they indicate this by the technical term *kept courses and miles* <note>original Dutch: "behouden coursen en mylen," referring to the actual path made good over the ground after accounting for leeway and currents</note>. This signifies that the courses and the miles are made "sound and whole" <note>original Latin: "sarta tecta," a legal metaphor for being in good repair</note>; by this they imply that, with faith, diligence, and a legitimate estimation of both [currents and winds] to which these are subject in the middle of the sea, these corrected values have been substituted for the observed and apparent ones, just as daily experience, the mistress of all things, has taught them.

Let us take, therefore, an example from the ship's log for the fourth proposition, where from a given bearing and distance of travel, you will first derive the change in longitude and latitude through art—or if you prefer, rather from a ready-to-hand <note>original Greek: "procheirō" (προχείρῳ), meaning a handy table or manual</note> table. But you must also note that the position of the place toward which one is going is here *further East* <note>original: "orientalior"</note> than the place from which one departs, and furthermore also *further South* <note>original: "meridionalior"</note>. It was necessary to mention this so that you may afterwards correctly and without error establish the longitude and latitude of the desired location. The formula for this ship's log, based on estimation and observations, is such that on the first page <note>original: "selidi"</note>...

With this most recent leg of the journey, we finally arrived on the eighth of September at a promontory of Spain, commonly called <term>Cape Finisterre</term> <note>original: "Caput finis terrae," literally the "Cape at the End of the Earth."</note>, where Spain extends furthest to the west on its northern coast. To Ptolemy <note>The influential 2nd-century Greco-Roman geographer.</note>, this would be the Nerium promontory.

The measurement and direction of the first course are expressed in this log; specifically, the distance of 18 miles is in the fourth column <note>original: "selide," a term derived from the Greek for "page-column."</note>; the direction, however, of East-Southeast is in the third column; and the number of the rhumb line is in the second column to aid the memory. In this way, all things will be completed more successfully and accurately: nor will you stand on slippery ground <note>A metaphor for being uncertain or prone to error.</note> once you have finished this labor, so that...

...you should add the number of the second rhumb line <note>original: "loxodromiæ"—a path of constant bearing on a globe.</note>. Otherwise, it would be necessary to strain the memory even here, which would be dangerous amidst such a variety of data; likewise, it would not be very easy for those who are less practiced. It will therefore be permitted, as we have already expressed above, to use **handy tables** <note>original: "canonas προχείρους" (kanonas procheirous)—ready-reckoners or "handy rules" for quick use.</note>, and to derive from them the variation of latitude according to the rhumb lines and the **mesodynamic miles** <note>original: "miliaria mesodynamica"—Snellius's technical term for "mean-motion" miles used in his navigation system.</note>; if perhaps one is less practiced in calculation. 

I truly judge it both the safest and most convenient for one who is at all accustomed to **calculation** <note>original: "epilogismo"—the process of mathematical reckoning or logical deduction of results.</note> to fetch the calculation from the source itself: and for that purpose to have a small **table** <note>original: "canonion"</note> at hand, in which: 
- In the first place, the order of the rhumb lines and their quarters are arranged; 
- In the second, four times the **sine of the complement** <note>original: "sinuum quadruplum"—this refers to the cosine, multiplied by four for the specific scale Snellius uses.</note> of the angles; 
- In the third, the quarters of the **tangents** belonging to the angles of the rhumb lines themselves. 

For the second column will serve for finding the latitude through the corollary of proposition 28 and through proposition 30 of the first book. I have also subjoined the table itself here, based on a radius of five circles, so that I might also free you from the trouble of this calculation.

Now, therefore, I shall extract the sine number which belongs to the sixth course <note>original: "sextum cursum"—this refers to the sixth rhumb line, or a compass point of 67.5 degrees from the meridian.</note> (for that is given here as East-South-East <note>original: "O. Z. O." standing for *Oost-Zuid-Oost* in the Dutch maritime tradition Snellius is formalizing.</note>), namely 153,072. This number, when multiplied by the miles and divided by the radius, will give the **minutes** <note>original: "scrupula"—literally "small sharp stones," used here for minutes of a degree of latitude.</note> of the varied latitude, 27<sup>55296</sup>/<sub>153072</sub>; or, which is sufficient, 27<sup>5</sup>/<sub>14</sub>, according to the 19th proposition of the first book. I shall record this **variation** <note>original: "evariationem"</note> in the opposite table in its own **column** <note>original: "selide"—a term for a column of text or a section of a ledger.</note>, under the sixth column for the variation of latitude; and, because the journey proceeds toward the equator <note>original: "æquinoctialem"—the equinoctial line.</note> and decreases the latitude, I shall write the letter **S** in the following column in that region as a mark of **subtraction** <note>original: "subductionis".</note>. 

Next, I shall investigate the variation of longitude by the 30th proposition of Book 1 in this manner. The parallel from which the sailing began was 52 degrees 25 minutes; this most recent one, however, is 28 minutes less; and therefore it is 51 degrees 57 minutes. The difference of the numbers from the **canonical tables** <note>original: "canonicis"—referring to the standard mathematical tables of parallels.</note> of the parallels which belong to these is 45.6587, and four times that same value is 182.6348. Then, the quarter of the tangent which belongs to this **rhumb line** <note>original: "loxodromiam"</note>, divided by the radius, will give the **longitudinal factor** <note>original: "crus mecodynamicum"—Snellius's technical term for the geometric "leg" or side of a triangle that determines the change in longitude.</note> as <sup>60355</sup>/<sub>100000</sub>, which will be consistent with the minute of latitude, according to the corollary of proposition 28, book 1. 

From this, therefore, let it be done according to proposition 30: as 10,000 is to the Canonical 182.6348, so is <sup>60355</sup>/<sub>100000</sub>; or, with the remainders trimmed, 110. 228<sup>611</sup>/<sub>1000</sub>. That is to say, as 100,000 is to 182.63, so is 603<sup>55</sup>/<sub>100</sub> to the minutes of the varied longitude...

...of 110, <unclear>10/60</unclear>. These are 1 degree and 50 minutes. I shall write this number in the <term>column</term> <note>original: "selidi", referring to a specific column in a mathematical table</note> that is opposite. And because this course advances the journey toward the desired equator, I will assign the letter S <note>S for South, as the ship moves toward the equator from the North</note> in the following column. This course is represented in the attached table by the drawing of line *ae*. I have neglected <term>mecodynamic</term> miles <note>mecodynamic: Snellius’s specific term for distance units derived from his geometric theories of the Earth's dimensions</note> here, because the business is finished more easily this way. If anyone prefers it, and that method is pleasing to them, they are free to use it; for such a person could then find the variation of longitude in individual segments through Proposition 13 of the first book. However, that seems more laborious to me. We shall certainly continue to follow this path which we have now established. But if you wish to know by what single simple course the beginning and end of composite courses can be joined and connected, that matter would certainly have its use there; but the same thing was also explained by us above in Proposition 32 of Book 1. Therefore, we assign its own place to this column, even if we do not use it now.

The second course follows, which continues from point *e* under the 51 degree 57 minute parallel toward the East <note>original: "Ortum", referring to the rising sun or East</note> for 4 miles; this is designated by line *ei*. ...sixth, which course is expressed here by the line *ou*, of 14 miles, from which the change of latitude is given as 0 degrees 21 1/2 <term>minutes</term> <note>original: "scrupula", the 60th part of a degree</note>. The fifth course is *V Y*, again *O. Z. O*, or the sixth-order <term>rhumb line</term> <note>original: "loxodromia". A rhumb line is a path with a constant compass bearing, which appears as a spiral on a globe but a straight line on a Mercator map.</note> of 3 miles. From this, the change in latitude is given as 0 degrees 4 1/2 minutes to be subtracted, which I will enter into its <term>registers</term> <note>original: "laterculis", referring to the cells of a data table</note> accordingly. Furthermore, the change in longitude resulting from this is 0 degrees 17 1/2 minutes to be added to the previous totals, and thus you see them written in their registers.

The sixth course is *W. Z. W.* Likewise a sixth-order rhumb line of 11 miles, which is designated in the table by the line *Y S*, from which the change in latitude is given as 0 degrees 17 minutes. This still tends toward the south, and therefore must be subtracted; from this, the parallel passing through point *S* is given as 50 degrees 45 minutes. From this, the change in longitude is 1 degree 4 1/2 minutes, but because this course turns back toward the west, it must be subtracted; therefore, the difference in longitude between the <term>meridians</term> <note>Lines of constant longitude passing through the poles.</note> passing through points *A* and *S* will be only 4 degrees 1 1/2 minutes.

The seventh course is *W. t. Z.* likewise a seventh-order rhumb line of 1 1/2 miles, which is expressed by the line *S R*; from which the change in latitude will be found to be 0 degrees 1 minute to be subtracted, because it advances the course toward the south. And from that, a change in longitude of 0 degrees 8 minutes, likewise to be subtracted from the preceding totals because it increases the course toward the west, contrary to the target. And so I shall refer both these numbers to their places.

The eighth course is *Z. O. t. Z.*, a third-order rhumb line which is marked by the line *R L*, and is 2 1/2 miles; from which the change in latitude is given as 0 degrees 8 1/2 minutes, which likewise, like all the preceding...

<meta>This page continues the specific navigation example from page 102. Snellius is calculating the final legs of a voyage and then comparing the "dead reckoning" position (based on distance and direction) with a real-world celestial observation taken once the weather cleared.</meta>

->THE DUTCH TIPHY S, BOOK II<-

...maintaining the course, advances toward the south. From this, the change of longitude is found to be 0 degrees 8½ <term>minutes</term> <note>original: "scrupula", the 60th part of a degree</note>, which again, according to the plan, advances the course toward the desired goal. And so I shall record it in its places with notations: **S** for subtraction there, and **A** for addition here.

The ninth course is South-East by South <note>original: "Z. O. t. Z." (Zuid-Oost ten Zuiden)</note>, of the same kind as the preceding one expressed by the line *LM*, of 5 miles. It will give a change of latitude of 0 degrees 16⅔ minutes to be subtracted; and from that, a change of longitude of 0 degrees 17½ minutes to be added.

The tenth course *MN* is South-South-East <note>original: "Z. Z. O." (Zuid-Zuid-Oost)</note>, the second-order <term>rhumb line</term> <note>original: "loxodromia". A path of constant bearing.</note> of 14 miles. It will give a change of latitude of 0 degrees 51¼ minutes to be subtracted from the preceding totals, and a change of longitude of 0 degrees 33 minutes, which likewise advances the course toward the desired place. Therefore, I shall insert it in its proper places with its notes.

Thus far, tossed for four whole days upon the open Ocean, the sailors had no opportunity for celestial observation; now, at last, with clarity restored and the clouds scattered, an observation made at noon by the sun shows the <term>altitude of the pole</term> <note>original: "poli altitudinem". This refers to the latitude of the observer, measured by the height of the celestial pole (or the sun's relative position) above the horizon.</note> to be 48 degrees 38 minutes. I shall compare this with the value I derived from the rhumb line courses and their distances. For when the latitude changes of each leg—which have already been derived from the distances of the courses—are added together, and all being consistent movements from the north toward the south, their sum is given as 2 degrees 58 minutes. When this is subtracted from the initial altitude of the pole at point *A* (which was 52 degrees 25 minutes), it leaves the estimated altitude of the pole at point *N* as 49 degrees 27 minutes. 

However, the observed altitude was only 48 degrees 38 minutes; the difference between the two is 49 minutes. If, therefore, I trust this celestial observation and am certain of its accuracy (for you can verify it the next day by the sun, or at night by the <term>fixed stars</term> <note>original: "fixas", the stars that appear stationary relative to one another</note>, through repeated observation), I will here reject the latitude estimated from the courses. I shall transfer the ship on the map from point *N* to the observed <term>parallel</term> *FG* along the same meridian *NG*; so that the latitude indeed matches the celestial ob[servation]...

<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration of how a navigator calculates their position after a series of varied courses. Snellius is reconciling "dead reckoning" (the estimated position based on distance and direction steered) with a physical celestial observation made by the crew after four days of cloudy weather.</meta>

### WILLEBRORD SNELL
<note>original: "Willebrordi Snellii"</note>

I will arrange the observation. However, let me maintain the same longitude which I derived from the <term>rhumb lines</term> <note>original: "loxodromijs", paths of constant bearing on a sphere.</note>. This will likewise be elicited here from each of the individual courses, with the additions to be added and the subtractions to be subtracted, just as you see here:

| degrees | minutes | | degrees | minutes |
| :--- | :--- | --- | :--- | :--- |
| 1 | 50 | | 1 | 4 11/12 |
| 0 | 26 | | 1 | 8 |
| 1 | 10 | | --- | --- |
| 1 | 23 | | 2 | 12 11/12 **S.** |
| 0 | 17 1/2 | | | |
| 0 | 8 1/2 | | | |
| 0 | 17 1/4 | | | |
| 0 | 33 | | | |
| --- | --- | | | |
| 5 | 15 1/4 **A.** | | | |

The sum of the additions <note>marked **A.** in the table</note> is 5 degrees 15 1/4 <term>minutes</term> <note>original: "scrupula", the 60th part of a degree.</note>, and the sum of the subtractions <note>marked **S.** in the table</note> is 2 degrees 12 11/12 minutes. If, therefore, I subtract the latter from the former, there remains a change in longitude of 4 degrees 3 minutes; and by this much have we advanced from the island and point *A* toward the <term>east</term> <note>original: "ortum", the rising of the sun.</note> to point *F*. If, then, *A* is established under the 344th meridian, *A* and *F* will be under the 344th meridian <note>Snellius is likely correcting a previous assumption about the starting point or comparing the change relative to a fixed line.</note>, so that this translation of the endpoint *F* regards only the latitude, while the estimated longitude remains unchanged.

But here the first thing that occurs is that a difference of 49 minutes intervenes between the observed and the conjectured altitude <note>referring to the latitude calculated by observation versus that estimated by the crew's logs.</note>; this might seem to be quite a large difference, and for that reason, the observation might seem to have been conducted with less care. However, the consensus argues that no error underlies the observation. For since several, and indeed experienced, shipmasters were traveling on the same vessel, the majority of whom had visited both the East and West Indies more than once, and who had conducted observations of this kind...

<meta>This page continues Snellius's analysis of navigational errors. On the previous page, he calculated the change in longitude based on the crew's recorded courses. Now, he compares several different celestial observations of latitude taken by the crew to find the most reliable one, eventually concluding that an unknown ocean current has pushed the ship further south than their dead reckoning suggested.</meta>

<page-num>105</page-num>

->TIPHYS BATAVUS, BOOK II.<-

...were most expert in making observations; nevertheless, all their results remain below the forty-ninth degree. For that reason, I will note down the observations of every one of them accurately and distinctly.

| 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 | 5 | 6 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 48° 34' | 48° 8' | 48° 58' | 48° 38' | 48° 20' | 48° 7' |

I have followed the fourth observation in all my calculations. Its author was not only more skilled in the art than the others, but he also applied himself to this task with such supreme diligence and industry as to invite competition. You can see, in fact, that of the remaining five observations, four fall far below his, while only one exceeds it. This is evidence enough that this particular observation is closest to the truth; therefore, let it be accepted by us as the legitimate one. 

There is as great a difference between the conjectured latitude <note>The position estimated by "dead reckoning" or tracking distance and direction.</note> and the observed latitude as we expressed above: namely, 49 <term>minutes</term> <note>original: "scrupula", the 60th part of a degree.</note>. From this, it is now consistently argued that the motion of the sea in that region is carried from the North <note>original: "Borea"</note> toward the South by a hidden current. Consequently, the sailors progressed much further than they realized or could determine by their conjectures. 

Even if they were not ignorant of this current, and had defined and expressed their conjectures regarding the estimation of the journey completed and the direction of the course (made according to the position of the <term>magnetic needle</term> <note>original: "acus magneticæ", the compass.</note>) by taking that agitation of the sea into account, the reality of the situation speaks for itself: they have not yet fully grasped the truth in every respect. This same fact will also be confirmed below by the observation conducted on the 24th day. And although I suspect that the difference in longitude is also somewhat distorted by this motion, because no "path of footsteps" <note>original: "pedum nulla apparet via"; a metaphor meaning there is no visible evidence or data trail.</note> appears by which the longitude might be restored and corrected, we must retain the value produced by our calculation...

<sig>O</sig>

<meta>catchword: clo-</meta>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical correction of a ship's voyage. On the previous page, Snellius identified an error in the recorded position (point N) caused by a southern current and chose a more reliable observation (point F) as the new starting point for his calculations.</meta>

# WILLEBRORD SNELL

...derived from the <term>loxodromes</term> <note>Also called rhumb lines, these are paths with a constant compass bearing that appear as straight lines on a Mercator projection map.</note> has supplied. And from here, from point F, the remaining rhumb lines are to be arranged in succession, having set aside the endpoint N as erroneous.

From F, therefore, let the eleventh course begin, which is Rhumb Line SSE <note>original: "Z.Z.O." (Zuid-Zuid-Oost), the Dutch abbreviation for South-South-East.</note>, again the second [type of rhumb], for 2 miles; from which a change in latitude is given of 0 degrees 7¼ <term>minutes</term> <note>original: "scrupula," the 60th part of a degree.</note>, increasing the progress of the journey toward the south; and a change in longitude of 0 degrees 4½ minutes, likewise additive, as you see expressed in the mathematical table; which is designated on the map by the line FH.

From here, the twelfth course is SW <note>original: "Z. W." (Zuid-West), South-West.</note>, the fourth rhumb line, for 7½ miles, as represented by the line HK; from which the change in latitude will be given as 0 degrees 21¼ minutes, subtractive; and the change in longitude 0 degrees 32½ minutes, subtractive, because it turns toward the west, whereas our target, Fayal <note>One of the islands of the Azores.</note>, lies to the east from us. And so, I shall refer these numbers with their notes to the proper columns and small tables.

And because the rest of the table does not fit, and since we have expressed everything clearly enough with these examples, I will only note down the numbers by which the change in longitude and latitude is explained, drawn from our calculation in the table with their notes, and I will make an end here.

I will have first advised that I have also used fractions of minutes in latitude, and followed that minute precision <note>original: "λεπτολογίαν" (leptologian), meaning detailed or subtle calculation.</note>, not because the matter is of such great importance, but merely so that I might show by this example that for the highest accuracy <note>original: "ἀκρίβειαν" (akribeian), meaning exactness.</note> nothing remains here that could be rightly desired. And indeed, although these fractions could be filled in and the nearest minute assumed without loss in the first four (or less oblique) rhumb lines, certainly in the rest—especially after the fifth or sixth rhumb line—they are less safely neglected; since there, the neglect of them could easily induce a difference of one or two minutes, and in the most oblique ones even several.

Here follows the calculation of latitude and longitude and...

<meta>This page continues Snellius’s mathematical analysis of a sea voyage, specifically addressing the discrepancy between "dead reckoning" (estimation based on speed and heading) and actual celestial observations. ### THE DUTCH TIPHYS, BOOK II.

I have calculated the individual courses up to the twenty-fourth day, when an observation was again made from the heavens <note>Celestial navigation involving the measurement of the sun or stars to determine latitude.</note>, and the latitude of that place—or that <term>parallel</term> <note>A line of latitude circles the Earth parallel to the equator.</note>—was found to be 42 degrees 54 <term>minutes</term> <note>original: "scr." abbreviated from "scrupula," the 60th part of a degree.</note>. This is despite the fact that, by conjecture and estimation of the journey completed, I had reckoned it to be 43 degrees 58 minutes. Therefore, an error of an entire degree was made in the estimated latitude. Thus, I shall pass from conjectures to observation; and in place of the estimated parallel of 43 degrees 58 minutes, I shall settle the ship upon the parallel of 42 degrees 54 minutes.

For this reason—since both previously and now again it has twice been observed that the ship progressed an entire degree further south than the estimation; and since the celestial observations (as far as the tossing of the sea permits) were sufficiently accurate; and moreover, the estimated distance of the journey and the direction were approved by the common consensus of all (and indeed by the most skilled men, who were not ignorant of this tossing)—we nevertheless find in this example that the sea is driven from the North to the South even more powerfully than it appeared to them.

It would certainly be desirable for the frequent voyages of skilled <term>pilots</term> <note>original: "nauclerorum," the master or navigator of a ship.</note>, diligently recorded, to be studied by experts. In this way, a slightly more consistent judgment could be formed regarding that latent and hidden motion <note>A reference to ocean currents, which were a major source of error for 17th-century navigators who could not yet measure longitude accurately or account for the "set" of the sea.</note>. Pilots who frequent those seas could then be advised on how they ought to record estimated <term>loxodromes</term> <note>Also known as rhumb lines, these are paths with a constant compass bearing.</note>, or courses, in their logbooks, and which course they must substitute for another. Or rather—which I would prefer in such cases—that the observed loxodromes be recorded exactly as the <term>compass</term> <note>original: "pyxis," literally a box, referring to the compass housing.</note> showed them, without the sailor's own correction, and then separately recorded again according to their own judgment. For in this way, the truth would more easily be brought to light. This matter would truly be of the greatest use. I remember that men skilled in this art once told me of a certain pilot, no ordinary man, who once and again from Amster-

<meta>This page continues Snellius's analysis of the navigation logs from a voyage across the Atlantic. He has been demonstrating how to correct errors in "dead reckoning"—the estimation of a ship's position based on speed and direction—by comparing those estimates to actual celestial observations. The previous page discussed how hidden ocean currents can pull a ship off course, leading to significant discrepancies in latitude.</meta>

<page-num>108</page-num>

<header>WILLEBRORD SNELLIAUS</header>

...when he sought the island of Madeira, situated opposite the promontory of Africa, he returned home twice with a failed course and his business unfinished, nor was he able to reach it where it is situated in the middle of the sea. Clearly, that hidden agitation of the sea <note>referring to ocean currents</note> was not understood by him; and therefore, for both himself and his masters, that departure was ruinous.

Next follows the fourth observation on the twenty-sixth day: 39 degrees 24 <term>minutes</term> <note>original: "scr." or "scrupula," the 60th part of a degree.</note>, where the estimated latitude is given as 39 degrees 18 minutes—a very small difference between the two. The longitude, however, was 350 degrees 0 minutes.

Then the fifth observation on the twenty-ninth day: 39 degrees 16 minutes, with an estimated latitude of 39 degrees 31 minutes, and a longitude of 352 degrees 36 minutes.

From here, the sixth: 39 degrees 24 minutes. Then the seventh: 39 degrees 13 minutes. After this, the eighth: 38 degrees 58 minutes. Finally, the last observation on the 4th of August: 38 degrees 50 minutes, with the longitude derived from the <term>rhumb lines</term> <note>original: "loxodromijs." These are paths with a constant compass bearing, which appear as straight lines on a Mercator map but as spirals on a globe.</note> being 2 degrees 30 minutes.

And so we have crossed the <term>Prime Meridian</term> <note>In the 17th century, the "first meridian" was not yet fixed at Greenwich, but was often placed at the westernmost islands of the Azores.</note> which is drawn through the islands of *Corvo* and *Flores*. The island of *Fayal*, which we sought on this course, would be 2 degrees 30 minutes further east. Thus, the distance of the meridians from *Insula Bella* <note>“Beautiful Island,” a location on the American coast mentioned in the logs.</note> to this point will be 22 degrees 59 minutes, and the difference in latitude 13 degrees 35 minutes.

In no different manner, we have noted the variation of longitude and latitude derived from individual courses and compared them with the observed latitude from the island of *Fayal* to the furthest and highest promontory of Spain, commonly called *Cape Finisterre* <note>original: "caput finis Terræ," literally the "Cape of the End of the Earth."</note>. All of these we have recorded in their proper columns and tables in order. From this, the difference in latitude is given as 3 degrees 52 minutes, and the longitude 18 degrees 1 minute. The total difference in longitude between *Insula Bella* on the American coast and the Spanish promontory of *Cape Finisterre* is given as 41 degrees 0 minutes.

It seems to me that it should be noted here in passing, that the first meridia-

<meta>This page concludes Book II of Snellius's treatise. Having discussed the practical difficulties of navigation and the discrepancies between dead reckoning and observation, he now addresses the lack of a standardized Prime Meridian—the "zero point" for longitude—which was a major source of confusion for 17th-century cartographers and sailors.</meta>

<page-num>109</page-num>

<header>THE DUTCH TIPHYS, BOOK II.</header>

the [prime] meridian is established by some through the islands of ***Corvo*** and ***Flores***, because in that place the magnet-treated needle <note>the mariner's compass</note> points accurately toward the North and South; by others, however, it is placed through ***Terceira*** <note>Though Terceira is in the Azores, Snellius here associates it with the Canaries, perhaps reflecting contemporary geographical confusion</note>, one of the Canary Islands (or the "Fortunate Isles," as it seems to some), in which there is a high and lofty rock fifty <term>stadia</term> <note>An ancient Greek unit of distance; fifty stadia is roughly 5.7 miles, an exaggeration of the height of Mount Teide</note> in height, measured vertically. This rock is visible from a distance of four degrees to those approaching from the south or the north. For although the altitude of the pole <note>the latitude</note> at ***Tenerife*** is 28 degrees 20 <term>minutes</term> <note>original: "scr." or "scrupula," the 60th part of a degree.</note>, and at ***Madeira*** it is 32 degrees 30 minutes, yet from as far away as ***Madeira***, it appears to stand above the horizon with the height of a temple seen from a distance. Therefore, most people today establish this mountain, called ***El Pico*** <note>Mount Teide on Tenerife</note> by the inhabitants, as the beginning of the prime meridian, like a sign placed there by nature. Moreover, the meridian drawn through ***El Pico*** in ***Tenerife*** stands fourteen degrees apart from the meridian drawn through the islands of ***Corvo*** and ***Flores***. If you therefore establish the prime meridian here,

<margin>348 degrees 30 minutes</margin>

***Fayal*** will be located under the meridian at 348 degrees 30 minutes. And although it is all the same from where the beginning of the meridians is derived for investigating the difference of longitude from the ship's courses, it is nevertheless necessary that you know this accurately if you wish to record your journey on a map, so that you know in whose territory or vicinity you are traveling. Therefore, let it suffice to have given these warnings; and let this also be the end of the second book:

<meta>This page marks a significant shift in the manuscript from Book II to Book VII. While the previous section (page 109) dealt with the practicalities of the Prime Meridian and the height of Mount Teide, this page (page 208) delves into the philosophical and metaphysical definitions of "location" (locus). Snellius uses Scholastic logic to categorize how physical objects, spiritual beings, and the Divine occupy space, likely to provide a theoretical foundation for the concept of coordinates and position.</meta>

they make, which can be brought forward regarding these at the end.

It appears, however, in this manner: There is a certain thing which, though it exists as it does, nevertheless does not seem to be in a "place," just as the soul is in the body, and God is in the world; and this is throughout the entire essence, not through a specific part, etc.

On the other hand, there are certain things which are in a place, such as physical bodies; and these have magnitude <note>original: "magnitudinem," referring to physical size or volume</note>, and their locations are bounded, and are determined by the magnitude of the bodies.

<margin>To chapter 17.</margin>
Certain things are indeed in a place, but do not have magnitude, such as an angel or a separated soul <note>The "separated soul" refers to the human soul after death, when it is no longer joined to a physical body</note>; and of these, it is said that they are in a place by **circumscription**, or by the application of their power. And of these, it seems that they are in a place circumscriptively, because their place is finite and bounded, and they are not outside that place, but within it.

But concerning angels and separated souls, it appears that they are not in a place circumscriptively <note>original: "circumscriptive"; this means being bounded by a surrounding surface like a liquid in a jar</note>, because they do not have magnitude by which they might be circumscribed; and therefore it is said that they are in a place **definitively**, because they are so in one place that they are not in another; and this is what it means to be in a place definitively. But of God, it is said that He is in every place through essence, presence, and power.

<note>Vocabulary list: soul, body, God, place, circumscription, definition, essence, presence, power, angel</note>

<summary>In this passage, Snellius explores the metaphysical definitions of location, distinguishing between how physical bodies (bounded by size), spirits (located in specific spots without size), and God (present everywhere) occupy space.</summary>
<keywords>metaphysics, Scholasticism, soul, omnipresence, angels, space and place, circumscription, definitive location</keywords>

--- Page 175 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition into Book VII of Snellius’s work. While the previous section focused on the physical measurements of the Earth and the Prime Meridian (specifically Mount Teide), this page begins a metaphysical investigation into the nature of "location" (locus). Snellius is laying the philosophical groundwork for coordinates; by defining how bodies, souls, and the Divine occupy space, he provides a theoretical basis for the mathematical "placement" of points on a map. This scholastic approach was typical for 17th-century scholars who sought to reconcile physical science with theology and Aristotelian logic.</meta>

It appears, however, in this manner: There is a certain kind of being which, though it exists as it does, nevertheless does not seem to occupy a "place" in the traditional sense, just as the soul is present within the body, and God is present in the world; and this presence is through the entire essence, not through a specific part <note>This refers to the Scholastic concept where the soul is "all in the whole and all in every part" of the body, rather than being located in just one organ</note>, and so forth.

On the other hand, there are certain things which are situated in a place, such as physical bodies; and these possess magnitude <note>original: "magnitudinem," referring here to physical extension or the property of taking up space</note>, and their locations are bounded and determined by the physical volume of the bodies themselves.

<note>This table provides the essential data for what we now recognize as the Mercator projection's mathematical foundation. Snellius uses sexagesimal divisions (primes, seconds, thirds, and fourths) to provide high-precision values. Each column (1-4) represents a step of 60 units higher than the first column, allowing sailors to find values for larger distances or higher latitudes without starting their calculations from zero.</note>

<note>This table provides the values for "meridional parts," which are essential for navigation. On a globe, the distance between degrees of longitude shrinks as one moves toward the poles. To create a flat Mercator map where the rhumb lines (constant compass bearings) are straight, the distance between degrees of latitude must be mathematically stretched or "increased." These numbers represent that precise stretching for latitudes 25 through 29, measured down to the minute.</note>

<note>This table displays what are known as "meridional parts" for latitudes between 30 and 34 degrees. <note>This table continues the "increased latitudes" <note>original: latitudines auctae</note>. These values allowed cartographers to determine exactly how much to "stretch" the distance between parallels of latitude on a map to ensure that a constant compass course appears as a straight line.</note>

These values are essential for constructing a Mercator projection map. By using these calculated distances instead of simple degrees, a navigator can draw a straight line on a flat map and follow it as a constant compass heading (a rhumb line) across the curved surface of the Earth.</note>

<note>This table presents the "increased latitudes" <note>original: latitudines auctae</note>—a concept now known in navigation as meridional parts. Snellius calculated these values to allow sailors to plot loxodromes (lines of constant bearing) on a flat map. As a navigator moves north or south from the equator, the distance between degrees of latitude on a Mercator projection must be mathematically "stretched" to maintain accurate directional angles. These numbers represent that precise stretching for latitudes 50° to 54°.</note>

<note>This table provides the values for "increased latitudes" <note>original: latitudines auctae</note>, known today as meridional parts. These calculations are essential for the Mercator projection, allowing a navigator to represent a course of constant bearing—a rhumb line—as a straight line on a flat map. As the latitude increases toward the pole, the spacing between the parallels of latitude is mathematically "increased" to maintain the correct proportions relative to the shrinking distance between meridians of longitude.</note>

<note>These tables of "increased latitudes" <note>original: latitudines auctae</note> are essential for Mercator projection navigation. By using these values, a sailor can calculate the change in longitude when traveling along a constant compass bearing, or rhumb line.</note>

<term>Logarithms</term> <note>original: Logarithmi</note>: A mathematical tool used to simplify complex calculations of multiplication and division into addition and subtraction; here, they underpin the calculation of the meridional parts.

<term>Mathematical table</term> <note>original: tabula mathematica</note>: A structured list of calculated values used by astronomers and navigators to avoid repeating laborious arithmetic.

## CANONS

# OF RHUMB LINES

<note>original: "Canones Loxodromici"</note>

->Loxodromic degrees<-

<note>The term "Loxodromic" refers to a rhumb line—a path of constant bearing on the globe. These "canons" or tables were essential for sailors using Mercator charts, as they allowed for the calculation of distance and direction over long voyages by accounting for the way meridians of longitude converge at the poles.</note>

# ANALOGOUS RHUMB LINE CANONS
<note>original: "CANONES LOXODROMICI ἀνάλογοι"</note>

<note>Willebrord Snellius, a Dutch mathematician, used these tables to solve the "Dutch Tiphys" problem—improving navigation by mathematically describing the rhumb line, which is a path with a constant compass bearing. The term "analogous" refers to the proportional relationship between the distance traveled and the change in latitude and longitude.</note>

<note>The miles used here are likely German miles (one degree of the great circle being 15 miles). The <term>mecodynic</term> column (from Greek *mêkos* for length) relates to what we now call meridional parts, which allow a navigator to account for the stretching of latitude on a Mercator map.</note>

<note>The "mecodynic" (mecodyna) miles refer to the meridional parts, a concept essential for creating Mercator projection charts where the distance between lines of latitude increases as one moves toward the poles. <note>
**Technical Terms Used in This Table:**
*   **Loxodromic Miles:** The distance measured along a rhumb line (a path of constant bearing).
*   **Mecodynic Miles:** A term coined by Snellius (from the Greek *mekos* for length/longitude and *dynamis* for power/value) to describe the "change in longitude" or the longitudinal distance gained. 
*   **Degrees (gr.):** The change in latitude.
*   **Minutes (scr.):** Fractions of a degree (scrupula).
</note>

# HANDY LOXODROMIC TABLES <note>original: "CANONES LOXODROMICI πρόχειροι." The Greek term "πρόχειροι" (procheiroi) means "ready-at-hand" or "handy," implying these tables were intended for quick practical use by sailors and cartographers.</note>

-> 3 3/4 <note>This heading signifies the 3 3/4 rhumb line. In the maritime system of 32 compass points, each point is 11.25 degrees. The 3 3/4 rhumb represents a constant heading of 42.1875 degrees away from the North-South meridian.</note> <-

| deg. <note>short for Latin *gradus*, degrees of latitude.</note> | loxodromic miles | mecodynic miles <term>mecodynic: A term coined by Snellius from the Greek "mekos" (longitude) and "dynamis" (power). -> 4 <note>This indicates the 4th rhumb line. In the traditional 32-point compass, the 4th rhumb corresponds to a 45-degree angle (such as North-East). At this specific angle, the "mecodynic" or longitudinal power is equal to the latitudinal distance, as the tangent of 45 degrees is 1.</note> <-

### HANDY LOXODROMIC TABLES <note>original: "CANONES LOXODROMICI πρόχειροι." The Greek word "πρόχειροι" (*procheiroi*) means "ready at hand" or "handy," indicating these are tables for quick reference.</note>

# HANDY LOXODROMIC TABLES <note>original: "CANONES LOXODROMICI προχείροι". The Greek word *procheiroi* translates to "handy" or "ready at hand," indicating that these tables were designed for practical, everyday use by navigators.</note>

# LOXODROMIC TABLES <note>original: "CANONES LOXODROMICI λοξόδρομος". The Greek word *loxodromos* literally means "oblique running," a term coined by Snellius to describe a ship's path when it maintains a constant compass heading, crossing all meridians at the same angle.</note>

-> $7 \frac{1}{2}$ <note>This heading indicates the calculations for the 7 ½ rhumb line. On a 32-point compass, this is the final fractional point before reaching the 8th rhumb, which would be a heading due East or West (90°).</note> <-

| deg. <note>short for Latin *gradus*, degrees of latitude.</note> | loxodromic miles <note>The actual distance a ship travels along the curved rhumb line path.</note> | mecodynic miles <term>mecodynic: A term Snellius derived from the Greek words for "longitude" and "power." It represents the "power" of a specific course to produce a change in longitude for every degree of latitude gained.</term> | min. <note>original: "scr." for *scrupula*, meaning minutes (1/60th) of a degree.</note> | loxodromic miles | mecodynic miles | min. | loxodromic miles | mecodynic miles |

# HANDY LOXODROMIC TABLES <note>original: "CANONES LOXODROMICI πρóχειροι". The Greek word *procheiroi* implies these are "handy" or "ready-at-hand" tables designed for practical use by navigators. "Loxodromic" refers to the rhumb line, or constant compass heading.</note>

-> 7 3/4 <note>This heading specifies that the following values apply to the 7 3/4 rhumb point. On a standard 32-point compass rose, this is the final fractional division before reaching the 8th rhumb, which would be a cardinal direction (East or West).</note> <-

<page-num>61</page-num>
<i>
<meta>catchword: Flori- (Flowering)</meta>

## CLAUDIAN ON THE MAGNET. <note>original: "CLAVDIANVS de MAGNETE". Claudian was a Latin poet of the late 4th century. Snellius includes this poem to provide a literary and philosophical perspective on the "miraculous" nature of magnetism, which allows the compass to function.</note>

*Whoever has followed the world with an anxious mind*
*and explores the seeds of things: by what failing the Moon labors*
*in eclipse, what cause commands the Sun to grow pale,*
*from whence come the reddening comets with their deadly hair,* <note>original: "ferali crine". Comets were historically viewed as omens of death or disaster; their tails were described as "hair."</note>
*from where the winds flow, what movement shakes the trembling*
*bowels of the earth, what origin leads the lightning,*
*whence the clouds thunder, and with what light the rainbow blooms:*
*Let him explain this to me as I seek—if my mind is strong enough*
*to grasp any truth. There is a stone named the Magnet,* <note>original: "Magnes".</note>
*discolored, dark, and common. It does not adorn the combed*
*hair of Kings, nor the white necks of a maiden,*
*nor does it shine with a notable clasp upon belts:*
*But if you see the strange wonders of this black rock,*
*then it surpasses beautiful ornaments, and whatever the Indian*
*searches for among the red seaweed on Eastern shores.* <note>original: "Eois Indus litoribus". This refers to pearls or coral gathered in the Indian Ocean.</note>
*It earns its life from iron, and upon the hardness of iron*
*it feeds. These are its sweet banquets, this the fodder it knows.*
*From here it renews its own strength; from here, the rough nourishment*
*poured through its limbs preserves its secret vigor.*
*Without this, it perishes. Its dying limbs grow numb*
*with grim hunger, and decay consumes its open veins.*
*Mars,* <note>original: "Mavors", an archaic name for the god Mars.</note> *who strikes cities with his bloody spear-point,*
*and Venus, who loosens human cares into leisure,*
*occupy the shared shrines of a golden temple.*
*The gods do not have the same image: but the form of Mars*
*shines in iron; a magnetic gemstone represents Venus.* <note>original: "magnetica gemma". In the poem’s allegory, the iron represents the god of war, and the loadstone represents the goddess of love.</note>
*For them, a priest celebrates a marriage according to custom.*
*The flame leads the dances. The thresholds are wreathed*
*with festive, leafy myrtle, and the couches rise with roses*

<vocab>Magnet (Magnes), Mars (Mavors), Venus, Claudian (Claudianus), magnetic gemstone (magnetica gemma)</vocab>

<summary>Snellius quotes a poem by the Roman writer Claudian, which uses the myth of Mars and Venus to explain the "hunger" and attraction of the loadstone for iron. This serves as a transition from mathematical navigation to the natural wonders of the magnetic compass.</summary>
<keywords>Magnetism, Claudian, Mars and Venus, Loadstone, Natural Philosophy, Allegory, Iron, Poetry</keywords>

--- Page 238 ---
<meta>The previous page introduced Claudian’s poem on the magnet, describing the stone as seemingly plain and dark but possessing a power far greater than Indian pearls or gold. It explained how the magnet "feeds" on iron to renew its strength. This page continues the poetic allegory, personifying the magnet and iron as the lovers Venus and Mars.</meta>

<page-num>62</page-num>

*...with flowers, and dotal purple veils the bridal chambers.* <note>original: "dotalis purpura". This refers to the purple cloth associated with a dowry and the marriage bed.</note>
*Here a wonderful work arises. The Goddess of Cythera* <note>original: "Cytherea", an epithet for Venus, who was born near the island of Cythera.</note> *willingly*
*seizes her husband, and imitating the former couches of heaven,*
*she binds the breast of Mars* <note>original: "Mavortia", pertaining to Mars, the god of war.</note> *with a playful breath,*
*and suspends so great a weight; she entangles his arms in his*
*helmet, and encircles him entirely in living embraces.*
*He, provoked by the long action of the breath,*
*is drawn from his spouse by the secret knots of the gem.* <note>original: "gemma de conjuge". The "gem" here is the magnet, personified as Venus attracting the iron, Mars.</note>
*Nature becomes the bridesmaid to the Gods, and a tenacious*
*air marries the iron. The divinities are joined in sudden thefts.* <note>original: "sociantur Numina furtis". This alludes to the secret, "stolen" love affair between Venus and Mars in mythology.</note>
*What heat pours mutual treaties into the twin metals?*
*What concord joins such hard minds?*
*The panting flint* <note>original: "silex", used here to describe the hard magnetic stone.</note> *burns, and wounded, feels its friendly*
*material, and the steel* <note>original: "chalybs".</note> *recognizes peaceful loves.*
*Thus Venus is accustomed to restrain the horrific King of War,*
*and to soften his countenance, when headlong he*
*seethes with blood, and sharpens his rages with drawn blades;*
*she alone meets the wild horses, and softens the swelling*
*of his breast, and tempers his heart with a charming fire.*
*A tranquil peace is given to his mind, and he deserts the hot*
*battles, and bends his reddish crests for kisses.*
*What power is not permitted to you, cruel boy?* <note>original: "sæve puer". This refers to Cupid (Eros), the personification of desire and attraction.</note>
*You overcome the great thunderbolt, and having left heaven,*
*you compel the Thunderer* <note>original: "Tonantem", an epithet for Jupiter.</note> *to bellow in the midst of the waves.* <note>A reference to Jupiter transforming into a bull to carry off Europa.</note>
*Now you strike frozen rocks, and limbs lacking*
*living sense. Now stones are subject to your weapons,*
*and their own passion drives the rocks, and the iron is held*
*by allurements. Flames reign in the rigid marble.*
